Culture in Competition Anyone who has been to Jinhua must have had this experience: after checking into a hotel, some scholarly country gentlemen in blue cotton gowns would come to question you: "Are you going to Fangyan to burn incense?

How many times have you come?Which inn did you stay at before?

" If you answered it was your first time to Fangyan, they would take out a business card and ask you to stay at that inn after going up Fangyan.

These are the "fangtou" of Yanxia Street—guest-receivers similar to but slightly different from inns.

Hundreds of li away, these dispatched agents drummed up business; on one hand, one can imagine the prosperity of Fangyan's incense market year-round, and on the other, infer the fierce competition among the four or five hundred families on Yanxia Street.

The so-called fangtou of Yanxia Street, those operating inns and relying solely on Hugong Temple for a living, numbered three to five thousand, mostly with the surnames Cheng and Ying, with a strong literary tradition and considerable property, all living in three-story houses.

Generally, the ground floor was built in the valley, the middle floor faced the street, and the upper floor was loft; each family had thirty to fifty rooms, and during prosperous incense seasons, every family was reportedly overcrowded.

Pilgrims came from Shaoxing, Chuzhou, Hangzhou, and nearby counties, already a considerable number, and the farthest came from Fujian.This "Quiet Records of Fangyan," whose every line brims with bustle, was completed by Mr.

Yu Dafu (1895-1945) in 1933.With a few strokes, he sketched the prosperous scene of Yanxia Street's inn industry at that time.

The "fangtou" mentioned here are the people dispatched by major inns to solicit and receive guests.Although not as many as the three to five thousand mentioned in the article, three to five hundred seems likely.

Elder Ying Yexiang, grandson-in-law of Cheng Longchang, says that before each year's incense season, Cheng Zhenxing would dispatch two to three hundred people to various places to receive guests.

The county gazetteer also mentions that Cheng Renchang had seventy to eighty guest-receiving clerks.These fangtou were mostly locals, and when short-staffed, hired outsiders.

They would set out over a month before the incense season to receive guests in Shanghai, Hangzhou, Ningbo, Wenzhou, Tiantai, Jinhua, Dongyang, Yiwu, and other places.Hard as it was, they also had to speak local dialects.

Fangtou who could receive many guests were highly respected in the inn.

It is said that the famous fangtou at Cheng Zhenxing Inn was Cheng Shuijing, son of the owner Cheng Zhaofu's sixth brother; he spoke many dialects and was a skilled guest-receiver, and even Cheng Zhaofu would listen to his suggestions on inn management.

Corresponding to fangtou were "xiangtou," who specialized in organizing pilgrims locally.

Xiangtou would generally write to the inn one or two months in advance, agreeing on lodging dates and the number of fellow travelers, usually specially noting "rain or shine" to show piety.

Subsequently, the fangtou dispatched by the inn would contact them with gifts and give each pilgrim a red cloth strip with the inn's name to tie on their luggage.

Small inns could not afford to dispatch people as grandly as large inns, so the inn owners had to go themselves.

This custom continues today; although current owners no longer need to receive guests in person, they call old pilgrims to greet them in the seventh lunar month, and visit every few years.

Before engines were widely used for long-distance passenger transport, devout pilgrims either relied on their own two legs or took boats to nearby Yongkang, Dongyang, or Yiwu before switching to walking.

It is unknown when sedan-chair agencies began appearing in Yongkang, Dongyang, Yiwu, Zhuji, and other places, specifically to transport pilgrims to and from Fangyan.

The novel "Enmity Records of Kuocang Mountain," set in late Qing society after the failure of the Taiping Rebellion (1851-1864), mentions sedan-chair agencies in Yongkang city transporting pilgrims to Fangyan.

"Yongcheng Mountain Songs," praising famous stores and sights of Yongkang, also mentions the famous Wang Jiankang sedan-chair agency in the county town.

In January of the 23rd year of the Republic (1934), the Hangjiang Railway was fully opened, with stations at Jinhua 51, Yiwu, and Zhuji.

The same year, the Fangyan Highway was completed and opened, with cars able to drive all the way to the Cheng Family Ancestral Hall entrance north of Chenglu Village.

To provide thorough service and, more importantly, secure customers, inns not only had to make proper arrangements with these out-of-town sedan-chair agencies to ensure service, but also dispatched many clerks to wait at Yongkang and Fangyan stations, welcoming guests whose luggage bore their own red cloth strips.

After the War of Resistance against Japan ended, the number of pilgrims and inns both increased greatly; at that time, any boy who could carry luggage would go to the station to receive guests.

In the early twentieth century, competition among inns on the street was fierce, especially between the two large inns Cheng Zhenxing and Cheng Renchang for customers.

Although Cheng Renchang and Cheng Zhenxing stood almost side by side on both sides of the old street, Cheng Renchang had a slight advantage in competition because it was to the north.

Since all guests originally planning to stay at Cheng Zhenxing would pass by its entrance, Cheng Renchang often stole Cheng Zhenxing's guests.

Therefore, Cheng Zhenxing's dispatched fangtou, when encountering groups of wealthy people—especially Shaoxing guests—would personally bring the guests back to Fangyan.

For ordinary guests, they could only hope that their clerks waiting at the station would be quick enough to grab luggage tied with "Cheng Zhenxing" red cloth strips.

For those not received at the bus station—especially first-time pilgrims to Fangyan—when they passed Cheng Renchang's entrance, they might be pulled into that inn by Cheng Renchang's clerks.

As this happened more often, Cheng Zhenxing's business was inevitably affected.So Cheng Zhenxing's owner Cheng Zhaofu went to his sixth brother Cheng Zhaogong to discuss a strategy.

Zhaofu suggested Zhaogong buy land in Chenglu Village at the north end of the old street; Zhaofu would use wood from his own mountains as material and pay wages to help Zhaogong build an inn.

Once the construction money was repaid, the inn would belong to Zhaogong.This new inn was named Cheng Longchang, and at its entrance would hang Cheng Zhenxing's sign.

Cheng Zhenxing's guests passing through would be led by Cheng Longchang's clerks to Cheng Zhenxing Inn, one li away.To this day, Cheng Zhaogong's grandson-in-law Ying Yexiang still preserves these two signs.

Even this was not enough, because even inns run by brothers would compete.

According to Elder Ying Yexiang, Cheng Zhenxing's skilled guest-receiver Cheng Shuijing suggested to Cheng Zhaofu that a chamber of commerce be established.

Its purpose was to unite all inns, establish trade rules, ensure no one stole guests, with fines for violations.

Specific clauses included: guests whose luggage bore a certain inn's red cloth strip could not be received by other inns; when a guest reached a certain inn's entrance or stepped one foot inside, other inns could not receive them; each inn's roadside guest-receivers should stand in fixed positions, etc.

But due to the cruelty of competition, and because the chamber had no real power to penalize guest-stealing inns, the chamber was established several times, yet each time gradually became a mere formality.

Of course, ultimately, what truly made an inn strong in competition was its meticulous service and reasonable charges.

Generally, pilgrims coming here stayed for "one night and two meals": they arrived in the afternoon of the first day, ate some snacks, then went up Fangyan, and left after breakfast the next day; some arrived the previous evening, went up Fangyan after breakfast, and left after lunch.

Upon arrival, clerks immediately helped with luggage, invited guests to rest in the hall, then served sugar tea, pastries, lotus root starch, and vegetarian noodles.

If guests had not yet gone up Fangyan, that evening's meal would be vegetarian to show piety; if they had already worshipped Hugong, upon returning to the inn they would see this scene: "The guest hall was already lit with two large red candles, set with many dishes of large meat and large chicken, waiting for us to eat dinner, with an abundance of vegetables.

" In the evening, the inn would often invite small singing troupes to entertain, and sometimes guests would call eager performers outside into their rooms to sing.

They mostly sang festive and lively excerpts from Wu opera such as "Hundred Longevity Picture," "Dragon-Tiger Battle," and "Grand Tutor Zhang Returns to Court.

" Because guests differed in wealth and came from different places, their preferences for dish types and flavors varied, but inns would all attend to them carefully.

Cheng Renchang Inn had three tiers of charging standards based on service and meals, with differences of twenty to thirty times.But even so-called "third-class" guests ate and drank as much as they wished, and were served a whole chicken.

Cheng Zhenxing Inn, favored by wealthy guests from Shanghai, Suzhou, Hangzhou, and Shaoxing, was especially distinctive in this regard.

The inn not only sent longan and lychee soup to these guests at night, but the chef also prepared meals according to their tastes; and when guests left, the inn gave gifts such as ham, flower umbrellas, tea, lotus root starch, "Fangyan Guide," and Fangyan maps.

Besides these basic services, many inns provided many extra services.For example, they mostly sold offerings such as incense candles on commission, and some prepared three-animal sacrificial offerings for pilgrims to rent.

Moreover, inn clerks would help carry offerings up the mountain, arrange them, and carry them back down; if it was a guest's first time up Fangyan, a clerk would guide them as a tour guide.

For guests, receiving such good service meant they not only returned as loyal repeat customers every year, but sometimes also sent the inn local specialties.However, they occasionally also "stole.

" Descendants of Cheng Zhenchang Inn say that in those years Wenzhou people often stole bowls from the inn for their children to use, and when the children achieved success, they would return to Fangyan to fulfill vows and repay the inn tenfold.

Providing such perfect service required joint efforts from the owner down to the waiters.The busiest times at Fangyan Mountain were the first, second, eighth, ninth, and tenth lunar months.

Needless to say, in the first month, locals and nearby people vied to go up Fangyan on New Year's Eve to burn the first incense, then draw a peace talisman for the new year.

Fangyan people generally believed the twenty-fifth day of the second lunar month was Hugong's birthday; around that day, as was customary, Guangci Temple would invite people to perform temple fair operas for two days and three nights before the Hugong Main Hall.

From the ninth day of the eighth lunar month to the fourteenth day of the ninth lunar month was the over-one-month Hugong Temple Fair, when all seventy-two townships in Yongkang would come to Fangyan to "perform Luohan martial arts," with two peaks on the thirteenth day of the eighth month and the Double Ninth Festival.

The "Kuchuan Hu Family Genealogy" from Hu Ze's hometown records that the thirteenth day of the eighth lunar month was Hugong's birthday.

People on the old street, however, believed this day was when Hugong "reviewed" the Luohan troupes, or when he was "deified." As a folk custom, people do not investigate its true origin.

So during the temple fair, tens of thousands of people went up Fangyan daily with different purposes.After the temple fair, it coincided with the slack farming season, so most pilgrims were from out of town.

This was also the inns' busiest time of year, with inns large and small on the old street all overcrowded.

So inn owners would treat all clerks to a meal on the Mid-Autumn Festival of the fifteenth day of the eighth lunar month, hoping everyone would unite and work hard for over a month to run the inn well together.

A folk ballad of the time sang: "Don't marry your daughter to Yanxia Street, for in the eighth and ninth months she becomes a signboard.Wipe her eyes once, and when her parents pass by she doesn't even drag them in.

" During this period, inn clerks were mostly temporary workers, paid by the day.Although wages were not high, there were generous tips.

Total tip income was distributed among all staff according to the principle of "reward diligence and punish laziness," with the owner not participating.So although hard, it was considered a good job.

Large inns like Cheng Zhenxing needed to hire thirty to forty, even seventy to eighty clerks each year, all available on call.The most distinctive feature of inn operation was their "jargon.

" Many trades have their own jargon, existing to prevent outsiders from understanding, thereby protecting the trade's interests.

The jargon common on the old street was also called "the speech of those who eat Hugong," so those selling general merchandise and incense candles also understood it.

For those in the inn business, it was especially important because it avoided many unnecessary embarrassments.

For example, inns inevitably borrowed things from each other such as tofu, eggs, even bed boards and bedding; if guests heard such talk, it would be somewhat humiliating, and might even cause guests who had just stepped through the threshold to turn and leave, so such exchanges were conducted in jargon.

The jargon here was vivid and easy to remember.It mostly replaced things with their characteristics—for example, fish was called "water shuttle," noodles "long," soy sauce "red water," etc.

Some involved splitting Chinese characters: thus rice became "eight wood," and fire "two dots person." Because of this, jargon is still used by some people today.

Provincial Government's Wartime Temporary Office Location 26th Year of the Republic (1937) July 7, Marco Polo Bridge Incident!July 29, Beijing fell!The next day, Tianjin fell!August 13, Battle of Shanghai broke out; November 12, Shanghai fell!

November 5, Jiaxing and Huzhou lost, Hangzhou in critical danger!The Kuomintang Zhejiang Provincial Government was forced to move to Jinhua.

Early December, Zhejiang Provincial Government decided to move from Jinhua to Fangyan.December 24, Hangzhou fell...

27th Year of the Republic (1938) January 1, the Kuomintang Zhejiang Provincial Government began operating in Shoushan Keng north of the old street.

In those turbulent wartime years, because some major highways were occupied by the Japanese, and the Hangjiang Railway—only opened for a few years—was destroyed by Kuomintang troops due to wartime needs, the number of pilgrims coming from afar to worship Hugong at Fangyan also decreased.

But the old street welcomed some refined, well-dressed city people with their families.

The street's inns were transformed into government agencies such as the Procuratorate, Civil Affairs Department, and Accounting Office.

During this special period, Fangyan, with its special terrain and the superior accommodation conditions on the old street, was chosen by the Kuomintang Zhejiang Provincial Government as the wartime temporary government location.

The provincial government operated in Fangyan from January 1, 1938 (27th year of the Republic), until May 1941 (30th year), when it moved to Songyang; in August it returned to Fangyan.

In May 1942 (31st year), it moved again to Songyang, and in August transferred to Yunhe, remaining there until the victory of the War of Resistance against Japan.

The Zhejiang Provincial Government operated in Fangyan for a total of 4 years and 5 months.

During this period, two provincial administrative conferences, one provincial police conference, and one provincial land administration conference were held.

Provincial government committee meetings, party-government joint meetings, capital and provincial capital fall commemorations, mobilization monthly meetings, commemoration weeks, and various national commemorations were held on schedule.

Especially in early April 1939 (28th year of the Republic), Zhou Enlai, in his capacity as Vice Minister of the Political Department of the Kuomintang Government Military Committee, came to Fangyan and, together with Zhejiang Provincial Government Chairman Huang Shaohong, personally planted two plane trees in Shoushan Keng.

On July 7 of the same year, the provincial government built a Monument to Martyrs Who Died in the War of Resistance before Taohua Peak, one of the Five Peaks, and held gatherings at the square before the monument every July 7 to commemorate soldiers who died on the anti-Japanese battlefield.

Accompanying the provincial government to Yongkang were some twenty to thirty provincial government parallel agencies, over twenty subsidiary institutions of various departments, and over twenty other organizations and groups.

Many of these agencies were scattered on the old street or in temporarily built houses beside Wufeng Road.

Moreover, accompanying government civil servants and their families, as well as some fleeing common people, would rent inns or private houses on the old street for long-term residence.

According to elders, at that time almost every family had rooms for rent.

If private houses, they mostly lived on the second floor originally used for storage; for inns, owners would reserve some first-floor rooms for sporadic pilgrims coming to worship Hugong at Fangyan, and rent out the upstairs guest rooms.

At the north end of the old street and in Chenglu Village, due to proximity to the provincial government located in Shoushan Keng, every floor was crowded.

Perhaps because of the decades of turmoil following the founding of New China, people did not tell this history to their descendants as an honor-bringing family story.

The elders still alive today were children then, and their memories are mostly vague.

Only one-sentence stories remain, such as: "They were very clean and always fetched water from the well in the courtyard"; "Once my father went to collect rent and was beaten by them instead"; "There was an ophthalmologist Dr.

Zhuo at the other end of the street who even treated my diphtheria"; "That bureau chief (police chief) was tall and big, a bit chubby, always smiling.

" The large inns on the old street, with many rooms and corresponding service facilities, naturally became the first choice for government agencies.

The Civil Affairs Department was successively located in Xinlongxing Inn and Shaochang Ancestral Hall.

Descendants of Cheng Chengchang Inn say the Civil Affairs Department head lived in their home at that time; there were over thirty Civil Affairs Department civil servant households, some living upstairs and the rest nearby.

Cheng Renchang Longji then provided some office space for the provincial government Finance Department and served as dormitories for its staff.

The third floor of Xinrenchang Inn served as the office and dormitory of the provincial government Accounting Office.

Cheng Zhenxing Inn, not yet divided at that time, on one hand provided office space for the Provincial Education Department and the National News Agency; on the other hand, it used the unique open space in the ancestral hall to provide venues for other activities.

According to Cheng Zhaofu's grandson Cheng Chongxue's recollection, the inn specially invited three senior chefs from Tianxianglou Duoyichu in Hangzhou to cook Chinese and Western dishes and host banquets for provincial government meetings, weddings, and other celebrations.

Elder Cheng Zhenkang, living opposite Cheng Zhaofu's ancestral hall, recalls that as a child he saw a Western-style wedding held in the hall, with the bride in a long white veil, very beautiful.

Cultural activities were also held in the hall—for example, a Peking opera troupe from the province performed there several times.

The square west of Cheng Zhenxing Inn was then called "People's Sports Ground" (now the Fangyan bus station parking lot), where large-scale gatherings were held.

Compared with the concentration of civil servants in the middle section of the old street, the southern section housed many "cultural people.

" The Zhejiang Provincial High Court and High Court Procuratorate were both located near Huilong Bridge; Court President Zheng Wenli, Chief Prosecutor Wang Bingyi (now 158 Yanshang Street), and Accounting Office Chief Chen Baolin (now 138 Yanshang Street) also lived there.

Elder Cheng Shouyue, living west of Huilong Bridge, says his family had 11 rooms, and several doctors lived there at that time.

The provincial government's years in Fangyan had considerable impact on the old street's economy, culture, and other aspects.

During the war, the number of pilgrims coming to Fangyan decreased significantly.

Although government civil servants paid low rent for private houses—some even paid nothing—those rooms would otherwise be empty, so renting them out brought some income.

In those turbulent times, having a stable income from the government could be considered a luxury.

Without devout pilgrims, and without these refined city people, the old street's economy would likely have collapsed due to the war, let alone the post-war revival of the inn industry.

Just as when the Southern Song Dynasty moved its capital to Hangzhou, what went south included not only civil and military officials but also large numbers of literati, bringing advanced northern culture and technology; this time, many celebrities from all walks of life also took refuge in Fangyan with the Zhejiang Provincial Government.

Moreover, Yongkang—originally a place "not where boats and carts converge, nor where merchants gather"—suddenly became the center and transit hub for goods and information in Zhejiang Province.

Due to the war, the Zhejiang-Jiangxi Railway and Qiantang River water routes were forced to中断, and provincial goods transport had to be rerouted through Wenzhou.

The Wenzhou to Lishui section went by water, handled by transit agencies; the 75-kilometer Lishui to Yongkang section relied entirely on cart drivers from "civilian handcart teams," "refugee handcart teams," and "Yongkang Relief Committee handcart teams" traveling on foot.

One person with one cart transported at most 225 kilograms each time, taking six days for a round trip.

A large portion of these goods ultimately reached the small valley where the old street was located, supplying the city people living there.

Undeniably, the large flow of goods also indirectly promoted the old street's development.

For example, to facilitate transportation, the Nationalist government built a gravel road connecting Chenglu and Nanyan at the foot of Fangyan Mountain.

This road finally replaced the old street's position nearly half a century later, with inns and restaurants lining it, becoming the main route for pilgrims going up Fangyan.

These civil servants from big cities were not easily satisfied with the simple pastoral life of rising at dawn and resting at sunset.

The urban culture they brought, grafted onto the original local culture of the old street, had a unique charm.

Such scenes as Peking opera in ancestral halls and Western-style weddings would be hard to see in other Jiangnan villages.

Teacher Chen Zhihua, who came here with his father, says that on the open ground in front of Cheng Chengchang Inn, wandering performers often set up cloth curtains and sang "Shaoxing opera.

" It is said the provincial government's military band was also there, and sometimes performed in Cheng Zhaofu's ancestral hall.

Elder Cheng Jufang, living at the north end of the old street, recalls that in those years, at the small square outside the Sanjiantou ravine mouth, not only were operas performed, but occasionally silent films were shown.

Sometimes Chairman Huang Shaohong would come out of Shoushan Keng with a group of people, beating gongs and drums, to watch the opera; after watching, they would beat gongs and drums back.

More importantly, these outsiders gradually integrated themselves into local life.

It is said that the police bureau, while protecting government officials, would also go up Fangyan Mountain to maintain order when there were many pilgrims; military doctors in the hospital also treated locals; school students included both civil servants' children and Cheng descendants.

Ruan Yicheng, former head of the Civil Affairs Department, wrote this describing his residence and life in Fangyan: "Bamboo fences and thatched cottages, pine trees forming a forest.

A small stream before the door, crossed by a wooden bridge—my happiest rural dwelling place in life, locals call it Back Mountain.

In the seventh lunar month of 1940 (29th year of the Republic), my third son was born here, named Dafang, to record that he was born in Fangyan.

" It can be said with certainty that if not for Shoushan Keng with good air-raid conditions and the Wufeng Academy and caves beyond the reach of aerial bombing, the provincial government would not have chosen Fangyan as its wartime temporary office location.

If these city people had not come, the old street's economy and life would have been severely damaged by the war.

Fortunately, during the eight years of Japanese aggression against China, perhaps due to the protection of Guangci Temple—a famous Tiantai sect temple—and the Great Emperor Hugong, this "feng shui treasure land" of the old street not only suffered no damage but actually developed economically through the provincial government's presence.

So after the war ended, the inn industry here reached its peak.

In just three years from 1944 to 1946, 11 inns including Cheng Baochang, Cheng Qianchang, Cheng Zhengchang, Cheng Zhongxing, and Cheng Zhengxing opened successively on the old street.

These inns were mostly small and medium-sized husband-and-wife shops; except for a few located in large courtyards, most were two-story buildings along the street.

Inn income was insufficient to cover the family's annual expenses, so in off-seasons they also farmed or did other trades to supplement household income.

Changes After Liberation The inns on the old street, in the first thirty years after the founding of New China, as a microcosm, vividly recorded a series of major historical events.

People on the old street had no deep experience of war.

In their minds, the Kuomintang government was not only those approachable and real city people who once lived upstairs in their homes, but also an honest government that built roads, installed street lamps, and moved cesspits here.

So when the provincial government evacuated, many villagers were reluctant to see it go.Thus, during the transition between old and new regimes, they perhaps had the same confusion as their ancestor Cheng Quan.

Cheng Quan worried that "officials at the beginning of a dynasty cannot be sure of surviving from morning to evening"; they perhaps similarly felt uncertain about future life.

Coupled with the first few years after liberation, when various religious activities were suppressed, the old street's inns also temporarily closed due to the absence of pilgrims.

The land here had long been insufficient to support the street's population, so people recall that life was hardest then.Many young people also had to leave home to find work at that time.

This "sudden" hard life, in stark contrast to the bustling scene on the old street before liberation, inevitably made people on the old street full of doubts about the new regime.

In the early 1950s, together with tens of millions of farmers across China, people on the old street experienced the vigorous "Land Reform Movement.

" "Land Reform" aimed to abolish the feudal exploitative land ownership of the landlord class, confiscate this land, and redistribute it to the broad poor peasantry, thereby developing agricultural production.

The "Land Law" explicitly stipulated that land and property directly used for industry and commerce could not be confiscated, and the interests of industry and commerce must not be infringed.

These policies, which now seem somewhat imperfect, caused many mix-ups during implementation.Of course, these have all become part of history, imprinted on the old street's architecture and culture.

In the valley where the old street is located, farmland is very limited.Land on the old street was so scarce that it could not be measured in "mu" or even "fen," but in "ba.

" Seventy ba equaled one mu, meaning one ba was only about 9.5 square meters—a small plot about 3 meters square.In other words, the net area of one side room in Degeng Residence was larger than two ba.

The Cheng Chongshun family living near Songmen Bridge was not wealthy at that time, especially compared to those doing business and running shops, but his family owned 800 ba of land.

Because Cheng Chongshun taught away from home year-round and the children were young, 300 ba were rented out and 500 ba were farmed by hired labor, making them one of the top rich peasants on the old street—subject to both house and land division.

In contrast, families operating large inns such as Cheng Longchang, Xinlongxing, and Laolongxing, because they also farmed themselves, became middle peasants instead.

Except for the super-large inns Cheng Zhenxing and Cheng Renchang, the remaining inns did not operate year-round but were semi-agricultural and semi-commercial, with guest rooms used for storage or left vacant in off-seasons, so their property was not protected as "land and property directly used for industry and commerce.

" If a family lacked labor and hired people to farm, they were classified as landlords or rich peasants, and even guest-room property would be divided—Cheng Hongchang Inn at the north end of the old street is one example.

Conversely, the operators of Xinzhenxing, Cheng Zhenxing (i.e., Laozhenxing), Xinlongxing, and Cheng Renchang (Laorenchang) had "class status" as industry-commerce plus landlord.

The landlord portion was naturally divided, but their inns were all protected.They did not bear the heavy label of "landlord origin" but became protected "industrialists and businessmen.

" In 1951, after "land reform" ended, five inns—Xinlongxing, Cheng Renchang, Xinzhenxing, Cheng Zhenxing, and Ying Baoxing in West Village—reopened.

Starting in 1956, New China launched a socialist transformation movement of capitalist industry and commerce.At that time, the transformation of private industry and commerce adopted policies of peaceful redemption and joint state-private operation.

Conditions for joint state-private operation were: private industry and commerce with capital over 3,000 yuan, employing 5 or more workers, and voluntarily applying for joint operation.

For inn operators on the old street, what they could understand was that joint state-private operation seemed to be the only way out.

After joint operation, all houses would be sold to the state at appraised value, and they could receive payments four times a year for subsequent years.

As private-side personnel, they could choose to continue working in the inn as ordinary staff, receiving fixed wages, but no longer having decision-making power.

In April 1957, five inns—Cheng Renchang, Xinzhenxing, Cheng Zhenxing, Ying Baoxing, and Guangci Temple Guesthouse—merged into the Joint State-Private Fangyan Inn.

Xinlongxing Inn, which had reopened that year, had too few usable rooms to meet joint operation conditions, so it was forced to close again.

To this day, on the outer walls of Cheng Zhenxing and Cheng Renchang, one can still see the four characters "Fangyan Inn," over half a meter high.In 1958, the "Great Leap Forward" people's communization reached a climax.

The village implemented a large canteen system; Cheng Zhengwei Ham Shop in the middle of the old street, with its spacious courtyard and food-processing conditions, was selected as the large canteen for the whole village.

People on the old street were also busy with the backyard steelmaking campaign, taking blue bricks from their courtyard walls to fire kilns, so they had to temporarily set aside the Great Emperor Hugong who could command wind and rain.

Although Fangyan Inn remained open, it no longer received devout pilgrims, but rather people on business trips, attending meetings, or "visitation groups" organized by factories and government agencies.

Following this were three consecutive years of economic difficulty.The Jiangsu-Zhejiang region, originally a land of fish and rice, also experienced a great famine unseen in a thousand years.

Yongkang, supported by its handicraft industry, was relatively better off.

Even so, of Yongkang's 300,000 population at that time, about 100,000 left home along the Zhejiang-Jiangxi Railway for Jiangxi, because Jiangxi was an old revolutionary base area with relatively better conditions.

The Cheng descendants on the old street, originally bound by blood, also turned against each other because "your land suffered hail damage and poor harvest, I don't want to share grain with you.

" The old street was thus split in two at the location of Laolongxing Inn, called Yanshang Brigade and Yanxia Brigade respectively.

When times were hard, people naturally thought again of the omnipotent Great Emperor Hugong sitting atop Fangyan Mountain.

So figures of pilgrims from nearby villages appeared again on the old street.

The good days of 1961-1962 had barely lasted two days when, in 1963, a "Four Cleanups" movement began, aiming to "clean accounts, clean warehouses, clean work points, clean property.

" In those years, a work team from the county came and stayed in nearby Paixi.

In the words of the Cheng descendants who for generations "ate Hugong and used Hugong," their purpose was to "discredit" Hugong.

Cadres coming from near and far also held meetings in Cheng Zhaofu's ancestral hall—where operas were once performed for pilgrims—to publicize the importance of this movement and criticize the former owners of Xinrenchang and Cheng Zhongxing inns.

The inns became quiet again, and private-side personnel in the shops were gradually transferred to state-owned units such as supply and marketing cooperatives.In May 1966, the vigorous Cultural Revolution began.

At the end of August that year, most buildings of Guangci Temple atop Fangyan Mountain, including Heling Temple, were demolished.

Still, out of nearly a thousand years of affection for Hu Ze, the Pingfeng Pavilion—originally slated for demolition by Fangyan Commune—was preserved on a pretext, successively used as a village meeting place, then a warehouse, post and telecommunications school, and Factory 602.

Over the next ten years, not only pilgrims but everything related to religion and culture was forced to stop.

The remaining private-side personnel at Fangyan Inn were all replaced, and Cheng Zhaofu's ancestral hall was used as a village meeting place.

Later, inn furniture was moved away or sold, and the inn was converted into an instrument factory and warehouse.

Two years after the end of the Cultural Revolution, on the first day of the eighth lunar month in 1978, two or three women on the old street secretly carried a newly molded statue of Hu Ze up the mountain and enshrined it in Pingfeng Pavilion.

The following year, Guangci Temple welcomed its first abbot in over a decade, Master Yunming.People suppressed for over a decade surged again from all directions, prostrating before the Hugong statue.

Although people were not wealthy then, they all gave generously.They hoped Guangci Temple could rebuild its former glory, provide Hugong with a permanent dwelling, and that this hard-won good life would last long.

Unlike before, some of the long-absent pilgrims did not walk along this three-li old street.

The tractors or long-distance buses they took could drive along the gravel dirt road at the foot of Fangyan Mountain all the way to the mountain pass west of Huilong Bridge.

Meanwhile, new policies under Reform and Opening Up affirmed farmers' rights to private plots and private livestock, and encouraged them to develop household sideline businesses.

So some Yanxia Village farmers confident in Reform and Opening Up opened small restaurants on their private plots by the road.

Unexpectedly, business grew increasingly prosperous; the small restaurants evolved from temporary sheds to spacious and bright brick-tile houses, also operating inn businesses.

In 1985, this gravel dirt road was transformed into a cement road over ten meters wide.Since then, more inns, restaurants, and general stores opened on both sides.

By the mid-1990s, all inns had brand-new facades on the new street.

These new buildings of varying heights had fully open ground floors, usable as both restaurants and parking; rooms on the second floor and above generally had half with private bathrooms, while the other half could serve as restaurant "private rooms" or budget guest rooms.

It seemed everything had changed, but what remained unchanged was the culture retained in this industry.Inns still charged lodging guests by "one night and two meals," with high, medium, and low standards.

Devout pilgrims need only spend one-fifth of the Fangyan mountain entrance ticket to stay one night and eat two meals at an inn, and can still hear inexplicable terms like "water shuttle" and "sharp mouth."

关键术语 Key Terms (382)
吃胡公的行业 现在永康人不用远赴他乡 现代化机器运输工具替代双手扁担 永康制作送到府府县县 老街上只剩一位铁匠程明星1944年生圣字辈程氏22代 松门桥以南老街以东第一幢房子 小作坊院落西北角临街 红彤彤炉火前挥舞铁锤 嘴里叼半支香烟青烟随叮当声翩翩起舞 房子一百多年历史太公祖父先后建成 以前卖酒卖瓷器供香客歇脚 土改时他家800把地70把一亩 岩下村数一数二富农 富农身份父亲1958年反右失去工作全家生活窘迫 六十年代初离开家到新昌学打铁后来江西浙江 程茂发旺季新街卖菱角土特产淡季儿子小工厂帮忙 四十多年背井离乡走街串巷打铁生涯终生烙印 老人背部扭曲走路一跛一跛 背井离乡日子辛苦年龄小学徒 尊师如父精神抢着挑重担干重活 比师傅早起晚睡吃饭看师傅脸色吃不饱常事 程明星师傅待他好没吃过苦 每天5点起床师傅起来前做好所有事 泥巴修炉子烧开水木炭浆好调水准备开工 烧水沏茶师傅洗脸水挑水 每天工作量按件计算两人一天8件大件或10件小件 器件多为农具生活用具 剪子最难打还算小件打起来最吃亏 行业规矩讲究 老街上工匠不多发展晚没尊崇鲁班太上老君行业神 ...等共382个
四、方岩百货
中文 Chinese

旅游经济中必不可少的就是独具特色的旅游纪念品。据民国时期《永康县志》记载："岩下街有商店100余家，方岩山顶有商店20多家，多经营京货、杂货。"到改革开放以后，方岩镇共有售商店280多家，摊位70余个。

其中大部分都是经营当地制作的传统旅游工艺品，俗称方岩货，其中包括各种儿童玩具、纸花和雨伞等。纸花 最具特色的方岩货就属方岩纸花。纸花的制作始于何时已经无从考证，但是据1991年编纂的《永康县志》记载，它在"清代乾隆年间制作已盛"。

做纸花用的纸通常有大红、浅红、粉色、黄色、蓝色等几种颜色，但是配以不同样式的剪裁，层次或套色，便可以做出茶花、牡丹花、菊花、月季花、蔷薇花、杜鹃花、芙蓉花、紫罗兰等品种，煞是艳丽。在方岩山脚下曾被称为"花廊"的街上，现在只有一个卖纸花的摊位，摊位的主人是年近古稀的老人白玲珑。

白玲珑老人并不是当地人，她在解放前随父亲从广东乐昌县迁到永康来谋生，后嫁入程家，现住在方岩山脚的岩上村。据她回忆，在她30来岁的时候，她便和村里的许多妇女们开始做纸花。制作纸花的工艺是从当时寄住在方岩山半山腰的罗汉洞里，一个自称是来自丽水缙云县，名叫"老杨"的流浪汉手上学到的。

她和村里的其他妇女一样，用饭菜和"老杨"换来了学做牡丹花的方法。她们在此基础上稍作变化，便形成了今天这么多的品种。纸花的制作分成"凿"、"染"和"制"三道工序。第一步就是用一把刀口形状近似为初月的小凿子给一打一打的彩纸勾画出花或叶的轮廓。"染"就是用和彩纸协调的颜色，在"花瓣"尖上做一点点套色和退晕。这好比是画龙点睛的一笔，如果恰到好处不但可以增加纸花的立体感，还可以使色彩更加艳丽。

白玲珑老人讲"染"是最关键的环节，也最费时间的。她通常都是把一叠一叠染过色的彩纸放在鞋盒大小的盒子里，连同铁丝和糨糊一起带上山，在山上只需几分钟的时间就可以做出一朵栩栩如生的纸花。据老人回忆，"文革"结束后，随着拜胡公的人数逐渐增加，纸花的生意也越来越好。

当时在这条不足一百米的街上，有七八十个小摊位，仅卖纸花的人就有五六十人，而来拜胡公的人也干脆称它为"胡公花"。在拜胡公的淡季，她还会和其他妇女一样，带上几盒染好色的彩纸到外省去卖。她自己就去过福建、江西、湖南等地，其中以湖北和广东生意最好。每次一走就是一个月左右，等把带去的材料都用完了就回来。由于纸花当时只卖五分钱一束，所以后来火车票贵了，也就不去了。

近一二十年，由于方岩山门票价格的逐渐上涨，再加上塑料替代品的盛行，卖纸花的人也就越来越少了。但是村里的妇女们，包括老人的女儿、媳妇都会做纸花，她们会在逢年过节或喜庆的日子里做上几朵以增添喜气，有人会把纸花插在龙灯上，有人也会拿出来卖。玩具 方岩制作玩具的历史悠久，而且种类繁多、做工精巧。这其中不但有乖巧可爱的，还有能动会叫的。

例如，有竹子做的竹箫、节节龙，泥塑的会啼叫的小雄鸡，木头雕的小鸡吃米、小宝剑，还有金属铸的响铃和钗钹等。而这其中最有名的就是用各种材料做的哨子。最普通的就是金属铸的白铁哨，声音嘹亮。木竹哨，又叫喇叭哨，约有拇指大小，声音粗犷。里面是一个竹子做嘴儿，外面套一个木质的、漆成大红色的小圆筒。泥哨就是用泥塑成小鱼、小鸡等小动物形状，吹的时候对准动物的嘴，靠留空的内腔震动而发出清脆的声音。最有特点的要算泥制的皮老虎。

它两端各有一个泥做的圆板，外侧分别画上老虎的头和尾，中间用褶皱的纸相连，靠双手收放两边的圆板发声。如今，传统玩具却面临着塑料玩具和电动玩具的挑战。雨伞 方岩的油纸花伞也是远近闻名。雨伞的样式主要分为三种：卖给老年人用的雨伞都比较大；卖给成年人用的多绘有图案，还有特别给女青年准备的漂亮花伞；卖给小孩子用的伞不但小，伞把儿还做成一个可以吹响的哨子。油纸花伞的制作工序有几十道，从选料到做工都十分讲究。做伞架用的毛竹一般要去芝英买，因为那里产的毛竹竹壁较厚。

毛竹先要泡，晒干后削成一段一段的，再用发线穿好做成伞骨，其边缘还要用棉纱线绕上几圈，以确保结实耐用。伞面用的棉纸则要去诸暨、义乌采购，棉纸要裁成三角形，然后放进柿子油里浆一下。柿子的选择也是有讲究的，要在五六月的时候到新楼去买没有熟的柿子，拿回来打散、取出子后再放进装满水的大缸里待用。浆好的棉纸会逐一的被裱在伞骨上，晾干后就可以在上面描绘花、鸟、山、水等图案了。

程振丰（1939年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，做伞最难的环节是"接油"，油是桐油和青油的混合，只有上了油，纸伞才可以防水。"接油"的同时要用明火烘干，所以"火候"是最重要的，油太老容易坏，太嫩则不容易打开。在老街的南端，靠近回龙桥附近，原来有好几家自产自销的雨伞店。住在桥头的程寿月老人说他家在解放前一直是靠做雨伞、卖雨伞为生。

他还得意地讲，他家做的伞在旺季时会供不应求，于是不得不从温州买一些雨伞回来，再卖给上方岩的温州人。他长大以后，钢骨布伞、塑料伞逐渐替代了纸伞，他也只好改行做漆匠。程振丰老人的祖父和父亲都在老街尽端做纸伞生意，据他说，有一年阴历九月初九一天就卖了1900多把纸伞。金华火腿 在老街上，还有一个极负盛名的特产，那就是金华火腿。

在历史上，现在的永康、义乌、东阳、浦江、金华等地都属金华府管辖，所以在这些地区内生产的火腿统称为"金华火腿"，而永康则是金华火腿的主要产地之一。金华地区，民间腌制火腿始于唐代。在唐开元年间（713—742年），陈藏器撰写的《本草拾遗》中就提道，"火腿，产金华者佳"。到南宋，金华火腿已经被列为贡品。

以前岩下老街家家都养猪，出笼的猪会在腊月十五后被宰掉。一般人家都会把猪腿卖给村里做火腿生意的人家，自己只留下猪头和内脏过年。只有有钱的人家才会把猪腿腌起来留着自己吃，把猪肠拿来做香肠。这种冬天腌制的火腿叫"冬腿"，和春腿相比，冬腿味道更为鲜美，而且可以储藏较长的时间。老街上做的火腿基本上都是以后腿为原料的"冬腿"，前腿由于肥肉太多而味道欠佳，所以并不是首选。

做火腿的第一步是先要把猪腿用盐炒一下，然后放进大缸里腌，每次腌十几天，前后腌三次。腌好后先要蒸一个晚上，随后拿到太阳下晒三天，最后在走廊下挂上半个多月。这样做好的火腿可以保证两年不会坏。在岩下老街上有几家专做火腿生意的店铺，其中以位于上胡坑坑口的程正伟火腿店最为著名。

年已七十多岁的程圣言（1935年生，圣字辈，程氏22代）老人是这家的后人。据他讲，太公程正伟经营火腿生意起家，并在十九世纪中叶，盖了紧邻老街西侧的一幢东西向两进院落，前院地面还用卵石铺八卦图案。后来，生意由爷爷程宣尧接任并不断扩大，又贴着老屋的西墙盖了一幢南北向的两进院落。

老屋因雕饰精美，还曾是文保单位，但是不幸毁于火灾。解放前，火腿店因程宣尧的去世而随之关门，但是这座建筑却成了当年那红红火火生意的见证。这座建筑走廊和厅堂屋面下的梁枋，不但格外粗壮，而且还比通常做法更密。梁枋的侧面，每隔四寸左右还会伸出一个木楔子。

程圣言老人讲这是挂火腿用的，以前走廊下、厅堂里，前院、后院都挂满了火腿，足有上千只。因为火腿生意和旅店生意一样，都是季节性的。所以老街上有几家大的旅店，在住宿的淡季也兼做火腿生意，程仁昌旅店就是一例。

据《永康名店名厂》记载，每年当秋天的香汛过后，店里就开始准备腌制火腿。生意好的时候，每年可以腌制4000只。火腿的招牌为"雪舫仁昌隆记"，并按"天地玄黄，宇宙洪荒"八个字，编号并运往杭州销售。还有一些中小型的店面，例如程振昌，为了能在和大店的竞争中生存下来，主人不但要苦心经营，还要做到面面俱到。它们不但做火腿生意，还利用沿街的房屋开店。

一边卖方岩货，还有从青田贩来的石雕，另一边卖自家做的火腿。

· · · · · ·
英文 English

Indispensable to the tourism economy are distinctive tourist souvenirs.

According to the Yongkang County Gazetteer from the Republican period: "Yanxia Street has over 100 shops, and atop Fangyan Mountain there are over 20 shops, mostly selling Beijing goods and general merchandise.

" After Reform and Opening Up, Fangyan Town had over 280 retail shops and more than 70 stalls.

Most of these sold traditional tourist handicrafts made locally, commonly known as Fangyan goods, including various children's toys, paper flowers, and umbrellas.

Paper Flowers The most distinctive Fangyan goods are Fangyan paper flowers.

It is impossible to verify when paper flower making began, but according to the Yongkang County Gazetteer compiled in 1991, it was "already flourishing during the Qianlong period of the Qing Dynasty.

" Paper used for making paper flowers usually comes in several colors: bright red, light red, pink, yellow, and blue.

But with different styles of cutting, layering, or color overlaying, one can make varieties such as camellia, peony, chrysanthemum, Chinese rose, rose, azalea, hibiscus, and violet, all brilliantly colorful.

On the street at the foot of Fangyan Mountain, once known as the "Flower Corridor," there is now only one paper flower stall.Its owner is Bai Linglong, an elderly woman nearly seventy years old.

Elder Bai Linglong is not a local; before liberation she moved with her father from Lechang County in Guangdong to Yongkang to make a living, later married into the Cheng family, and now lives in Yanshang Village at the foot of Fangyan Mountain.

According to her recollection, when she was around thirty, she and many other women in the village began making paper flowers.

The craft of making paper flowers was learned from a wanderer who called himself "Old Yang," claiming to be from Jinyun County in Lishui, who was then living in Luohan Cave halfway up Fangyan Mountain.

Like other women in the village, she exchanged meals for "Old Yang" to teach her how to make peonies.On this basis they made slight variations, eventually forming so many varieties today.

Paper flower making is divided into three processes: "chisel," "dye," and "assemble.

" The first step is to use a small chisel with a blade shaped like a crescent moon to outline the contours of flowers or leaves on stacks of colored paper.

"Dyeing" means using colors coordinated with the colored paper to add a little color overlay and gradient at the tips of the "petals.

" This is like the finishing touch that brings a painting to life; if done just right, it not only increases the three-dimensional effect of the paper flower but also makes the colors more vivid.

Elder Bai Linglong says "dyeing" is the most critical step and also the most time-consuming.

She usually puts stacks of dyed colored paper in shoebox-sized boxes, brings them up the mountain together with wire and paste, and can make a lifelike paper flower in just a few minutes on the mountain.

According to the old woman's recollection, after the end of the Cultural Revolution, as the number of people worshipping Hugong gradually increased, the paper flower business grew better and better.

At that time, on this street less than one hundred meters long, there were seventy to eighty small stalls, with fifty to sixty people selling only paper flowers, and people coming to worship Hugong simply called them "Hugong flowers.

" During the off-season for Hugong worship, she would, like other women, take several boxes of dyed colored paper to other provinces to sell.

She herself went to Fujian, Jiangxi, Hunan, and other places, with the best business in Hubei and Guangdong.Each trip lasted about a month, and she returned only after using up all the materials she brought.

Since paper flowers then sold for only five fen a bunch, when train tickets became more expensive later, she stopped going.

In the past one or two decades, due to the gradual increase in Fangyan Mountain ticket prices and the prevalence of plastic substitutes, fewer and fewer people sell paper flowers.

But the women in the village, including the old woman's daughter and daughter-in-law, all know how to make paper flowers.

They make a few during festivals or喜庆 days to add festivity; some insert paper flowers on dragon lanterns, and some take them out to sell.

Toys Fangyan has a long history of making toys, with a great variety and exquisite workmanship.Among them are not only cute and lovely ones, but also ones that can move and make sounds.

For example, there are bamboo xiao flutes and jointed dragons made of bamboo, clay little roosters that crow, wooden carved chicks pecking at rice and small swords, as well as metal-cast bells and cymbals.

The most famous among them are whistles made of various materials.The most common are white iron whistles cast in metal, with a loud and clear sound.Wooden-bamboo whistles, also called horn whistles, are about the size of a thumb, with a rough sound.

Inside is a bamboo mouthpiece, and outside is covered with a wooden small cylinder painted bright red.

Clay whistles are made of clay shaped into small animals such as little fish and chicks; when blown, one aims at the animal's mouth, and the hollow interior vibrates to produce a crisp sound.The most distinctive must be the clay-made leather tiger.

It has a clay round plate at each end, with the tiger's head and tail painted on the outer sides respectively, connected in the middle by pleated paper, producing sound by the hands squeezing and releasing the round plates on both sides.

Today, however, traditional toys face challenges from plastic toys and electric toys.Umbrellas Fangyan's oiled paper flower umbrellas are also famous far and near.

Umbrella styles are mainly divided into three types: umbrellas sold to the elderly are relatively large; those sold to adults are mostly painted with patterns, and there are also beautiful flower umbrellas specially prepared for young women; umbrellas sold to children are not only small, but the handle is also made into a whistle that can be blown.

The making of oiled paper flower umbrellas involves dozens of processes, with great attention paid from material selection to workmanship.

The moso bamboo used for umbrella frames is generally bought in Zhiying, because the moso bamboo produced there has thicker walls.

The moso bamboo must first be soaked, dried in the sun, then cut into sections, threaded with hairline to make umbrella ribs, and its edges must also be wrapped with cotton yarn several times to ensure sturdiness and durability.

The cotton paper used for the umbrella surface must be purchased in Zhuji and Yiwu; the cotton paper is cut into triangles, then dipped in persimmon oil for sizing.

The selection of persimmons is also particular: in May and June, one goes to Xinlou to buy unripe persimmons, brings them back, crushes them, removes the seeds, and puts them in large water-filled jars for later use.

The sized cotton paper is then pasted one by one onto the umbrella ribs; after drying, patterns such as flowers, birds, mountains, and water can be painted on it.

Elder Cheng Zhenfeng (born 1939, Chong generation, 21st generation of the Cheng family) says the hardest part of making umbrellas is "applying oil"; the oil is a mixture of tung oil and green oil, and only after oiling can the paper umbrella be waterproof.

While "applying oil," it must be dried over an open flame, so "heat control" is most important—oil that is too old is easily damaged, while oil that is too tender is difficult to open.

At the southern end of the old street, near Huilong Bridge, there were originally several umbrella shops that made and sold their own products.

Elder Cheng Shouyue, who lives at the bridgehead, says his family before liberation always made a living by making and selling umbrellas.

He also proudly says that during peak seasons, the umbrellas his family made were in short supply, so they had to buy some umbrellas from Wenzhou and resell them to Wenzhou people coming up Fangyan.

After he grew up, steel-ribbed cloth umbrellas and plastic umbrellas gradually replaced paper umbrellas, so he had to switch to being a lacquerer.

Elder Cheng Zhenfeng's grandfather and father both did paper umbrella business at the end of the old street; according to him, one year on the ninth day of the ninth lunar month, over 1,900 paper umbrellas were sold in a single day.

Jinhua Ham On the old street, there is also a highly renowned specialty: Jinhua ham.

Historically, present-day Yongkang, Yiwu, Dongyang, Pujiang, Jinhua, and other places were all under the jurisdiction of Jinhua Prefecture, so ham produced in these areas is collectively called "Jinhua ham," and Yongkang is one of the main production areas of Jinhua ham.

In the Jinhua region, folk ham curing began in the Tang Dynasty.

During the Kaiyuan period of the Tang Dynasty (713-742), Chen Cangqi wrote in "Supplement to Materia Medica": "Ham, those produced in Jinhua are excellent.

" By the Southern Song Dynasty, Jinhua ham had already been listed as a tribute.

In the past, every family on Yanxia Old Street raised pigs, and pigs ready for market were slaughtered after the fifteenth day of the twelfth lunar month.

Ordinary families would sell the pig legs to people in the village who did ham business, keeping only the pig's head and internal organs for New Year.

Only wealthy families would cure pig legs to keep for their own consumption, and use pig intestines to make sausages.

This kind of ham cured in winter is called "winter leg"; compared with spring leg, winter leg tastes more delicious and can be stored for a longer time.

The ham made on the old street is basically "winter leg" made from hind legs; front legs, due to too much fat and inferior taste, are not the first choice.

The first step in making ham is to stir-fry the pig leg with salt, then put it in a large jar to cure, each time for over ten days, curing three times in total.

After curing, it must first be steamed for one night, then taken out to sun for three days, and finally hung under the corridor for over half a month.Ham made this way can be guaranteed not to spoil for two years.

On Yanxia Old Street there are several shops specializing in ham business, among which Cheng Zhengwei Ham Shop, located at the mouth of Shanghu Keng, is the most famous.

Elder Cheng Shengyan (born 1935, Sheng generation, 22nd generation of the Cheng family), over seventy years old, is a descendant of this family.

According to him, his great-grandfather Cheng Zhengwei started the ham business and, in the mid-nineteenth century, built an east-west two-courtyard compound immediately west of the old street, with the front courtyard floor paved with cobblestones in a Bagua pattern.

Later, the business was taken over and continuously expanded by his grandfather Cheng Xuanyao, who built another north-south two-courtyard compound against the west wall of the old house.

The old house, due to its exquisite carvings, was once a cultural relic protection unit, but unfortunately was destroyed by fire.

Before liberation, the ham shop closed following Cheng Xuanyao's death, but this building remains a witness to the thriving business of those years.

The beams and rafters under the corridor and hall roofs of this building are not only exceptionally thick and sturdy, but also denser than usual.

On the sides of the beams, about every four inches, a wooden wedge protrudes.

Elder Cheng Shengyan says these were for hanging ham; in the past, under the corridors, in the halls, and in both front and rear courtyards, ham hung everywhere, totaling over a thousand.

Because ham business, like inn business, is seasonal, several large inns on the old street also did ham business during the off-season for lodging, and Cheng Renchang Inn is one example.

According to "Famous Stores and Factories of Yongkang," every year after the autumn incense season, the shop begins preparing to cure ham.In good business years, up to 4,000 hams could be cured annually.

The ham's brand was "Xuefang Renchang Longji," numbered according to the eight characters "Heaven, Earth, Dark, Yellow, Universe, Vast, Flood, Wilderness," and shipped to Hangzhou for sale.

There were also some small and medium-sized shops, such as Cheng Zhenchang; to survive in competition with large shops, the owners not only had to manage painstakingly but also be comprehensive in every aspect.

They not only did ham business but also used street-facing houses to open shops.

On one side they sold Fangyan goods and stone carvings brought from Qingtian, and on the other side they sold homemade ham.

第四章 岩下老街的建筑和空间

5
节
一、老街
中文 Chinese

建筑是生活的舞台，同时也是文化的载体。通过描绘老街上的住宅、祠堂、宗教建筑、桥梁和塘堰等不同的建筑和空间形式，可以从侧面，更生动地展示这里的生活模式。通过讲述发生在老街上、厅堂里，还有水塘边的故事，可以更深入、更真实地了解这里的乡土文化。

一、老街 让我们随着当年香客的脚步，再走一遍这三里多长的老街吧。想象今天是民国36年（1947年）阴历九月初八，一个秋高气爽的下午，太阳已经渐渐西斜。当地人通常会用"重阳初八夜，岩上岩下要撑破。有草席，无稻借"来形容这天的热闹场面。这时的确有许多成群结队的、操着各地口音的虔诚香客。

想必他们都是在急着找店住下，以便可以连夜上山。老街北头的第一段属橙麓村境内。当老街和溪水肩并肩的时候，就离热闹不远了。老街在大溪桥附近拐了两拐，路面临水的一侧有连排的石栏杆，想必是为了防止香客拥挤掉入溪水而设的。栏杆上还刻了"香客捐造"的字样，有一个还是光绪四年（1878年）建造的。

从大溪桥到松门桥这一段，在右手一侧肩并肩地坐落着一连串的旅店，依次是程同昌、程元昌、程洪昌、程隆昌和程乾昌；左手边上偶尔有几家卖方岩货的小店。人虽然很多，但还好视野开阔，可以看到不远处散落在田野间的几幢宅院和袅袅升起的炊烟，还能隐约听见涓涓的溪水声。

松门桥是一个长约10米，宽约1.5米的石板桥，同时也是橙麓村和岩下村的村界。过了松门桥，老街稍适宽阔，路边的店面也变得稀疏了许多。夹道而坐的尽是些挑着担子来卖柑橘、菱角、小五金和熏豆腐干的小商贩；有时甚至旁边还会陪上几个乞丐。

他们不会去向旁边卖熏豆腐干的当地人讨一块豆腐吃，反倒是跟着我们，嘴里念念有词的亨唱着"上岩烧香、下岩修福、有福不修、回家火烧屋"，"大嫂嫂，大姑娘，大妈妈，烧烧香、修修福、多子多孙"等小曲儿。这一段的老街，西边是突兀陡峭的狮子山，东边是一片田地，山谷的北侧就是寿山坑的坑口。

街两边稀稀松松的旅店，除了程大昌一家外，余下的都是近几年才盖好的新房，而且多半是没有内院的临街店面。继续往前，直到老街上横跨有两个路亭，路亭之间的大门紧锁，门上写着"周氏宗祠"四个字。

据说是草墅周氏的后代，几百年前在这里居住过很短的时间，后来不知什么原因搬走了，却留下了这个祠堂和祠堂后面山坡上的祖坟。路亭一侧坐着四五个从远道赶来的艺人，他们趁着这里阴凉、人又少，填饱肚子后再打个盹儿、养精蓄锐，好在当晚唱遍各家旅店。

由此向南，眼前的这段老街是最热闹的，两三米宽的街道两旁店家林立，其中程仁昌、新仁昌、程振兴和新振兴是超大型的豪华旅店，程成昌和新隆兴规模也不小。人们摩肩接踵的簇拥在高墙林立、狭窄的街道上，步履维艰。

不禁踮脚向前张望，可以看到不远处有几顶上颠下颤的轿子，旁边随行着几个青布长衫，文质彬彬的乡下先生，猜想轿子里坐的多半是从上海来的，商定好要住程振兴的阔太太们。两旁的店里不时会窜出来个貌似跑堂的伙计，嘴里嘟囔着一些诸如"尖嘴"、"水梭"等奇怪的字眼。眼前也时常会有三两个十来岁的男孩，肩上扛着系有红布条的行李，从大人们的腿边挤过，一溜烟儿地钻进旁边的店里。

透过一扇虚掩的大门向旅店院里张望，看到院子角落里摆着好几摞凳子，旁边还竖着许多一米多宽的木板，地上又堆了许多稻草，想必这是给打地铺的客人准备的。这段老街末尾是两家几乎是面对面的火腿店。过了火腿店，视野再次开阔。眼前夹在方岩山和蜈蚣山之间的，是一片绿油油的田地，地里长满了近人高的玉米和夹在中间的萝卜菜。

田地之间靠蜈蚣山的一侧，孤零零地矗立着一幢连排的大宅子，紧紧地卡在老街和方岩溪之间。这幢连排的宅子有三个院子，原属三兄弟，其中两家香火不盛，才有空房开旅店，分别为义丰雅记和义丰老店。

义丰雅记是有一百五十多年历史的宅院，内部建筑雕饰异常精美；而义丰老店则是老街上历史最长的旅店之一，风风雨雨已将近百年。老街的最后一段，是程氏迁到这里时最早的聚居地，因此这一片的建筑是鳞次栉比，街道也是三步一曲、九步一折。

由于空房不多，所以旅店只有规模不大的程永成和程阮阮，以及老街上的第一家旅店老隆兴。余下的都是临街卖百货、食品或香烛的小店。这样一来，原本既曲折又窄小的街道就显得更加紧凑。

这些店面多集中在老隆兴旅店前后，还有回龙桥附近，余下的部分就都是高墙大院的住宅。这些香客们只想趁机在晚饭之前，采购一些当地的特产。他们假装若无其事地审视着台面上琳琅满目的货品：花花绿绿的雨伞、长长短短的蜡烛、以假乱真的纸花、会动的小鸡吃米、能出声的皮老虎、腌制好的整只火腿、还有各式各样的小五金及竹编制品；随手拿起一样，和老板讨价还价。

每走不远，还可以透过两边建筑之间的夹道看到后面的水塘，还有坐在水塘边洗衣服、唠家常的妇女们，以及追逐嬉戏的孩童。老街的尽端，有一个叫回龙桥的小型石拱桥。桥的栏杆上还记载着它的历史，即建于光绪二十三年（1897年），重建于民国29年（1940年）。回龙桥前面，老街就被一分为二了。

如果继续向南，依照桥栏杆上所刻"处州方岩大路"，就会通向处州；走回龙桥跨过方岩溪向西近百米，才是上方岩山的山路。至此，三里长的老街也就画上了句号。

· · · · · ·
英文 English

Architecture is the stage of life and also the carrier of culture.

By depicting different architectural and spatial forms on the old street—residences, ancestral halls, religious buildings, bridges, and ponds—we can more vividly show the lifestyle here from a side perspective.

By telling stories that happened on the old street, in the halls, and by the ponds, we can gain a deeper and more authentic understanding of the local vernacular culture.1.

The Old Street Let us follow in the footsteps of the pilgrims of yesteryear and walk once more along this over-three-li-long old street.

Imagine today is the eighth day of the ninth lunar month in the 36th year of the Republic (1947), a crisp autumn afternoon with the sun gradually slanting west.

Locals would usually describe the bustling scene of this day with: "On the night of the Double Ninth's eighth day, Yanshang and Yanxia are packed to bursting.With straw mats, but no rice to borrow.

" At this time, indeed there were many groups of devout pilgrims speaking various regional accents.Presumably they were all hurrying to find inns to stay in, so that they could go up the mountain that very night.

The first section at the north end of the old street belongs to the territory of Chenglu Village.When the old street runs shoulder to shoulder with the stream, one is not far from the bustle.

The old street makes two turns near Daxi Bridge, and on the side facing the water there are continuous rows of stone railings, presumably built to prevent pilgrims from crowding and falling into the stream.

The railings are also engraved with the words "donated and built by pilgrims," and one was built in the fourth year of Guangxu (1878).

Along the section from Daxi Bridge to Songmen Bridge, on the right-hand side, a string of inns stand shoulder to shoulder, in order: Cheng Tongchang, Cheng Yuanchang, Cheng Hongchang, Cheng Longchang, and Cheng Qianchang; on the left-hand side there are occasionally a few small shops selling Fangyan goods.

Although there were many people, fortunately the view was open—one could see several courtyard houses scattered among the fields not far away, with wisps of cooking smoke rising, and could faintly hear the gurgling sound of the stream.

Songmen Bridge is a stone slab bridge about 10 meters long and 1.5 meters wide, and also serves as the village boundary between Chenglu Village and Yanxia Village.

After crossing Songmen Bridge, the old street becomes slightly wider, and the roadside shops become much sparser.

Sitting along both sides of the road are all small vendors carrying poles to sell oranges, water chestnuts, small hardware, and smoked dried tofu; sometimes there are even a few beggars beside them.

They would not ask the locals selling smoked dried tofu next to them for a piece of tofu to eat, but instead follow us, chanting little tunes with words in their mouths such as "Go up the rock to burn incense, go down the rock to cultivate blessings; if you have blessings but do not cultivate them, go home to a burning house," and "Big sister-in-law, big girl, big mama, burn incense, cultivate blessings, many sons and many grandsons.

" Along this section of the old street, to the west is the abruptly steep Lion Mountain, to the east is a stretch of farmland, and on the north side of the valley is the mouth of Shoushan Keng.

The inns sparsely lining both sides of the street, except for Cheng Dachang, are all new houses built only in recent years, and most are street-facing storefronts without inner courtyards.

Continuing forward, until two road pavilions straddle the old street, with the gate between them locked tight, bearing the four characters "Zhou Family Ancestral Hall.

" It is said that descendants of the Caoshu Zhou family lived here for a very short time several hundred years ago, and later moved away for unknown reasons, but left behind this ancestral hall and the ancestral graves on the hillside behind it.

On one side of the road pavilion sit four or five performers who have come from afar; taking advantage of the shade and few people here, they fill their stomachs then take a nap to conserve energy, so that they can sing at every inn that evening.

From here southward, the section of old street before us is the busiest—on both sides of the two-to-three-meter-wide street, shops stand in great numbers, among which Cheng Renchang, Xinrenchang, Cheng Zhenxing, and Xinzhenxing are super-large luxury inns, and Cheng Chengchang and Xinlongxing are also not small in scale.

People jostle each other, crowding on the narrow street lined with high walls, walking with difficulty.

One cannot help but tiptoe to look ahead, and can see several sedan chairs bobbing up and down not far away, accompanied by a few scholarly country gentlemen in blue cotton gowns; one guesses that those sitting in the sedan chairs are mostly wealthy ladies from Shanghai who have arranged to stay at Cheng Zhenxing.

From the shops on both sides, a waiter-looking fellow would occasionally dart out, muttering strange words such as "sharp mouth" and "water shuttle.

" Before one's eyes there would often be two or three ten-year-old boys, carrying luggage tied with red cloth strips on their shoulders, squeezing past the adults' legs and darting into a nearby shop in a flash.

Peering through a half-closed gate into an inn courtyard, one sees several stacks of stools in the corner, many one-meter-plus-wide wooden boards standing beside them, and piles of straw on the ground—presumably prepared for guests sleeping on floor mats.

At the end of this section of the old street are two ham shops almost facing each other.Past the ham shops, the view opens up again.

Before us, sandwiched between Fangyan Mountain and Centipede Mountain, is a stretch of green farmland, filled with nearly person-high corn and radish greens growing in between.

Between the fields, on the side toward Centipede Mountain, a large connected compound stands alone, tightly wedged between the old street and Fangyan Stream.

This connected compound has three courtyards, originally belonging to three brothers; two of them had weak family lines, so they had spare rooms to open inns, named Yifeng Yaji and Yifeng Laodian respectively.

Yifeng Yaji is a courtyard house with over 150 years of history, with exceptionally exquisite architectural carvings inside; while Yifeng Laodian is one of the oldest inns on the old street, having weathered storms for nearly a hundred years.

The last section of the old street was the earliest settlement area when the Cheng family moved here, so the buildings in this area are densely packed, and the street also winds with a turn every three steps and a bend every nine steps.

Because there were few spare rooms, the inns here are only the modest-sized Cheng Yongcheng and Cheng Ruanyuan, as well as Laolongxing, the first inn on the old street.

The rest are small street-facing shops selling general merchandise, food, or incense and candles.As a result, the originally winding and narrow street appears even more compact.

These shops are mostly concentrated around Laolongxing Inn and near Huilong Bridge, while the remaining parts are all high-walled courtyard residences.

These pilgrims only wanted to take the opportunity to buy some local specialties before dinner.

They pretended to be nonchalant as they examined the dazzling array of goods on the counters: colorful umbrellas, candles of various lengths, paper flowers indistinguishable from real ones, moving chicks pecking at rice, sounding leather tigers, whole cured hams, and various small hardware and bamboo-woven products; they would casually pick up one item and haggle with the shopkeeper.

Every short distance, one could also see through the alley between buildings on both sides the pond behind, as well as women sitting by the pond washing clothes and chatting, and children chasing and playing.

At the end of the old street, there is a small stone arch bridge called Huilong Bridge.

The bridge's railings also record its history: built in the 23rd year of Guangxu (1897), rebuilt in the 29th year of the Republic (1940).In front of Huilong Bridge, the old street is split in two.

If one continues southward, following the inscription "Chuzhou-Fangyan Main Road" carved on the bridge railing, one will reach Chuzhou; crossing Huilong Bridge over Fangyan Stream and going west for nearly a hundred meters is the mountain path up Fangyan Mountain.

At this point, the three-li-long old street comes to an end.
