二、住宅
中文 Chinese

住宅是最基本的建筑类型，也是祠堂、寺庙等其他建筑形式的原型。对一幢住宅的建造背景、住宅建筑本身的研究，再加上对其居住历史、居住状况的了解，是掌握该地区自然条件、社会状况和文化传统的重要途径。一幢住宅所包含的这些信息，是通过人的建造和使用积累下来的。

这里的"人"，不是一个人，也不仅仅是一个家庭中的几代人，而是来自于不同家庭，甚至不同家族的若干代人前前后后共同完成的。在这个漫长的过程中，"自然条件、社会状况和文化传统在变异，而且先前形成的住宅又限制和塑造着人们对住宅的建造理念和使用方式。这是一个没有尽头的相互磨合的过程"。与此同时，建筑本身也在被悄无声息地改变着。这种改变可能是有痕迹的，例如这里加了一扇窗，或是那里少了一堵墙。

在更多的情况下，这种改变是看不见痕迹的，例如卧室变成了卖货的小店，供祖宗的厅堂改作储藏等。所有这些变化，都上演着居住者们自己的故事。住宅如人，也有生老病死，胖瘦美丑。了解一个民族，并不能只关心漂亮的年轻人，了解住宅亦如此。

以下列举的住宅，其建造年代涵盖了从明末到解放前四百多年的时间，它们的建筑质量和保留状况也并不一定是老街上最好的，却是具有代表性的。对每一幢住宅的介绍，都力求在了解"创建者的身份和地位、家庭和经历、财力和学识"的基础之上，并且关注建筑本身，及其使用情况在"磨合过程"中的发展和变化。

得耕居——早期住宅建筑 背景 得耕居是现存建筑中历史最悠久的一幢，它被岩下村的程氏尊为祖屋。

"得耕"两字是为纪念岩下村程氏始祖程权（1333—1385年），以及他所建造的厅堂中有匾为"得耕"的住宅。这座住宅的原址就在得耕居门前水塘的东侧。

得耕居最早由七世祖程鑅（1474—1548年）建于十六世纪初，但是清乾隆年间（1736—1795年）的一场大火烧毁了除前厅和"树德堂"以外的所有房屋。村里的人认为，前厅之所以能幸免于难，是因为有礼部侍郎程文德的墨宝悬挂于此。

乾隆己酉年（1789年），得耕居得以重建，并保留至今。环境 得耕居坐落在上坑坑口内，老街西侧，朝向基本上是坐北朝南。俗话说，"山旺人丁水旺财"，程鑅在选址和建造这座新宅的时候，为了程家日后能人丁兴旺、财源茂盛，不但充分考虑了周围的环境，还对不利的因素，人为地做了相应的调整，使其具有背山面水的理想格局。

得耕居以蜈蚣山为靠山，住宅的北墙几乎紧挨着其西端的第一个小山包。祖山是在蜈蚣山北面，更为高大的狮子山。案山则是在它南面一百多米的朱公山。朱公山又叫"前山头"，是蜥蜴山的第一个小山包。在正对着得耕居的地方，山上原有一棵大柏树，柏树下原有供奉"朱相公"的本保庙。

关于朱相公来历，据民间传说，朱相公的原型为朱丹溪（1281—1358年），金华人，是元代著名的医学家，死后成为当地主要的民间神祇之一。依照风水术的描述，"玉带环腰"是"吉宅"的一大特点，也称作"腰带水"。它指的是有河流呈圆形、半圆形或弧形从建筑前流过。

在得耕居对面，正好有下坑溪沿蜥蜴山北麓蜿蜒流过，但是这条溪水却在前山头脚下的一座院子前拐了两拐，沿着院墙继续向西，最终汇入坑口外的方岩溪，所以在得耕居前并没有形成"玉带环腰"的形态。从另一个角度来看，风水术还提到"低一寸即为水"，因此路也可以算作"水"。得耕居前的水池为半月形，这是村里唯一一个弧形的水池。

如此一来，沿着水池南岸的路，得耕居就形成了"玉带环腰"的态势。这个半月形的水池叫前塘，据《世远公宗谱》记载，池底有卵石镶嵌成的"双龙戏珠"图案。水池旁边原建有一亭，是程鑅晚年自在逍遥并宴请宾客的地方。因亭在方岩山的东面，他也因此得名为"东亭居士"。水池和建筑之间是用卵石作为铺地，宽敞的场坪。

从水池和场坪到老街之间，原有一个花园，并有一条小径穿过花园与老街相连。据说花园里原本除了奇花异草之外，还摆放着被当地人称作"基戈"的形状各异的赏石。如今，在花园的旧址上，有几间两层高的一进房屋，从建筑样式推测，它们可能是在二十世纪初，由于旅店业的兴起，而先后盖起来的一些店面兼住宅。

在得耕居与老街之间，还有十几间两层高的住宅。在把角的地方，还围了一个小内院。据说，部分建筑有近二百年的历史。如此看来，得耕居虽然并没有临街建造，但是从老街上，以前至少可以看见雕饰华美的重檐楼牌式门楼，还有水池和亭榭。

建筑 得耕居面宽近28米，深约54米，包括前厅后堂两进大院，两侧各有厢房15间。从独松村留下来的几幢同时期的住宅来看，这种平面格局代表了当时住宅的一种典型布局形式。

正因如此，部分在得耕居内没能完整保留下来的建筑元素，可以从其他类似的住宅中找到答案。得耕居的正立面中间部分为砖砌，并雕刻精美的重檐楼牌式门楼，两侧辅以东西厢房素平的山墙。从整体上看，高低错落有致，重点突出。

门楼主体结构是在乾隆己酉年（1789年）重修的，两边厢房的山墙无论从砖的大小、质量和垒法都可以看出其建筑年代远远晚于门楼，是近几十年重修的。门楼的门额正中砖刻"得耕居"，并在两侧辅以麒麟，下方衬以花鸟，门匾上还有雕刻精美的"双狮抢球"。

此外，在两边的墙上还有祥云、花鸟、鲤鱼、骏马、人物等精美砖雕。第一进院是一个近方形的三合院，正房三开间，通常被称作花厅。花厅里原悬挂礼部侍郎程文德的墨宝，即"树德堂"横匾和"栋宇初成家有庆，簪缨世显国同休"的楹联。

村民们认为，正是因为有程文德的保佑，花厅在乾隆年间的大火中才免遭一劫。花厅正面全部为这扇门，需要时可以全部开敞，厅内空间高大通畅。背面通常只有在明间设门，在门前、后金柱位置设屏风，又称太师壁，用以遮挡视线。屏风后有一组楼梯通向花厅后面两层高的戏台。

前院两侧各有三间厢房，再加上花厅两侧各有两间，共五间。厢房和花厅山墙之间有走廊，通往后院。后院是一个长方形的院落：花厅的后面是一个两层高戏台，架空在一个被称为日月井的水池之上；与戏台相对的是三开间的后堂；院落两边各有厢房十间。

后堂分上下两层，一层明间属公有，直接向院落开敞，次间和厢房一样，属于居住空间。在明间内靠后墙的位置，有楼梯通往二层。二层三间连通，属家族公有空间。平日里，二层和一层明间一起用来布置红白喜事，在演戏的时候则是看戏人的天地。

得耕居的厢房全部属于居住空间，随着人口的逐渐增加，大多数人家只能拥有楼上楼下一个开间。楼下居住、楼上储藏，两层之间以楼梯相连，人多的时候楼上也要住人。作为程氏的"老祖屋"，得耕居的房产应该是人人有份，但是历代有钱的程氏后人都选择在附近择地另建新房，所以留在这里的都是相对穷苦的程氏后人。

与得耕居同一时期盖的院落，在平面布局上都有一个特点：围合院落的几排房并没有连在一起，而是被山墙分成一段一段的，山墙之间还有走道通向外面。以得耕居为例，在花厅每侧的厢房之间，各有一条一米多宽的东西向走道，外侧设门，通向院外。在独松村的一些老院子里，走道二层还有走廊相连。

得耕居花厅和后堂的山墙两侧也各有走道，连接厅堂前后的空间。后堂两侧的走道外侧同样设门，通向院外。追其原因可能有三点，一是因为院落太大，用山墙分开力求在失火时把损失减到最小；二是对于这种大型的院落，如果分开分段建造，在技术上也会相对容易；三是对于共同居住在这里的众多家庭，多设几个门，也方便进出。

生活的舞台 作为岩下村现存历史最悠久、面积最大的住宅——得耕居还兼有祭祀的功能，同时也是村里文娱活动的中心。在正月里，凡是家里没有厅堂的族人，就会在花厅内侧的山墙上悬挂自己太公太婆的画像，借这块地方来孝敬他们。宅前的空场地还是每年闹龙灯的重要空间。

龙头、龙尾、再加上一百多"桥"，龙身就是在这里接好后，再出发的。后院的戏台，每年在除夕或正月的时候会演一次"平安戏"。之所以这样叫，是因为人们希望除了热闹之外，还能通过唱戏来祈求四季平安、人丁兴旺。平日里，碰上村里有钱的人家做寿，就会有戏班在这里演三天三夜的戏；或者那些家境富裕的人家，偶尔也会出钱请来戏班，权当做好事。

即使在家家有电视的年代，演戏仍旧是仅次于闹龙灯和赶庙会之外，村里最热闹的事了。每年除夕或正月请戏班的开销，通常是由全村人共同分摊。在演戏期间，花厅即使后台也被戏班子用作临时的住所。开演之前，村民们大都会自己带着凳子来到这里。整个后院、后堂明间和二层都挤满了男男女女、老老少少。住在厢房的人家，还会邀请亲朋好友，坐在走廊里，或是到家里来看戏。

有时候，在后堂的明间里还会有人卖些花生米、熏豆腐、馄饨之类的小吃。此外，得耕居前的空场地和水塘，也是村里人演绎他们日常生活的舞台。

从早到晚都可以看到这样的情景：妇人们在塘边洗衣、唠家常；孩子们在空场上嬉戏、打闹；男人们围坐在墙根处下棋、摆龙门阵…… 程立重宅院——十八世纪中叶的多进院大型住宅 背景 程立重（1736—1793年），字逢时，号毅庵，是岩下程氏第十五世祖。

程立重的祖父备受乡人尊敬，并拥有个人祠堂的作勳公；其父亲是作勳公的小儿子，经臣，即恭常房祖；程立重和他的三个哥哥又分为发、强、刚、毅四房。目前老街上程立重的后人最多，家谱上称这支为"恭下毅常"，他们还有自己的祠堂，即绍常祠堂。

据说，这幢三厅两堂的宅院是程立重的丈人，在送女儿过门之前，派人来村里盖的。这段"岩脚竖花厅，丈人倒贴银"的故事，一直都是村里人茶余饭后的美谈。依照对程立重的年龄推算，这幢住宅的建造年代应该在十八世纪中叶。环境 程立重宅院位于下坑口的以南，蜥蜴山的西麓。在它建成之前，村里的住宅基本上都位于下坑口内，下坑溪和方岩溪交汇的三角河谷地段。

这里地势较高又相对平坦，易于建造，住在这里既可以上山砍柴，又方便照料位于下坑里的田地，建筑内院还可以沐浴在早晨的阳光里。随着人口的不断增加，程氏后人不得不在其他地方建造新宅，程立重宅院就是建在下坑口以外的第一幢住宅。老街在出了下坑口以后，就紧紧地贴着几乎是拔地而起的蜥蜴山。这段蜥蜴山又被称为"前山头"，又因山上的朱公庙而得名为"朱公山"。

程立重宅院大体上坐北朝南，它的东西院墙恰到好处地卡在南北走向的老街和方岩溪之间。院落的西南角还就着方岩溪的走势抹了一个角，做成了弧线形。这座院落在平面布局上采用院套院形式，所以建筑面积比两个得耕居加在一起还要大许多。院墙里面不但有气势恢弘的三进宅院用于居住，还有菜地、花园、"屋里塘"和"屋里井"。

由于老街和方岩溪在这里有将近一层的高差，所以院落内的居住部分位于地势较高的东侧临街，水塘和菜地等位于西侧近水。从方岩溪这边看去，整组建筑坐落在用巨石垒砌的高台之上，更显威严。建筑 程立重宅院的主入口位于院落东墙偏南的位置，面向老街。进门以后是一条被称作"八尺路"的小巷，一直通向位于西墙上的另一个院门。

走在八尺路上，左手边先是开阔的菜地和花园，右侧则是被灰墙黑瓦围合起来用于居住的院落。紧贴这组院落的西墙外，是一个面积比一般住宅还大的长方形水塘，叫"澈月塘"，跨过八尺路和水塘相对的还有一个水井。这种在建筑内部的塘和井又被称作"屋里塘"和"屋里井"。在那个时代，屋里塘和屋里井通常是深宅大院和财富的代名词。水塘西边原本就是院墙，现在只剩下两米多宽、用石块和夯土垒砌的墙基。据说沿墙有廊，廊下的空间既可以做储藏，又可以养家畜。

居住空间坐北朝南，原本是一个拥有三厅两堂的三进院落。这组院落以一座单檐门楼式大门作为入口，眉檐上的砖雕还清晰可见。第一进院落是一个三合院，明堂为方形，尺度与得耕居一进院落明堂相当。

其檐廊下的雀替和二层栏板的样式都属于明末清初当地民居的典型做法，老街上只有同时期建造的绍常祠堂和义丰雅记采用同样做法。

比较这两幢建筑，可以看出那个时期建筑的另一个特点就是在正房和厢房的转角处各做一个小的天井以便通风采光，但是在这幢院落中天井则被两间厢房替代。这种改变可能是因为人口的增加，也可能发生在将住宅改作旅店的年代。

院落的维护结构已几重建，小木装饰也基本没有保留，但是房屋的大木构架相对保存完好，甚至包括一些精美雕饰。

三面走廊全部采用月梁，正房檐廊的梁托上刻卷龙图案，厢房檐廊则用卷草。

雀替采用的雕刻主题也有区别，正房一侧镂雕花鸟走兽，厢房则是卷草。正房明间月梁梁头上的卷草纹更是线条优美，比例匀称。

居住在这里几十年的卢奶奶讲：正房明间原有六扇精美的雕花门扇；次间用于居住，各有一组雕花门窗；明间太师壁两侧有门，可以通向后面的院落。后面两进院子在解放后便逐渐坍塌，只能从留下来的房基和台明铺地上寻找它们的痕迹。

二进院是一个四合院，明堂宽度和一进院相同，进深大约减半。第三进院落又是一个三合院，明堂进深再次减半。宅院内原本是菜地和花园的位置，现在零零散散地盖满了几幢相对简易的住宅。

除了八尺路南侧的住宅始建于十九世纪初以外，其他住宅应该建于"土改"前后，由分到地的穷困人家建造的。义丰雅记建筑群——十八世纪末的单进三合院住宅 环境 在蜈蚣山西侧，老街与方岩溪之间，孤孤单单三幢宅院并肩落在那里。

这三幢院落的西墙紧贴着方岩溪，跨过溪水就是山谷里现存面积最大的一片稻田。远远望去，经过几百年雨水冲刷而略带绛红色的青砖和黑瓦，在青山和绿野的映衬下，显得格外的深邃和凝重。

这三幢宅院中建造最早的是中间的"义丰雅记"，其次是北边的"义丰老店"，南边的最晚，姑且称它为"义丰南院"，它们一起构成了"前厅13间，娘屋9间，后厅13间"的建筑群。可以看出，这三幢建筑在建造过程中，对周围的环境进行了一系列的改造。在平面上，义丰雅记的西墙在逆时针方向上转了几度。可以猜测，这是方岩溪一百多年前的走向。

义丰南院在建造的时候，并没有顺着义丰雅记西墙的方向继续建造，而是建成了一个方方正正、完全对称的院落。由此认为，义丰南院的建造者把方岩溪原有的河道向西推了推，这或许就是方岩溪在义丰南院南墙外，拐了一个近九十度大弯的原因。在剖面上，和程立重宅院相同，这三幢院落都稳稳当当地坐落在巨石和夯土垒砌起来的、近一层高的基础上。

使用这种做法的原因是，街靠山，溪沿谷，老街的高度随地形起伏变化，所以在局部区域就会和山谷有近一层的高差。这种抬高院落地平的做法相对费时费工，所以到十九世纪初，这里的院落都采用了另外一种形式，即"下层系建筑在谷里，中层沿街，上层为楼"。背景 老街上的大部分人都认为，义丰雅记的建造者也是十五世祖程立重。

但是住在义丰雅记里的程人龙老人讲，这座宅院建造于嘉庆二年（1797年），由此推算，建造者应该是程立重（1736—1793年）的后人。岩下老街的程氏在十六世纪初分为福、禄、寿、柏四房。这四房中柏房的人口最少，福、寿两房虽说人丁还算兴旺，但是和禄房相比就逊色很多。

作勳公和他的子孙，还有后来街上开店的大多数人家都是禄房下的。在义丰雅记建好的几十年后，福房下的程乐镛（1832—1866年），即程人龙的太公，却从程立重的后人那里买下了义丰雅记。我们无法确定义丰老店和义丰南院是否是这笔交易的一部分，或者说他们的建造者是谁。从建筑风格上看，义丰老店的建筑形式非常简朴，雕饰也极其简单，这表明建造者当时稍适薄弱的经济状况。

相反，从义丰南店的精美大木构造和小木雕饰中，都可以看出建造者当时的财力。程乐镛是程礼进（1778—1862年）唯一的儿子，据家谱中记载，他们父子俩都曾任"州司马"。从另一方面考虑，卖房卖地一向是封建家族在财力不济时的最后选择。

由此推测，程乐镛当时可能只买下了义丰雅记和义丰老店这两幢建筑，随后为他的三儿子加建了义丰南院。县志中记载程义丰旅店开业于清咸丰年间（1851—1861年），即由程乐镛创建。程乐镛有三个儿子，程昭贡、程昭梭和程昭明。

据老街上九十岁高龄的程崇舜老人讲，这三座院落曾以三兄弟的房号为名，自南向北分别被称作"风、雅、颂"。分家之后，小儿子程昭明住北院继续经营程义丰旅店并改名为义丰老店；老二程昭梭住中间，以房号为店号经营义丰雅记；老大程昭贡住南院，因为家中有四个儿子，住房紧张，所以未曾开店。

建筑 义丰雅记原本是一个正房三间、厢房一边各三间的三合院，沿院落南墙有走廊连接东西厢房。院落的入口原本在南面门楼正中的，当义丰南院建好之后，义丰雅记的入口就挪到了院落东南角，和老街直接相通。

据程人龙老人讲，义丰雅记的正堂内曾挂匾，上书"怡金堂"，据说这是先人做官时别人赠的，所以义丰雅记又被称为怡金堂。比较这三幢建筑，可以看出在短短半个多世纪内老街上建筑的发展与变化。从平面布局上看，义丰雅记正房和厢房的转角处一边各有一个长方形的小天井。

于是在平面上，天井和明堂一起构成了一个"品"字，"品"字形的天井通常含有"官高一品"的寓意。在另外两幢住宅中，天井的位置都各被两间厢房取而代之。

三幢建筑正房的进深基本相似，但是由于义丰雅记的檐廊较宽，正房和后墙之间又有一个走廊，所以明间进深相对较小，室内采用整根月梁纵跨正堂。

义丰雅记和义丰南院的正房明间均明显宽于次间，义丰老店的做法和老街上大部分宅院相同，即三间正房宽度仅有略微不同。义丰雅记和义丰老店正房高度均为八米左右，义丰南院却矮了半米。

老街上随后建成的院落，正房高度均为七米五左右，显然这个新的高度代表了住宅建筑一个新的模数和做法。义丰雅记檐廊下采用雀替，二层栏板的样式与同时期建造的程立重宅院和绍常祠堂相同。

另外两幢建筑廊下既没有雀替，栏板的做法也简单许多。三幢院落明堂的做法也各不相同：义丰雅记台明外侧做排水沟，明堂用条石铺地；义丰老店明堂用鹅卵石铺地，无排水沟；义丰南院有排水沟，却用鹅卵石铺地。

比较老街上的其他建筑，排水沟是一种早期的做法，日后建成的建筑均用鹅卵石满铺明堂，雨水通过明堂四角的落水口排出。

另外一点显著的不同则体现在木构件的雕饰上：义丰老店的檐廊下既没有采用月梁，小木装修也非常朴素，仅采用了简单的格子窗；义丰雅记的大木雕饰或小木装修多采用抽象的几何图案作为装饰母题，雕刻精美却不失典雅与质朴之美；义丰南院的雕饰最为精美却略显做作，不但花窗中饰满花草鸟兽，就连檐廊下的小小梁托两侧也雕尽亭台楼阁。

以上这些建筑细节的演变，固然在一定程度上客观地反映了建造者当时的财力状况。但是如果把这些变化作横向的比较，就可以看出人们对建筑空间和建筑本身的价值观和审美取向的细微转变。

在程立重的年代，建造者的财力体现在宽大高敞的空间，"品"字形的天井，厅堂中两根粗犷却不乏雕饰的月梁，铺满条石的明堂，以及画龙点睛的雕饰。

而对于清朝末年的建造者们，由于人口的增加，新的经济形式的出现以及人们对居住空间功能需求的变化，这使他们更关心建筑的实用性与经济性。相反，由于建筑技术的发展在当时已经相对停滞，所以导致不论是建造者还是工匠都对建筑装饰还是一味追求。

程成昌旅店——二十世纪初的旅店式四合院住宅 背景 程成昌旅店是一家典型的旅店式合院住宅，它位于老街以东，下胡坑坑口以北，院落东墙紧贴着狮子山的一角。

其建造年代虽然不能确定，但是可以通过周围院落的建造时间做出推测。在程成昌旅店的南侧，有两座院落属于程振兴旅店。南端的院落于1880年落成，中间的建于19年后。在程成昌北面、老街西侧，程振兴旅店在1924年又拥有了属于它的第三幢院落。

由此推断，程成昌旅店应该建造于1909年至1924年之间。老街的形成过程，总体上来说是一个以上坑为中心，逐渐向南北线性发展的过程。街上许多相邻的建筑，多数情况下是沿街道向南或向北依次建成的。特别是属于一家人的几幢院落，如果可能的话都会并肩建造，并在空间上相互连通。

程成昌旅店的创始人叫程昭丰（1855—1928年），他的二哥程昭夫拥有老街上最大的旅店，堂号程振昌；六弟程昭贡则创建了位于老街北头、橙麓村境内的程隆昌；四弟程昭路开的程振昌杂货店也在"文革"后转做旅店生意。

可以说在二十世纪初，远道来方岩的香客，至少有三分之一住在这弟兄四人开的旅店中。程昭丰其实是程立重的五世孙，其父为程乐涞，祖程礼书，曾祖程宜槲。程礼书作为程宜槲的长子，继承了建在程立重宅院内花园上的一处小宅院。

程礼书本无后，程乐涞是礼书的三弟礼齐过继给兄长的，这样程乐涞便继承了其父亲的全部财产，包括这幢住宅。乐涞一家的生活应该不算太拮据，所以他的每个儿子在成家后都能从父亲那里得到一点本钱和部分生活必需品，搬出去独立生活并开始经营自己的小买卖，而乐涞也曾利用自家的住宅经营过一个小旅店。

"继子"和"独子"的存在，在中国封建父系社会里是两种很特别并且有意义的现象。兄弟之间相互过继儿子，在短时间内当然不会对这个家族的人口有任何影响。但是从长远考虑，家庭背景及生活环境的多样性，大大增加了整个家族持续繁衍生息的可能性。翻阅老街上程氏的《世远公宗谱》就不难发现，这种过继现象非常普遍。

有时收养继子的人并不是没有儿子，只是不多，这时如果他的兄弟有较多的儿子，也会过继一个给他；有时送子的人家可能也只有两个儿子，但是为了整个家族的利益，也会忍痛把小儿子过继给没有儿子的兄弟；有时兄弟几个，一人无后，其他兄弟便每人过继给他一个；如果亲兄弟之间不能过继，偶尔也会从同宗的堂兄弟之间选择。如果说多生儿子是为了更好地延续香火，那么"独子"就是一种很不利的情况。但是"独子"的最大意义就在于，他能免受分家的痛苦，顺顺当当地继承父亲的全部财产。

老街上旅店业的创始人基本上都是程氏十九世的子孙们，在这个行业里做得不错的有三家兄弟，其中两家兄弟的父亲都是家中的独子，或许这种巧合中也有一定的客观因素。程昭丰去世后，他的独子程宣儯继承了父业。

在宣儯的四个儿子中，长子早亡；二儿子迁居异乡；小儿子程镇仪一直在经营程成昌旅店，并于1991年搬到新街上，改名为"新程振昌"；三儿子程镇康一家，以及店里的一些伙计还依旧住在老房子里。建筑 程成昌旅店的平面布局和建筑形式是十九世纪末、二十世纪初老街上新建住宅的典型。

同以前建造的宅院相比，这些新建院落最大的不同就是建造者已经把"开旅店"作为建筑的主要功能来考虑，而这种变化最直接的反映就是在建筑的平面上。

以前以居住为主要功能的院落，不论是十六世纪初建造的得耕居，十八世纪末的程立重宅院和义丰雅记，还是十九世纪中后期建造的程正伟火腿店及其加建，它们或许是一进三合院，或者是由多个三合院组成的多进院落，也就是说，院落里没有房间朝北。北向日照不好，冬天冷、夏天热，不适宜居住，舍去是情理之中的事。

这样做的另一个好处就是，院落立面可以变得比较自由，起起伏伏，更利于突出中间的门楼式入口。但是如果用来做旅店，则是房间越多越好。

程成昌和程振兴旅店主要院落的占地面积和义丰雅记差不多大小，都约为430平方米，明堂面积也大相径庭，但是义丰雅记一层只有九间房，舍弃两个小天井的义丰老店和义丰南院也不过有十三间，而这些新建的旅店却有十六间。

究其原因，以程成昌旅店为例，其正房和厢房的进深相比义丰雅记都各短一米左右，这样省下来的面积就转化成了内院北侧的一排房。相比同时期橙麓村的住宅，由于没有开旅店这种功能需求，所有院落不论朝向，都没有倒座这排房，当地人还以一层房间数目的多少，将这些院落俗称为"九间头"、"十一间头"等。

程成昌旅店大体上为正南北朝向，有正房三间，左右厢房各五间，倒座三间。正房和两侧厢房之间各有一个一米多宽的夹道，有楼梯通向二楼。

正房中间的一间，在这里被称为西间，它既是旅店和住宅的公共空间，也是这个家庭的礼制中心：一家老少在这里吃饭；客人来了在这里休息、喝茶；逢年过节在这里祭祖；亲人去世在这里吊唁；就连因为各种原因上不了方岩的香客，也会在这里设香案，朝向方岩山祭拜胡公。

西间的后金柱之间是太师壁，即使是平时的时候，太师壁上也会挂着祖宗的像。由于功能的需要，西间通常可以全部开敞，如果设门的话，也是可摘除的板门壁，太师壁后面是储藏间，通过旁边的一个耳门进入。

西间的东西两间分别被称为上大房和下大房，东为"上"，地位略高，通常在分家的时候会优先分给长子，下大房给次子。

东边的厢房自北向南分别叫里厢房、外厢房、小西间、元房和过路间。西厢房临街，作为门面房，也叫店堂。靠南的两间店堂顺街势向外突出了几米，最北的一间在二层还形成了一个小的跨楼，横跨老街。

遗憾的是，这两间房在程昭夫手上被卖了出去，余下的三间自南向北依次称作上店堂、中店堂以及下店堂，其中下店堂这个开间就是院落及旅店的入口。倒座三间自东向西分别是东朝厅、朝厅与西朝厅。朝厅进深较小，所以是院落内，不论一层二层，唯一没有檐廊的一面。

名堂宽为12米，进深约7.5米，即三间正房和两间厢房的宽度。名堂用鹅卵石铺地，地平中间略高，四周稍低，并在四个角落设落水口，和街道两边的排水沟相接。院落中间还有代表财力和地位的"屋里井"。井水清澈甘甜，至今还是程镇康一家人饮用水的主要来源。

经过院落东南角的过路间，就是三开间宽的储物间并卫生间，出了储物间北边的门，便是后院。后院里有一口小水塘，大约有半间屋那么大，以前是清洗马桶用的，现在用来清洗农具。紧贴着储物间的山坎儿上，还有一块小菜地。院落北边临街的位置原本是一个面积很大的伙房，这是因为伙房里不但要做饭，还要酿酒，更要养猪、鸡、鸭、鹅等牲畜。在旺季，程昭丰的家人并带伙计也会住在伙房的二楼，以便把更多的房间腾给香客。

解放后，一部分伙房卖给了外人；十九世纪八十年代，余下的伙房倒塌，于是改用小西间做厨房。变迁 程成昌旅店的营业初期，客房十分简陋，主要接待来自于自东阳、义乌、永康、金华、蒲江、嵊县等地的香客。他们的要求不高，大可以满足于两人挤一张"床"的住宿条件。所谓"床"就是用两个凳子架一块一米多宽木板，上面铺薄薄一层稻草。逢上八九月的香汛，只要能在有屋顶的地方打个地铺，客人也就心满意足了。

在这种情况下，楼上楼下一共能住小三百人。但是随着胡公的名声越来越大，来自于上海和绍兴等大城市的客人也随之增加，他们便对旅店的饮食和设施等方面提出了更高的要求。程成昌在这方面最终没能和像程振兴这样的大旅店竞争，所以只能接待一些"中等"或"下等"的香客。

二十世纪四十年代，在浙江省国民政府进驻方岩的那几年，和其他大部分旅店一样，程成昌几乎停止了营业。虽然没有了香客，但是程成昌变得更热闹了。出出进进的都是在民政厅工作的公务员及其家属，店堂还出租做了一个印刷厂。这些公务员对居住条件的要求较高，为此程成昌还进行了一系列的改造，其中包括在一层地面上加铺木地板以便防潮；把位于外墙上的窗户加大以利于采光；还有出于对安全的考虑，在过路间的位置加建了一个楼梯。

抗日战争结束以后，程成昌恢复营业，旅店生意又红火了几年。从1949年到1980年，旅店开了停，停了又开了若干次。在1958—1960年期间，还先后做过养老院、芝英人民公社制伞厂和岩下大队制伞厂。不过庆幸的是，由于家里人口多，所以房子没有在"土改"时分出去，这也是这幢院子至今还保存良好的重要原因。

1980年8月，旅店再次恢复营业，随着生意的逐渐好转，程振仪于1991年把旅店迁到了位于新街旁边的现代化小楼里，改名为"新程成昌"。程仁昌旅店、程振兴旅店、程铿别墅——二十世纪中叶的中西合璧式建筑 在二十世纪中叶，老街上先后落成了几幢中西合璧式的建筑。

这其中有两幢分别属于程仁昌旅店和程振兴旅店的一部分，另一幢则被称为程铿别墅。属于程仁昌旅店的这幢中西合璧式建筑位于狮子山西麓，老街西侧，程仁昌老店的北墙外。这原本是一幢四层高的木结构单体楼宇，其建筑材料、建造方法以及装饰母题都遵循当地传统建筑的特点，西方建筑的影响主要体现在空间布局上。

据程仁昌旅店的后人程镇隆老人回忆，当初建房时，程昭业本想盖一幢传统的四合院，是在潘工程师的建议下才改成独栋式宅院的。

与老街上的其他合院建筑不同，程仁昌旅店这幢建筑的院落紧邻老街，平面近方形的三开间楼宇坐落在院落西侧，房间与院落之间用前廊过渡。院落地平与山谷同高，位于老街上的入口处相当于二层的高度，进门之后通过两侧台阶到达一层。空间布局的另一点不同表现在剖面上。

老街上的宅院二层原本并不住人，只做储藏，所以二层栏板的做法只是把几块木板竖向排列在框架里。相比西方建筑，高层空间一向都是居住的主要空间，所以栏杆自然成为装饰的重点。旅店业兴起之后，二层空间在香汛期间成为重要的居住空间，二十世纪中叶以后建成的建筑多少都对栏板做了些装饰。

这幢建筑每层的栏杆都有二十九块十分精美的雕花栏板。三十年代中期，程昭业的大儿子程宣祜继承了这幢楼房和后面的一个小院，改店号为新仁昌。程宣祜有三子，解放初由于经济条件不好，被迫卖掉了建筑的三层和四层。程铿别墅位于方岩山入口处，老街南侧。

程铿（1895—1968年），依家谱记载名程宣铿，为程昭纯长子。据程昭纯之孙程振锋（1939年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人回忆，程昭纯（1864生，卒年不详）才是这幢别墅的建造者，在他去世后，别墅为长子和二子共有，三子即其父，分得了老屋。

程铿作为二十世纪初当地一位小有名气的画家，他年轻时就离开家到上海发展，"八一三"事变后回到永康定居。虽然他可能并没有在这里居住过，而且也不拥有整幢建筑，人们还是因为他的名气而称这幢建筑为程铿别墅。程铿别墅也是一幢单体木结构建筑，相比程仁昌旅店，西方建筑对空间布局的影响在这里更为显著。整幢建筑坐落在一个一米多高的石砌台基上，坐西朝东，四周有院落空间环绕。

平面三开间，由六个近方形的空间组成，四面有廊。前、后正间为开敞或半开敞的厅堂，南北四间居住，向南、北走廊开门。这样布局的最大好处之一就是每间卧室都向两个方向开窗，有利于通风，难怪住在这里的程铿后人强调"这房子很凉快"。与程仁昌旅店相同，程铿别墅的二层栏板也有精美的镂空木雕。

在程振兴旅店所拥有的众多建筑中，有两幢连排西洋式楼屋。北边的一幢由程昭夫建于二十世纪三十年代，这是一座二层高六开间的砖石结构建筑。它北面和程昭夫祠堂南墙相接，东墙紧邻老街。

从外面看，几乎看不到当地建筑的影子，取而代之的是西方建筑中常见的砖砌壁柱、圆形小窗及宽大明亮的玻璃窗。南边的一排建于1942年，由程昭夫的儿子程宣楣和程宣吉委托杭州营造厂建造。这座三层高七开间的洋楼其实依旧是一个木构建筑，所有房间坐北朝南并南北通风，各层房间用走廊连接并向南面的内院开敞。

西方建筑元素主要体现在维护砖墙的细部处理上，例如用砖发券的拱形窗和西洋式的门楼。二十世纪四十年代中，程振兴一分为二，程宣楣的儿子程崇学继承了这两幢建筑，改店号为新振兴。解放初，由于经济原因卖掉了第二幢洋楼中的一层，现仅剩两层。

· · · · · ·
英文 English

Residential architecture is the most basic building type and also the prototype of other architectural forms such as ancestral halls and temples.

Research on the construction background of a residence, the residential building itself, plus understanding of its residential history and living conditions, is an important way to grasp the natural conditions, social situation, and cultural traditions of a region.

The information contained in a residence is accumulated through human construction and use.

The "people" here are not one person, nor merely several generations of one family, but several generations from different families and even different clans, completed jointly over time.

In this long process, "natural conditions, social situation, and cultural traditions are changing, and previously formed residences in turn restrict and shape people's construction concepts and usage patterns.

This is an endless process of mutual adaptation." At the same time, the building itself is also being quietly changed.Such changes may leave traces, such as a window added here or a wall removed there.

In more cases, such changes leave no visible trace, such as a bedroom becoming a small shop selling goods, or a hall for ancestral worship being converted into storage.All these changes stage the residents' own stories.

Residences are like people—they also experience birth, aging, illness, and death, and have their own fatness, thinness, beauty, and ugliness.

To understand a nation, one cannot only care about beautiful young people; the same is true for understanding residences.

The residences listed below cover construction periods from the late Ming Dynasty to before liberation, over four hundred years.

Their architectural quality and state of preservation are not necessarily the best on the old street, but they are representative.

The introduction of each residence strives to be based on understanding "the founder's identity and status, family and experience, financial resources and learning," while focusing on the building itself and the development and changes of its usage in the "adaptation process.

" Degeng Residence — Early Residential Architecture Background Degeng Residence is the oldest surviving building and is revered as the ancestral house by the Cheng family of Yanxia Village.

The two characters "Degeng" (Obtaining Cultivation) commemorate Cheng Quan (1333-1385), the founding ancestor of the Cheng family in Yanxia Village, and the residence he built with a hall bearing the plaque "Degeng.

" The original site of this residence was east of the pond in front of Degeng Residence.

Degeng Residence was first built by the seventh-generation ancestor Cheng Heng (1474-1548) in the early sixteenth century, but a fire during the Qianlong period of the Qing Dynasty (1736-1795) destroyed all buildings except the front hall and the "Shude Hall.

" Villagers believe the front hall survived because it displayed the calligraphy of Cheng Wende, Vice Minister of Rites.

In the Jiyou year of Qianlong (1789), Degeng Residence was rebuilt and has been preserved to this day.

Environment Degeng Residence is located within the mouth of Shangkeng, west of the old street, facing essentially south with its back to the north.

As the saying goes, "Mountains prosper population, water prospers wealth.

" When selecting the site and building this new residence, Cheng Heng, hoping for the Cheng family's future population growth and prosperity, not only fully considered the surrounding environment but also artificially adjusted unfavorable factors to create an ideal layout with mountains behind and water in front.

Degeng Residence uses Centipede Mountain as its backing mountain, with the north wall of the residence almost touching the first small hill at its western end.

The ancestral mountain is Lion Mountain, taller and grander, north of Centipede Mountain.The approach mountain is Zhugong Mountain, over a hundred meters to its south.

Zhugong Mountain, also called "Qianshantou" (Front Hilltop), is the first small hill of Lizard Mountain.

Directly facing Degeng Residence, there was originally a large cypress tree on the mountain, and beneath the cypress tree was a local protection temple enshrining "Lord Zhu.

" According to folk legend, Lord Zhu was modeled after Zhu Danxi (1281-1358), a native of Jinhua and a famous physician of the Yuan Dynasty, who after death became one of the main local folk deities.

According to feng shui descriptions, "Jade Belt Encircling the Waist" is a major feature of an "auspicious residence," also called "Belt Water.

" It refers to a river flowing in a circular, semicircular, or arc shape in front of a building.

Opposite Degeng Residence, Xiakeng Stream winds along the northern foot of Lizard Mountain, but this stream makes two turns in front of a courtyard at the foot of Qianshantou, continues west along the courtyard wall, and finally flows into Fangyan Stream outside the ravine mouth, so it does not form a "Jade Belt Encircling the Waist" pattern in front of Degeng Residence.

From another perspective, feng shui also mentions "an inch lower is water," so a road can also count as "water." The pond in front of Degeng Residence is crescent-shaped, the only arc-shaped pond in the village.

Thus, along the road on the south bank of the pond, Degeng Residence forms a "Jade Belt Encircling the Waist" posture.This crescent-shaped pond is called Qiantang (Front Pond).

According to the "Shiyuan Gong Genealogy," the pond bottom has a "Double Dragons Playing with a Pearl" pattern inlaid with cobblestones.

Beside the pond was originally a pavilion, where Cheng Heng spent his later years in leisure and entertained guests.

Because the pavilion was east of Fangyan Mountain, he also took the name "Dongting Jushi" (East Pavilion Scholar).

Between the pond and the building is a spacious courtyard paved with cobblestones.

Between the pond/courtyard and the old street was originally a garden, with a path through the garden connecting to the old street.

It is said that besides exotic flowers and plants, the garden originally displayed various shaped ornamental stones called "Jige" by locals.

Today, on the former garden site, there are several two-story single-courtyard houses, which from architectural style推测 may have been built successively in the early twentieth century due to the rise of the inn industry, as shopfronts combined with residences.

Between Degeng Residence and the old street, there are also over a dozen two-story residences.At the corner, a small inner courtyard is enclosed.

Some buildings are said to have nearly two hundred years of history.

Thus, although Degeng Residence was not built facing the street, from the old street one could formerly see at least its exquisitely carved double-eave gatehouse, as well as the pond and pavilions.

Architecture Degeng Residence is nearly 28 meters wide and about 54 meters deep, including two large courtyards with front hall and rear hall, with 15 wing rooms on each side.

Judging from several contemporary residences preserved in Dusong Village, this planar layout represents a typical layout form of residences at that time.

For this reason, some architectural elements not fully preserved in Degeng Residence can find answers in other similar residences.

The central part of Degeng Residence's front facade is brick-built with an exquisitely carved double-eave gatehouse, flanked by plain gable walls of the east and west wing rooms.

Overall, it is高低错落有致 with prominent focus.

The main structure of the gatehouse was rebuilt in the Jiyou year of Qianlong (1789), while the gable walls of the wing rooms on both sides, judging from brick size, quality, and laying method, are far later than the gatehouse, having been rebuilt in recent decades.

The gate lintel is brick-carved "Degeng Residence" in the center, flanked by qilin, with flowers and birds below, and an exquisitely carved "Double Lions Grabbing a Ball" on the gate plaque.

Additionally, both side walls have exquisite brick carvings of auspicious clouds, flowers and birds, carp, fine horses, and figures.

The first courtyard is a nearly square three-sided courtyard, with the main house of three bays, commonly called the flower hall.

The flower hall originally displayed the calligraphy of Cheng Wende, Vice Minister of Rites—namely the "Shude Hall" horizontal plaque and the couplet "When the mansion is first built, the family has cause for celebration; when official ranks are passed down through generations, the nation shares in the repose.

" Villagers believe it was precisely because of Cheng Wende's protection that the flower hall survived the Qianlong-era fire.

The entire front of the flower hall consists of doors that can be fully opened when needed, with a tall and spacious interior.

The back usually has a door only in the central bay, with a screen (also called Taishi Wall) at the front and rear gold pillar positions to block the view.

Behind the screen is a staircase leading to the two-story stage behind the flower hall.

Each side of the front courtyard has three wing rooms, plus two rooms on each side of the flower hall, totaling five rooms.

There are corridors between the wing rooms and the flower hall gable walls, leading to the rear courtyard.

The rear courtyard is rectangular: behind the flower hall is a two-story stage elevated over a pond called the Sun-Moon Well; opposite the stage is the three-bay rear hall; each side of the courtyard has ten wing rooms.

The rear hall has two floors: the ground floor central bay is communal, open directly to the courtyard, while the side bays, like the wing rooms, are residential space.

In the central bay against the back wall, a staircase leads to the second floor.The three bays on the second floor are connected, belonging to family communal space.

On ordinary days, the second floor and ground floor central bay are used together for weddings and funerals; during performances, they become the audience's space.

All wing rooms of Degeng Residence are residential space; as population gradually increased, most families could only own one bay upstairs and downstairs.

Living downstairs, storing upstairs, connected by stairs, and when there are many people, the upstairs is also used for living.

As the Cheng family's "old ancestral house," Degeng Residence's property should belong to everyone, but wealthy Cheng descendants over generations chose to build new houses nearby, so those remaining here are relatively poor Cheng descendants.

Courtyards built in the same period as Degeng Residence share a feature in planar layout: the rows of houses enclosing the courtyard are not connected together but divided into sections by gable walls, with passages between gable walls leading outside.

Taking Degeng Residence as an example, between the wing rooms on each side of the flower hall, there is an east-west passage over a meter wide, with a door on the outer side leading outside the courtyard.

In some old courtyards in Dusong Village, the second floor of passages also has connecting corridors.

Both sides of the gable walls of Degeng Residence's flower hall and rear hall also have passages connecting spaces before and after the halls.

The outer sides of passages on both sides of the rear hall similarly have doors leading outside.

There may be three reasons: first, because the courtyard is too large, dividing with gable walls aims to minimize losses in case of fire; second, for such large courtyards, if built in separate sections, construction is technically relatively easier; third, for the many families living together, having more doors facilitates entry and exit.

Stage of Life As the oldest and largest surviving residence in Yanxia Village, Degeng Residence also serves a sacrificial function and is the center of village cultural activities.

In the first lunar month, clan members without a hall in their homes hang portraits of their great-grandparents on the inner gable wall of the flower hall, using this space to honor them.

The open ground in front of the residence is also an important space for the annual dragon lantern celebration.

The dragon head, dragon tail, plus over a hundred "bridges" of dragon body are assembled here before setting out.

The stage in the rear courtyard performs a "Peace Opera" once annually on New Year's Eve or in the first lunar month.

It is so called because people hope, besides the festivity, to pray for peace in all seasons and population prosperity through opera.

On ordinary days, when a wealthy family in the village celebrates a birthday, an opera troupe may perform here for three days and three nights; or well-off families occasionally hire an opera troupe as a good deed.

Even in the age of television in every home, opera performances remain the village's liveliest event after dragon lanterns and temple fairs.

The cost of hiring opera troupes on New Year's Eve or in the first lunar month is usually shared by the whole village.During performances, even the backstage of the flower hall is used as temporary accommodation by the opera troupe.

Before performances begin, villagers mostly bring their own stools.The entire rear courtyard, rear hall central bay, and second floor are crowded with men, women, and children of all ages.

Families living in wing rooms also invite relatives and friends to sit in corridors or come inside to watch.Sometimes, in the rear hall central bay, people sell snacks such as peanuts, smoked tofu, and wontons.

Additionally, the open ground and pond in front of Degeng Residence are also stages where villagers演绎 their daily lives.

From morning to night one can see such scenes: women washing clothes and chatting by the pond; children playing and romping on the open ground; men sitting around the wall base playing chess and telling stories...

Cheng Lizhong Courtyard — Multi-Courtyard Large Residence of Mid-Eighteenth Century Background Cheng Lizhong (1736-1793), courtesy name Fengshi, art name Yi'an, was the fifteenth-generation ancestor of the Cheng family in Yanxia.

Cheng Lizhong's grandfather, Zuoxun Gong, was highly respected by villagers and had his own ancestral hall; his father was Zuoxun Gong's youngest son, Jingchen, the ancestor of the Gongchang branch; Cheng Lizhong and his three older brothers were divided into the Fa, Qiang, Gang, and Yi branches.

Currently, Cheng Lizhong has the most descendants on the old street; the genealogy calls this branch "Gongxia Yichang," and they have their own ancestral hall, Shaochang Hall.

It is said this three-hall-two-courtyard residence was built by Cheng Lizhong's father-in-law, who sent people to build it in the village before sending his daughter to marry.

This story of "Building a flower hall at the foot of the rock, the father-in-law subsidizes silver" has long been a favorite topic of villagers after meals.

Based on Cheng Lizhong's age, this residence should have been built in the mid-eighteenth century.Environment Cheng Lizhong Courtyard is located south of the Xiakeng mouth, at the western foot of Lizard Mountain.

Before it was built, village residences were basically located within the Xiakeng mouth, in the triangular river valley where Xiakeng Stream and Fangyan Stream converge.

This area is relatively high and flat, easy to build on; living here allows both going up the mountain to chop firewood and conveniently tending fields in Xiakeng, and the inner courtyard can also bathe in morning sunlight.

As population continuously increased, Cheng descendants had to build new houses elsewhere, and Cheng Lizhong Courtyard was the first residence built outside the Xiakeng mouth.

After exiting the Xiakeng mouth, the old street closely follows Lizard Mountain, which rises almost abruptly from the ground.

This section of Lizard Mountain is also called "Qianshantou" and named "Zhugong Mountain" after the Zhu Gong Temple on the mountain.

Cheng Lizhong Courtyard generally faces south, with its east and west courtyard walls恰到好处地 wedged between the north-south old street and Fangyan Stream.

The southwest corner of the courtyard follows the course of Fangyan Stream with a beveled corner, made into an arc shape.

This courtyard adopts a courtyard-within-courtyard layout, so its building area is much larger than two Degeng Residences combined.

Inside the courtyard walls, there are not only three grand courtyards for residence but also vegetable plots, gardens, an "in-house pond," and an "in-house well.

" Because the old street and Fangyan Stream have a height difference of nearly one story here, the residential part of the courtyard is located on the higher east side facing the street, while the pond, vegetable plots, etc.

are on the lower west side near water.Viewed from the Fangyan Stream side, the entire building complex sits on a high platform built of giant stones, appearing even more majestic.

Architecture The main entrance of Cheng Lizhong Courtyard is located on the south side of the east courtyard wall, facing the old street.

After entering, there is an alley called "Bachi Road" (Eight-Foot Road), leading straight to another courtyard gate on the west wall.

Walking on Bachi Road, on the left are first open vegetable plots and gardens, while on the right is the residential courtyard enclosed by gray walls and black tiles.

Immediately outside the west wall of this residential courtyard is a rectangular pond larger than an ordinary residence, called "Cheyue Pond" (Clear Moon Pond); opposite the pond across Bachi Road is a well.

Such ponds and wells inside buildings are called "in-house ponds" and "in-house wells." In that era, in-house ponds and wells were usually synonyms for grand mansions and wealth.

West of the pond was originally the courtyard wall, now only a wall base over two meters wide, built of stones and rammed earth, remains.

It is said there was a corridor along the wall, and the space under the corridor could be used for both storage and raising livestock.

The residential space faces south and was originally a three-courtyard compound with three halls and two halls.

This compound uses a single-eave gatehouse-style main entrance, with brick carvings on the eyebrow eave still clearly visible.

The first courtyard is a three-sided courtyard with a square central courtyard, similar in scale to Degeng Residence's first courtyard.

The spandrel brackets under its eaves corridor and the second-floor railing panels belong to the typical style of local residences from the late Ming and early Qing periods; on the old street, only Shaochang Hall and Yifeng Yaji, built in the same period, adopt the same style.

Comparing these two buildings, one can see another feature of architecture from that period: small skylights are made at the corners between the main house and wing rooms for ventilation and lighting, but in this courtyard the skylights were replaced by two wing rooms.

This change may be due to population increase, or may have occurred when the residence was converted into an inn.

The courtyard's enclosure structure has been rebuilt several times, and small wooden decorations are basically not preserved, but the large wooden frame of the houses is relatively well preserved, even including some exquisite carvings.

All three-sided corridors use crescent beams; the beam brackets under the main house eaves corridor are carved with coiled dragon patterns, while the wing room eaves corridors use scrolling grass.

The spandrel brackets also differ in carving themes: the main house side has openwork carvings of flowers, birds, and beasts, while the wing rooms use scrolling grass.

The scrolling grass patterns on the crescent beam heads of the main house central bay are especially elegant in line and balanced in proportion.

Grandma Lu, who has lived here for decades, says: the main house central bay originally had six exquisitely carved door leaves; the side bays for residence each had a set of carved doors and windows; the Taishi Wall in the central bay had doors on both sides leading to the rear courtyard.

The latter two courtyards gradually collapsed after liberation, and their traces can only be found in the remaining foundations and platform paving.

The second courtyard is a four-sided courtyard, with the central courtyard width the same as the first courtyard but depth about halved.

The third courtyard is again a three-sided courtyard, with the central courtyard depth halved once more.

The positions originally used for vegetable plots and gardens within the compound are now sparsely covered with several relatively simple residences.

Except for the residence south of Bachi Road, built in the early nineteenth century, the other residences should have been built around the time of "land reform," by poor families who received land.

Yifeng Yaji Building Complex — Single-Courtyard Three-Sided Residences of Late Eighteenth Century Environment West of Centipede Mountain, between the old street and Fangyan Stream, three courtyards stand side by side in isolation.

The west walls of these three courtyards closely follow Fangyan Stream, and across the stream is the largest remaining rice paddy in the valley.

Viewed from afar, the gray bricks and black tiles, slightly crimson after centuries of rain erosion, appear particularly profound and dignified against the green mountains and fields.

Among these three courtyards, the earliest built is the central "Yifeng Yaji," followed by the northern "Yifeng Laodian" (Old Store), and the southern is the latest, tentatively called "Yifeng Nanyuan" (South Courtyard).

Together they form a building complex of "13 front rooms, 9 mother rooms, 13 rear rooms." It can be seen that during construction, these three buildings underwent a series of modifications to the surrounding environment.

In plan, the west wall of Yifeng Yaji rotates several degrees counterclockwise.One can guess this was the course of Fangyan Stream over a hundred years ago.

When Yifeng Nanyuan was built, it did not continue in the direction of Yifeng Yaji's west wall but was built as a square, completely symmetrical courtyard.

It is therefore believed that the builder of Yifeng Nanyuan pushed the original channel of Fangyan Stream westward, which may explain why Fangyan Stream makes a nearly ninety-degree sharp turn outside the south wall of Yifeng Nanyuan.

In section, like Cheng Lizhong Courtyard, these three courtyards all sit steadily on a nearly one-story-high foundation built of giant stones and rammed earth.

The reason for this approach is that the street follows the mountain and the stream follows the valley, so the old street's height varies with terrain, creating a nearly one-story height difference with the valley in local areas.

This method of raising the courtyard ground level is relatively time-consuming and labor-intensive, so by the early nineteenth century, courtyards here adopted another form: "the lower level is built in the valley, the middle level faces the street, and the upper level is a floor.

" Background Most people on the old street believe the builder of Yifeng Yaji was also the fifteenth-generation ancestor Cheng Lizhong.

But Elder Cheng Renlong, who lives in Yifeng Yaji, says this courtyard was built in the second year of Jiaqing (1797), from which it is推算 that the builder should be a descendant of Cheng Lizhong (1736-1793).

The Cheng family of Yanxia Old Street was divided into four branches—Fu, Lu, Shou, and Bai—in the early sixteenth century.

Among these four branches, the Bai branch had the smallest population; although the Fu and Shou branches had reasonably prosperous populations, they were inferior compared to the Lu branch.

Zuoxun Gong and his descendants, as well as most later shopkeepers on the street, belonged to the Lu branch.

Several decades after Yifeng Yaji was built, Cheng Leyong (1832-1866) of the Fu branch—Cheng Renlong's great-grandfather—bought Yifeng Yaji from Cheng Lizhong's descendants.

We cannot determine whether Yifeng Laodian and Yifeng Nanyuan were part of this transaction, or who their builders were.

From architectural style, Yifeng Laodian's architectural form is very simple and its carvings extremely plain, indicating the builder's relatively weak economic situation at the time.

Conversely, the exquisite large wooden structure and small wooden carvings of Yifeng Nandian both show the builder's financial resources.

Cheng Leyong was the only son of Cheng Lijin (1778-1862); according to genealogy records, both father and son served as "Zhou Sima" (Prefectural Sima).

Considering from another angle, selling houses and land has always been the last choice of feudal families when financially strained.

It is therefore推测 that Cheng Leyong may have only bought the two buildings Yifeng Yaji and Yifeng Laodian, and subsequently added Yifeng Nanyuan for his third son.

The county gazetteer records that Cheng Yifeng Inn opened during the Xianfeng period of the Qing Dynasty (1851-1861), founded by Cheng Leyong.

Cheng Leyong had three sons: Cheng Zhaogong, Cheng Zhaosuo, and Cheng Zhaoming.

According to Elder Cheng Chongshun, over ninety years old on the old street, these three courtyards were once named after the three brothers' branch names, called from south to north "Feng, Ya, Song" (Wind, Elegance, Praise).

After the family division, the youngest son Cheng Zhaoming lived in the north courtyard, continued operating Cheng Yifeng Inn, and renamed it Yifeng Laodian; the second son Cheng Zhaosuo lived in the middle, operating Yifeng Yaji under his branch name; the eldest son Cheng Zhaogong lived in the south courtyard and did not open a shop because his family had four sons and housing was tight.

Architecture Yifeng Yaji was originally a three-sided courtyard with a three-bay main house and three wing rooms on each side, with a corridor along the south courtyard wall connecting the east and west wing rooms.

The courtyard entrance was originally in the center of the south gatehouse, but after Yifeng Nanyuan was built, Yifeng Yaji's entrance was moved to the southeast corner of the courtyard, directly connecting to the old street.

According to Elder Cheng Renlong, the main hall of Yifeng Yaji once hung a plaque inscribed "Yijin Tang" (Yijin Hall), said to have been gifted by others when an ancestor served as an official, so Yifeng Yaji is also called Yijin Hall.

Comparing these three buildings, one can see the development and changes of architecture on the old street within just over half a century.

In planar layout, Yifeng Yaji has a rectangular small skylight at each corner between the main house and wing rooms.

Thus in plan, the skylights and central courtyard together form a "Pin" (品) character; "Pin"-shaped skylights usually carry the寓意 of "official rank one grade higher.

" In the other two residences, the skylight positions were each replaced by two wing rooms.

The depths of the main houses of the three buildings are basically similar, but because Yifeng Yaji's eaves corridor is wider and there is another corridor between the main house and back wall, the central bay depth is relatively small, and the interior uses an entire crescent beam spanning the main hall.

The main house central bays of both Yifeng Yaji and Yifeng Nanyuan are明显 wider than the side bays, while Yifeng Laodian's practice is the same as most courtyards on the old street—only slight differences in width among the three main house bays.

The main house heights of both Yifeng Yaji and Yifeng Laodian are about eight meters, while Yifeng Nanyuan is half a meter shorter.

Courtyards subsequently built on the old street all have main house heights of about seven and a half meters; clearly this new height represents a new module and practice for residential architecture.

Yifeng Yaji uses spandrel brackets under its eaves corridor, and its second-floor railing panel style is the same as Cheng Lizhong Courtyard and Shaochang Hall built in the same period.

The other two buildings have neither spandrel brackets under their corridors nor simple railing panel practices.

The central courtyard practices of the three courtyards also differ: Yifeng Yaji has drainage ditches outside the platform and stone slab paving; Yifeng Laodian has cobblestone paving with no drainage ditch; Yifeng Nanyuan has drainage ditches but uses cobblestone paving.

Comparing other buildings on the old street, drainage ditches are an early practice; later buildings all use cobblestones to fully pave the central courtyard, with rainwater discharged through water outlets at the four corners.

Another significant difference is reflected in wooden component carvings: Yifeng Laodian's eaves corridor uses neither crescent beams nor elaborate small wooden decoration, only simple lattice windows; Yifeng Yaji's large and small wooden carvings mostly use abstract geometric patterns as decorative motifs, exquisitely carved yet不失 elegance and rustic beauty; Yifeng Nanyuan's carvings are the most exquisite yet slightly affected, with not only flower windows filled with flowers, plants, birds, and beasts, but even the small beam brackets under the eaves corridor carved with pavilions and towers on both sides.

The evolution of these architectural details, while objectively reflecting to some extent the builders' financial situations at the time, when compared horizontally reveals subtle shifts in people's values and aesthetic orientations toward architectural space and the building itself.

In Cheng Lizhong's era, builders' financial strength was manifested in spacious and lofty spaces, "Pin"-shaped skylights, two粗犷 yet carved crescent beams in the hall, stone-slab-paved courtyards, and finishing-touch carvings.

For late Qing Dynasty builders, due to population increase, emergence of new economic forms, and changes in people's functional needs for residential space, they became more concerned with the practicality and economy of architecture.

Conversely, because architectural technology development had relatively stagnated by then, both builders and craftsmen continued to pursue architectural decoration.

Cheng Chengchang Inn — Inn-Style Four-Sided Courtyard Residence of Early Twentieth Century Background Cheng Chengchang Inn is a typical inn-style courtyard residence, located east of the old street, north of the Xiahukeng mouth, with the east courtyard wall closely touching a corner of Lion Mountain.

Although its construction date cannot be确定, it can be inferred from the construction times of surrounding courtyards.

South of Cheng Chengchang Inn, two courtyards belong to Cheng Zhenxing Inn.The southern courtyard was completed in 1880, and the middle one was built 19 years later.

North of Cheng Chengchang, west of the old street, Cheng Zhenxing Inn acquired its third courtyard in 1924.

It is therefore inferred that Cheng Chengchang Inn should have been built between 1909 and 1924.

The formation process of the old street, generally speaking, was a process centered on Shangkeng, gradually developing linearly north and south.

Many adjacent buildings on the street were in most cases built successively south or north along the street.

Especially for several courtyards belonging to one family, if possible they were built side by side and spatially interconnected.The founder of Cheng Chengchang Inn was Cheng Zhaofeng (1855-1928).

His second brother Cheng Zhaofu owned the largest inn on the old street, with the hall name Cheng Zhenchang; his sixth brother Cheng Zhaogong founded Cheng Longchang, located at the north end of the old street within Chenglu Village; his fourth brother Cheng Zhaolu's Cheng Zhenchang grocery store also converted to inn business after the Cultural Revolution.

It can be said that in the early twentieth century, at least one-third of pilgrims coming from afar to Fangyan stayed in inns opened by these four brothers.

Cheng Zhaofeng was actually the fifth-generation grandson of Cheng Lizhong; his father was Cheng Lelai, grandfather Cheng Lishu, great-grandfather Cheng Yihu.

Cheng Lishu, as Cheng Yihu's eldest son, inherited a small courtyard built on the garden within Cheng Lizhong Courtyard.

Cheng Lishu had no biological heir; Cheng Lelai was adopted from Lishu's third younger brother Liqi, so Cheng Lelai inherited all his father's property, including this residence.

The Lelai family's life should not have been too straitened, so each of his sons, after starting a family, could receive some capital and basic necessities from his father, move out to live independently, and begin operating his own small business; Lelai also once used his own residence to operate a small inn.

The existence of "adopted sons" and "only sons" are two very special and meaningful phenomena in China's feudal patrilineal society.Brothers adopting sons to each other naturally has no impact on the family's population in the short term.

But in the long run, the diversity of family backgrounds and living environments greatly increases the possibility of the entire family's continued reproduction and prosperity.

Browsing the Cheng family's "Shiyuan Gong Genealogy" on the old street, it is not difficult to find this adoption phenomenon very common.

Sometimes a person adopting a son did not lack sons but had few; if his brother had more sons, one would be adopted to him; sometimes a family giving a son may also have only two sons, but for the benefit of the entire family, they would忍痛 adopt the youngest son to a brother without sons; sometimes among several brothers, if one has no heir, the others each adopt one to him; if adoption between biological brothers is not possible, occasionally selection is made from paternal cousins.

If having more sons is for better continuation of the family line, then an "only son" is a very unfavorable situation.

But the greatest significance of an "only son" is that he can avoid the pain of family division and smoothly inherit all his father's property.

The founders of the inn industry on the old street were basically descendants of the nineteenth generation of the Cheng family; among the three brother families who did well in this industry, the fathers of two brother families were only sons—perhaps there are certain objective factors in this coincidence.

After Cheng Zhaofeng died, his only son Cheng Xuantai inherited the family business.

Among Xuantai's four sons, the eldest died young; the second moved to a distant place; the youngest Cheng Zhenyi continued operating Cheng Chengchang Inn and moved to the new street in 1991, renaming it "Xin Chengzhenchang"; the third son Cheng Zhenkang's family, as well as some inn staff, still live in the old house.

Architecture The planar layout and architectural form of Cheng Chengchang Inn are typical of newly built residences on the old street in the late nineteenth and early twentieth centuries.

Compared with previously built courtyards, the biggest difference of these new courtyards is that builders already considered "operating an inn" as the building's primary function, and this change is most directly reflected in the building's plan.

Previously, courtyards with residence as the primary function—whether Degeng Residence built in the early sixteenth century, Cheng Lizhong Courtyard and Yifeng Yaji of the late eighteenth century, or Cheng Zhengwei Ham Shop and its additions built in the mid-to-late nineteenth century—were either single three-sided courtyards or multi-courtyard compounds composed of multiple three-sided courtyards; that is, there were no north-facing rooms in the courtyard.

North-facing rooms have poor sunlight, cold in winter and hot in summer, unsuitable for residence, so omitting them is only natural.

Another benefit of this approach is that the courtyard facade can become relatively free, undulating, more conducive to highlighting the central gatehouse entrance.

But if used as an inn, more rooms are better.

The main courtyards of Cheng Chengchang and Cheng Zhenxing inns occupy about the same area as Yifeng Yaji, both approximately 430 square meters, and their central courtyard areas also differ greatly, but Yifeng Yaji has only nine rooms on the ground floor; Yifeng Laodian and Yifeng Nanyuan, which abandoned two small skylights, have no more than thirteen rooms, while these newly built inns have sixteen.

The reason, taking Cheng Chengchang Inn as an example, is that the depths of its main house and wing rooms are each about one meter shorter than Yifeng Yaji's, and the saved area is converted into a row of rooms on the north side of the inner courtyard.

Compared with contemporary residences in Chenglu Village, due to the lack of inn-operation functional needs, all courtyards regardless of orientation have no this reverse-facing row of rooms; locals also colloquially call these courtyards "Nine-Room Head," "Eleven-Room Head," etc.

, based on the number of ground-floor rooms.Cheng Chengchang Inn generally faces true north-south, with a three-bay main house, five wing rooms on each side, and three reverse-facing rooms.

Between the main house and the wing rooms on both sides there is a passage over a meter wide, with stairs leading to the second floor.

The central bay of the main house, here called the "west room," serves as both the public space of the inn and residence and the ritual center of the family: the whole family eats here; guests rest and drink tea here; ancestors are worshipped here during festivals; the deceased are mourned here; even pilgrims unable to go up Fangyan for various reasons would set up an incense altar here, facing Fangyan Mountain to worship Hugong.

Between the rear gold pillars of the west room is the Taishi Wall; even in ordinary times, ancestral portraits hang on the Taishi Wall.

Due to functional needs, the west room can usually be fully opened; if doors are installed, they are removable panel doors.Behind the Taishi Wall is a storage room, entered through a side door.

The east and west rooms of the west room are respectively called the Upper Great Room and Lower Great Room, with east being "upper," slightly higher in status, usually优先分配 to the eldest son during family division, and the Lower Great Room to the second son.

The east wing rooms from north to south are respectively called Inner Wing Room, Outer Wing Room, Small West Room, Primary Room, and Passage Room.

The west wing rooms face the street, serving as shopfronts, also called shop halls.

The two southern shop halls protrude several meters outward following the street alignment, and the northernmost one forms a small overhanging building on the second floor, spanning the old street.

Unfortunately, these two rooms were sold by Cheng Zhaofu; the remaining three from south to north are依次 called Upper Shop Hall, Middle Shop Hall, and Lower Shop Hall, with the Lower Shop Hall bay being the entrance to the courtyard and inn.

The three reverse-facing rooms from east to west are respectively East Facing Hall, Facing Hall, and West Facing Hall.

The Facing Hall has a smaller depth, so it is the only side in the courtyard, on both first and second floors, without an eaves corridor.

The central courtyard is 12 meters wide and about 7.5 meters deep, i.e., the width of three main house bays and two wing room bays.

The courtyard is paved with cobblestones, with the ground slightly higher in the center and lower around, with water outlets at the four corners connecting to drainage ditches on both sides of the street.

In the middle of the courtyard is also an "in-house well" representing wealth and status.

The well water is clear and sweet, and to this day remains the main source of drinking water for Cheng Zhenkang's family.

Passing through the passage room at the southeast corner of the courtyard, there is a three-bay-wide storage room and bathroom; exiting the north door of the storage room is the rear courtyard.

In the rear courtyard there is a small pond, about half a room in size, formerly used for washing chamber pots, now used for washing farm tools.

On the hillside immediately adjacent to the storage room, there is also a small vegetable plot.

The position north of the courtyard facing the street was originally a large kitchen, because the kitchen not only cooked meals but also brewed wine and raised livestock such as pigs, chickens, ducks, and geese.

During peak seasons, Cheng Zhaofeng's family plus staff would also live on the second floor of the kitchen to free up more rooms for pilgrims.

After liberation, part of the kitchen was sold to outsiders; in the 1980s, the remaining kitchen collapsed, so the Small West Room was converted into a kitchen.

Changes In the early business period of Cheng Chengchang Inn, guest rooms were very simple, mainly receiving pilgrims from Dongyang, Yiwu, Yongkang, Jinhua, Pujiang, Shengxian, and other places.

Their requirements were not high, and they could be satisfied with two people sharing one "bed." The so-called "bed" was a one-meter-plus-wide wooden board supported by two stools, with a thin layer of straw on top.

During the August-September incense season, as long as one could sleep on a floor mat under a roof, guests were content.In such conditions, upstairs and downstairs together could accommodate nearly three hundred people.

But as Hugong's fame grew, guests from big cities such as Shanghai and Shaoxing also increased, raising higher demands on the inn's food, facilities, and other aspects.

Cheng Chengchang ultimately could not compete with large inns like Cheng Zhenxing in this regard, so it could only receive some "medium" or "lower" pilgrims.

In the 1940s, during the years when the Zhejiang Provincial Nationalist Government was stationed in Fangyan, like most other inns, Cheng Chengchang almost ceased operations.

Although there were no pilgrims, Cheng Chengchang became even busier.Those coming and going were civil servants working in the Civil Affairs Department and their families, and the shop hall was also rented out as a printing factory.

These civil servants had higher requirements for living conditions, so Cheng Chengchang underwent a series of renovations, including laying wooden floors on the ground floor for moisture protection; enlarging windows on the exterior walls for lighting; and for safety reasons, adding a staircase at the passage room position.

After the War of Resistance against Japan ended, Cheng Chengchang resumed business, and the inn industry thrived for several more years.From 1949 to 1980, the inn opened and closed several times.

During 1958-1960, it also served successively as a nursing home, Zhiying People's Commune Umbrella Factory, and Yanxia Brigade Umbrella Factory.

Fortunately, due to the large family population, the house was not divided during "land reform," which is also an important reason this courtyard has been well preserved to this day.

In August 1980, the inn resumed business again; as business gradually improved, Cheng Zhenyi moved the inn to a modern small building beside the new street in 1991, renaming it "Xin Chengchengchang.

" Cheng Renchang Inn, Cheng Zhenxing Inn, Cheng Keng Villa — Chinese-Western Combined Architecture of Mid-Twentieth Century In the mid-twentieth century, several Chinese-Western combined buildings were completed successively on the old street.

Among them, two分别 belong to parts of Cheng Renchang Inn and Cheng Zhenxing Inn, and another is called Cheng Keng Villa.

The Chinese-Western combined building belonging to Cheng Renchang Inn is located at the western foot of Lion Mountain, west of the old street, outside the north wall of Cheng Renchang Old Store.

This was originally a four-story wooden structure standalone building, whose building materials, construction methods, and decorative motifs all follow local traditional architectural features; Western architectural influence is mainly reflected in spatial layout.

According to Elder Cheng Zhenlong, a descendant of Cheng Renchang Inn, when the building was first constructed, Cheng Zhaoye originally wanted to build a traditional four-sided courtyard, but on the suggestion of Engineer Pan changed it to a standalone residence.

Unlike other courtyard buildings on the old street, this building's courtyard of Cheng Renchang Inn is adjacent to the old street, with the nearly square three-bay building located on the west side of the courtyard, and rooms transitioning to the courtyard through a front porch.

The courtyard ground level is the same as the valley, and the entrance on the old street is equivalent to second-floor height; after entering, one reaches the ground floor through stairs on both sides.

Another difference in spatial layout is reflected in section.

The second floor of old street residences originally did not house people, only serving as storage, so the second-floor railing panels were simply several wooden boards arranged vertically in the frame.

Compared with Western architecture, high-rise space has always been the main residential space, so railings naturally become the focus of decoration.

After the inn industry arose, second-floor space became important residential space during incense seasons, and buildings constructed after the mid-twentieth century more or less decorated their railing panels.

This building has twenty-nine exquisitely carved railing panels on each floor.

In the mid-1930s, Cheng Zhaoye's eldest son Cheng Xuanhu inherited this building and a small courtyard behind it, renaming the inn Xinrenchang.

Cheng Xuanhu had three sons; in the early liberation period, due to poor economic conditions, he was forced to sell the third and fourth floors of the building.

Cheng Keng Villa is located at the entrance of Fangyan Mountain, south of the old street.

Cheng Keng (1895-1968), according to genealogy records named Cheng Xuankeng, was the eldest son of Cheng Zhaochun.

According to Elder Cheng Zhenfeng (born 1939, Chong generation, 21st generation of Cheng family), grandson of Cheng Zhaochun, Cheng Zhaochun (born 1864, death year unknown) was the actual builder of this villa; after his death, the villa was jointly owned by the eldest and second sons, while the third son—his father—inherited the old house.

Cheng Keng, as a locally somewhat famous painter in the early twentieth century, left home young to develop in Shanghai and returned to settle in Yongkang after the "August 13" Incident.

Although he may never have lived here and did not own the entire building, people still call this building Cheng Keng Villa because of his fame.

Cheng Keng Villa is also a standalone wooden structure; compared with Cheng Renchang Inn, Western architectural influence on spatial layout is more significant here.

The entire building sits on a stone platform over a meter high, facing east with its back to west, surrounded by courtyard space on all sides.

The plan is three bays, composed of six nearly square spaces, with corridors on all sides.

The front and rear central bays are open or semi-open halls, while the four north-south rooms are residential, opening to the north and south corridors.

One of the greatest benefits of this layout is that each bedroom has windows in two directions, conducive to ventilation—no wonder Cheng Keng's descendants living here emphasize "this house is very cool.

" Like Cheng Renchang Inn, Cheng Keng Villa's second-floor railing panels also have exquisite openwork wood carvings.

Among the numerous buildings owned by Cheng Zhenxing Inn, there are two connected Western-style buildings.The northern one was built by Cheng Zhaofu in the 1930s, a two-story six-bay brick-stone structure.

Its north side connects to the south wall of Cheng Zhaofu Ancestral Hall, and its east wall is adjacent to the old street.

From the outside, almost no trace of local architecture can be seen; replaced by common Western architectural features such as brick pilasters, round small windows, and wide bright glass windows.

The southern row was built in 1942, commissioned by Cheng Zhaofu's sons Cheng Xuanmei and Cheng Xuanji from a Hangzhou construction firm.

This three-story seven-bay Western-style building is actually still a wooden structure, with all rooms facing south and ventilated north-south, rooms on each floor connected by corridors and open to the south inner courtyard.

Western architectural elements are mainly reflected in the detailed treatment of the enclosure brick walls, such as brick-arched windows and Western-style gatehouses.

In the mid-1940s, Cheng Zhenxing was divided in two; Cheng Xuanmei's son Cheng Chongxue inherited these two buildings, renaming the inn Xinzhenxing.

In the early liberation period, due to economic reasons, one floor of the second Western-style building was sold, leaving only two floors today.

关键术语 Key Terms (296)
