三、宗祠
中文 Chinese

在农耕社会里，人们无论是盖房、修路，或是开沟渠、整田亩，还是抵抗各种外来的天灾人祸，都不是一个人或是一个家庭可以独立完成的，而是需要许多人的相互扶持和共同努力。在中国几千年的父系社会里，建立在血缘和亲情基础之上的父系家族就很自然地承担起了这个责任。于是，聚族而居和血缘村落就自然而然地成为一种普遍的现象。这种客观现象的存在，以及历代中国社会都尊崇"孝道"的伦理准则，便为宗祠的产生提供了前提条件。

纵观中国历史，北方长年战乱并且人口流失严重，相比之下南方相对安定，更易于血缘村落的形成和发展。所以在江浙地区，特别是像岩下老街这样，地处丘陵地带、交通不发达的村落，一般都保留有较强的宗族意识和相对完整的世族体系。宗祠的基本功能在于，它是一个供奉和祭拜祖先的仪式性场所。

这里的祠堂里都供奉着历代先人的牌位；逢年过节和祭祀时，还要挂上本房祖宗的画像。有的宗祠里还设有谱房以便管理宗族人口的派系、行辈、婚姻等问题。祠堂同时也是商议族内大事、调解族人矛盾、旌表功名、办学堂、存放公共器物的场所。

在大一点儿的祠堂里还有戏台，在特殊的日子里，宗祠便暂时放下它的神圣地位，而成为全村的文化娱乐中心，甚至还允许族人在这里赌博娱乐。以上这些都体现了宗祠存在的意义和重要性，即通过一系列祖先崇拜的仪式及活动来加强程氏家族的内部凝聚力。"加强宗族的内聚力，就是加强人际合作的社会单元的能量，这是人们生存并发展的最重要依靠，从而促成了祖先崇拜，也便是血缘认同。

" 虽然橙麓村和岩下村供奉的是同一始祖程锴，但是老街旁曾先后建造了大大小小至少八座程氏各房派的祠堂。它们在村里都是最重要，通常也是建造最精美的建筑。在江浙地区的多数村落中，祠堂通常会位于村落相对中心的地带，其位置和朝向多少都会影响到村落的布局。

但是老街的发展由于受到地形的限制，所以这里的祠堂大多都沿街坐落，并没能对村落的空间序列起到决定性的影响。即便如此，在用地紧张的情况下，祠堂前通常都有水塘或空地，这个空间依旧是村落生活的舞台。

程氏宗祠 程氏宗祠始建于明正德己亥年（1515年），位于橙麓村象鼻岗山脚。

它是迁居这里的程氏所建的第一个祠堂，也是岩下老街众多祠堂中唯一被收入县志的，"三十五都程宗祠：始祖闰二闰三"。祠堂坐西朝东，东侧是螺蛳塘山，山上建有三世祖程权之墓。

在嘉靖年间，祠堂曾改建为前后两进；在道光丙午年重修成五开间，前后三进院落，并且用石柱代替已经糟朽的木柱。据当地人回忆：整个祠堂宽二十多米，进深近五十米；有牌门一座，享堂五间，寝室九间，还有厢房位于南北两侧。

在抗日战争时期，中共浙江省委书记刘英被关在程氏宗祠内，并于1942年5月13日在祠堂内遇害。1992年1月21日，程氏宗祠随着刘英烈士陵园的开工而被拆毁。

在当地程氏后人的努力下，部分石柱、柱础和门楣等建筑构件被送到文楼村保存，以便日后重建。居住在橙麓村的程家麟老人还花钱买下了一些柱础，这些柱础现在还依然摆放在他家的院墙外。

还有一些柱础上面架上了石条，成了村里人小憩和唠家常的地方。在橙麓村的水塘边和小路旁，还能看到许多似乎曾被用作为台明石的大块石料；水塘里的一块洗衣石上还有两个刚劲的楷体大字"之墓"。老人们已经不记得这块石头的来历，更无法寻找另一半墓碑的下落。这些点点滴滴的痕迹，都记录着那幢曾经矗立在村口长达五百年之久的建筑的辉煌。

世远公祠及作勳公祠 背景 世远公祠，俗称小祠堂，是为纪念程坦（1411—1477年）而建造的。程坦字世远，为永康程氏六世祖。

作勳公祠指的是十三世祖程际烈（1646—1725年）。作勳公祠又叫友恭常祠，友常和恭常分别代表作勳公的两个儿子程伊臣（1691—1765年）和程经臣（1697—1771年）的房派。

村里的老人们已经不记得这两座祠堂具体的建造年代了，但是他们大多都很肯定一点，那就是虽然世远公比作勳公早出生二百多年，但是作勳公祠建在世远公祠之前，并且它们都比不远处的绍常祠堂要早。

从两个祠堂的相互关系上看，世远公祠位于作勳公祠前，并挡住了其正门，也从侧面支持了世远公祠建造较晚的说法。

现住在作勳公祠里的程茂发（1934年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，作勳公祠是程伊臣和程经臣为其父亲共同建造的，所以又叫友恭常祠；而世远公祠是作勳公的孙子建造的。

这种说法听起来也不无道理，而且在时间上也是成立的，即作勳公祠建于十八世纪中叶，世远公祠可能在随后的一二十年后。

而曾参加过抗美援朝的程龙兴（1930年生，圣字辈，程氏22代）则振振有词地说，世远公祠是在明嘉靖年间（1522—1566年），由世远公的孙子建造的。

家谱中记载，世远公的长孙生活的年代为1494—1568年，所以这在时间上也是可能的。这两座祠堂的先后建成，标志着岩下村已经从一个杂姓聚居的村落，转变成了一个程氏的血缘村落。

之所以人们为作勳公建造祠堂，不仅是因为程际烈家境富足，更是因为他"资雄里闬，每遇岁歉必出粟赈饥，凡有求者缓急靡不相济。人皆德之。"不仅如此，程际烈的两个儿子也广受乡亲的尊敬。家谱记载其长子伊臣"文艺精通"，并且独修宗谱；次子经臣"善承家业"。

为世远公建造祠堂则意义更为重大，岩下村的程氏一直奉世远公为始祖，村里新修好的家谱就叫《世远公宗谱》。究其原因为，虽然三世祖程权是岩下村的始迁祖，但是他的子孙除程坦外，即世远公，均外迁他乡。自六世祖程坦以后，这支程氏才世代留居在了岩下村。

环境 这两座祠堂位于上坑口外，老街以西。方岩溪在它们的西边，紧贴着世远公祠的后墙绕了一个半圆后，继续向北。这两幢建筑的轴线几乎相互垂直，世远公祠朝东南，作勳公祠朝西南。外墙之间只有一条一米多宽的小道，叫世远巷。

紧贴着世远公祠的东墙和部分南墙，有一个长方形的水池，叫源流塘。水塘本身与世远公祠或作勳公祠之间不存在诸如轴线、对称或呼应等空间关系，却像是世远公祠"吃"掉了水塘的一个角。所以世远公祠也不能像其他祠堂一样，把入口冠冕堂皇地设在正中，而是设在了东北角上。

即便如此，还是有一部分入口，落在了水塘边界以内。世远公祠的另外一个入口，也是紧贴着水塘，位于侧立面的东南角上。次入口门外不远处，方岩溪缓缓流过，祠堂入口几乎正对着溪上的一座小桥。这是一座两侧有斜撑的平板石桥，叫望屏桥。过桥不远，便是绍常祠堂。

这组祠堂建筑群，没有一般宗祠建筑所具有的那种威严和霸气，反倒是十分生动而亲切。其灵活与不对称的空间构成，不禁让人想起著名的圣马可广场。祠堂建筑和老街之间有一个院落的距离，这样极好地回避了香客们的喧嚣。走在老街上，通过两座院落之间的"世远巷"，也可以隐约看到祠堂和水塘的一角。

这两个祠堂，以及源流塘所围合的空间，虽然不大，却是村里重要的公共空间之一。经常可以看到附近的村民来这里浣洗衣物；到了吃饭的时间就会有人坐在水塘边的石板上吃饭；也会有人在水塘和作勳公祠之间不大的空地上晒晒干柴。

世远公祠 按村里的说法，世远公祠是全村人都有份的祠堂。它的平面布局及构造，代表这个地区的一种普遍形式。祠堂长约26米，宽约17米，在横向上有五个开间。

中间三个开间纵向全部开敞，直接暴露抬梁式屋架，从前到后分别是前厅，天井，正堂，戏台及左右两个小天井，最后是享堂。侧面是穿斗结构的厢房，并有夹层。除了前厅里面的十根木柱外，其余的柱子均为石柱。

三种不同尺寸的柱子与五种规格各异的柱础之间的不同搭配，间接界定了空间的主次。最重要的空间当然是正堂，正堂位于三个天井之间，有八根圆柱并辅以有覆盆的巨型柱础。

正堂的堂号依旧取三世祖程权"终身得耕陇亩足矣"中的"得耕"二字，为"得耕堂"。

厢房和享堂内的柱子均为方柱，但是只有正堂两侧的柱子和享堂中间的四根，才配以一种规格较高的方形柱础；余下的只是采用很简单的，大小和柱子截面相当的四面倒锥形柱础。

前厅中间面向正堂的两根檐柱采用的是石圆柱，辅以鼓形柱础。

前厅内余下的柱子皆为较细的木柱，柱础也采用规格更小的鼓形柱础。

正堂两个前金柱内侧分别写有"六世衍宗祧有典有则，千秋隆孝享是训是行"，后金柱上的对联则在前面，为"后嗣相承祖德与方山并寿，前徽弗替孙谋偕丽水俱长"。"得耕堂"的匾也挂在后金柱之间，略向下倾斜。很显然，这两副对联和横匾共同"指向"的地方，就是程氏子孙在祠堂里双膝碰地的地方。

享堂宽五开间，两侧没有厢房，它的地面比正堂高四十公分。享堂后面原本是按照左昭右穆摆放牌位的地方，虽然这些木构的台面、神龛现已全部缺失，但是从柱子侧面的榫口可以推断出它们的位置和高度。

以前老街上如果有程氏男丁过世，家人就要做一个牌位送到世远公祠里来；如果是友恭常下的后人，还要做一个送到作勳公祠；如果又是恭下毅常的，则要做第三个送到绍常祠堂；对于已经结婚的男丁，则要做夫妻牌位。

如果当时妻子还在世，则用一块红布把妻子的牌位暂时罩起来；如果妻子先走一步，则要等到丈夫去世后一起进祠堂。正堂两侧的厢房主要用于储藏一些祭祀用品。二层的夹层则是堆放一些大型的器物，例如每年舞龙灯时用的龙头和龙尾。

祠堂南墙外还有一个不规则的小屋，平时是教书先生办公及居住的地方，在过年和清明的时候用做灶堂。作勳公祠 作勳公祠的建筑规格则小许多，简单地说就是一个三合院。

正厅为三开间，抬梁式结构，尺度和世远公祠正堂的尺寸十分接近。东西厢房各三间，为穿斗式结构，靠南的两间有夹层。厢房的首间和正厅围合形成了一个进深约三米的内院。自从1958年"生产大跃进"后，作勳公的房产由四家人共有。

据住在西厢房的程茂发（1934年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，他的继父程宣占由于很穷，在1946年便分到了作勳公祠的一部分房产，他和继父，以及生父一家人就住了进来。在这以前由于家里穷没有房，也会到祠堂里租房子住。

之后，又有三家人买房。由于居住的需要，作勳公祠如今被划分出了许多小的隔间。但是据程茂发老人讲，以前祠堂里的正房都是开敞的，现在各家用来当作门窗的隔板就是原来正堂里排位前面的门板拆下来的；祠堂里以前有一块写着"贡元"的匾，不幸在"文革"期间被损坏。

绍常祠堂 据说绍常祠堂是程立重（1736—1793年）为报答丈人在"岩脚竖花厅"的赠银之恩，于乾隆五十三年（1788年）建造的。程立重字逢时，号毅庵，为作勳公之孙，程经臣之子。

程经臣的四个儿子各以"发"、"强"、"刚"、"毅"为房派，所以程立重的后代又被称为"恭下毅常"。由于程立重排行最小，所以"毅常"又被俗称为"小常"。永康话"小"与"绍"同音，所以得名为绍常祠堂。

环境 绍常祠堂坐北朝南，位于方岩溪以西，背后斜对着方岩山。在建造绍常祠堂以前，这可能只是一片田地。

在绍常祠堂建成以后的很长一段时间里，都是孤零零地独自坐落在方岩溪的这一侧。祠堂前面有一块较大的空场，这里既是秋收后村里人晒谷子的草场，又是正月里迎龙灯的高潮所在。空场的南面还有一幢几十年前建的电影院，如今家家都有了电视，电影院便冷清了。

空场的东缘由方岩溪界定，溪水东岸就是程立重丈人出资修建的，有屋里塘、屋里井，有花园、菜地的大宅院。在空场的南、北两端各有一个小桥横跨方岩溪通往宅院。从绍常祠堂向北不远，还可以通过望屏桥走到世远公祠和作勳公祠。

建筑 绍常祠堂的平面布局和世远公祠基本相似，正堂和享堂的尺寸也相当，但是总面积却是世远公祠的两倍。

与世远公祠相比，绍常祠堂在平面上的主要区别体现在如下几点：前厅和厢房进深较深，厢房和轴线上的主要空间之间局部有走廊，三个主要天井面积都比较大，在正堂两侧的厢房之间还各有一个有楼梯的小天井。

祠堂的正立面左右两端为高低错落的马头墙，主入口在中间，两边各有一个次入口连接厢房和前厅的走廊。除此之外，在这青砖墙上还高低错落地安插了几个方形和圆形的小窗，主要用来给尽端厢房和前厅次间通风。

前厅明间通高，次间二层的空间与两侧厢房相连。明间用月梁，前后单步梁，中间双步梁上又立童柱，上层采用穿斗结构。

穿过前厅是和前厅同宽的明堂，明堂四面有廊，有十二根檐柱环绕。每一根檐柱上方均有一个雕着卷龙、鸟兽或花木的牛腿，牛腿上面还有一组精美的叠斗，用以支撑上面的挑檐枋。

叠斗外侧均有镂雕插叶，内侧为盘龙形蠖梁。正堂三间梁架全部露明，为九檩进深。

明间有四柱，采用前后双步梁加中间五架梁；次间用五柱，穿斗与抬梁相结合，柱间有四组双步梁。

正堂整个屋顶的重量依次通过檩枋、叠斗、大斗、大斗下的花墩、月梁、梁托，最后传到柱子上。

在支撑檩枋的叠斗和柱子之间，还用蠖梁做连接，蠖梁不但有极强的装饰效果，还能起到在纵向上稳定结构的作用。

除了蠖梁以外，叠斗、雀替、花墩、梁头和梁托都是绍常祠堂大木结构雕饰的重点。

此外，在两根上金檩、前下金檩、檐檩和挑檐枋底面还有精美的镂空浮雕花鸟鱼纹。正堂后面就是一个平面为方形的戏台，戏台两侧各有一个近方形的天井。

从这里开始，厢房的进深变浅，从而使轴线上的主要空间可以拓宽成五开间。享堂五开间，位于一个两步高的台基上。享堂的梁架结构和正堂大体类似。

不同的是前后的双步梁变成了单步梁；后面的单步梁外面又增加了穿枋结构的一个廊，作为摆放神橱的空间；明间前面由于需要和戏台衔接，所以对梁架做了一些变化。享堂中间三开间在金柱间设门，但是可以全部开敞。

据老人们讲，门上曾雕有精美的二十四孝，用以教育逢年过节来这里祭拜的程氏子孙。享堂的大木雕饰比正堂的稍简洁，在檩下叠斗之间没有用蠖梁，只是在叠斗两侧饰卷草形插叶。东西厢房自南向北分别被分隔成大小不同的五个空间。

中间三间各自朝向不同的天井开窗采光，尽端的两间则利用外墙开窗通风。厢房与前厅、一进天井、正堂之间均有走廊，在正堂侧壁后面的厢房之间还各有一个小天井，天井一侧各有一个楼梯通往二层。

周氏宗祠 周氏宗祠位于狮子山西麓的一个小山坳里，坐东朝西，紧邻老街。

祠堂的位置是这段老街地势最高的部分，它和方岩山之间没有任何建筑物遮挡，有与方岩对视、俯瞰山谷的感觉。周氏宗祠是老街上唯一一座非程姓的祠堂。

据《游川周氏宗谱》记载，这支周氏被称为"草墅周氏"。其始祖时文公（1254—1315年）在元至元十七年至二十八年（1280—1291年）期间任两浙提举，因"奉敕兼督理台温分司盐课，秩满道经永康，爱其风俗之美"，所以从金华泮塘迁居永康升平乡草墅，由此得名为"草墅周氏"。

自此，时文公的子孙便从草墅迁往永康各地。周氏宗祠建在这里是因为始祖周时文公墓位于它后面不远处，一个叫公道山"狮子撰屙"的洞穴里。据说周姓人家在老街上只居住了很短的时间，并在程氏迁到这里之前就离开了；也有人说周氏从未在这里居住过，只是在清明的前十天会有上百人来这里祭奠周氏始祖。

《游川周氏宗谱》中记载这里原来只有"平厅三楹，厅前又有庵屋数间"，用以在清明时给远道来祭祖的子孙提供休息场所。后来由游川、岘川、璋川三宗扩建成现在的规模，现存建筑为光绪三十四年（1908年）重修。

在国民党浙江省政府迁驻方岩的时候，这里曾作为浙江省警察大队的办公场所，其中还有一个小型的监狱。周氏宗祠是一个接近方形的四合院。

临街一面两侧是马头墙，白色的墙头上曾绘有彩画，但是如今只能依稀看见斑驳的色彩。正门居中，两旁各有一个偏门通向院内走廊。一进为三开间、七檩进深的拜厅，这是整幢建筑中雕饰最为精美的地方；天井后面，高台之上为五开间、进深略浅的享堂；两侧各有五间厢房供前来祭祀者暂住。

不仅如此，"祠外两旁小厅，并楼屋八间，为看守者安身之宅也"。这"小厅"指的是周氏宗祠两边，跨过老街架设的路亭，这两个路亭是老街上为处不多的既能避风雨，又能小憩的地方。"楼屋八间"现在只有三间归周氏所有，守墓人周岩好夫妇及其大嫂现居住在这里。

家谱中记载，祠墓应由游川、岘川、璋川三宗轮流派可靠能干的子孙驻守，而"岩好公为游川小四房子孙，自其曾祖起住守始祖公祠墓至今已逾百年"。其他祠堂 闰二宗祠位于橙麓村东侧，始建于明代。从建筑布局上看，应该和世远公祠类似，即三进正堂、品字形天井布局。

基于"土改"时期的政策，闰二宗祠属于五代以上的祠堂，被归为国有。于是它先是作为小学校，又在1988年改作橙麓村老年协会的活动场所。2006年，祠堂内部被修葺一新，地面和部分墙面用混凝土重做，中部的大跨度屋顶也改用钢桁架结构。

程昭夫祠堂位于狮子山西麓，老街西侧。由程昭夫建于1924年，为程振兴旅店的一部分。祠堂形制和世远公祠基本相同，平面尺寸略大。

祠堂在香汛时期也做客房使用，中间开敞空间不仅作为旅店的厅堂来招待香客、设摆晚宴，晚上还会请艺人在这里唱戏。浙江省国民政府临时迁驻方岩那段时间，这里还是承办政府会议、各式筵席甚至举办婚礼的场所。启文公祠位于回龙桥西侧，其建造者为程振兴旅店老板的弟弟程昭纯。

程昭纯（1864生，卒年不详），字启文，为程乐涞五子，并于1900年在老街南端开办了程成兴雨伞。据程昭纯孙程振锋（1939年生，崇字辈，程氏21代）老人讲，祠堂布局原本和世远公祠类似。现在可惜只剩下了中间的三开间。

老街上还有一个几乎已经被人们忘记的祠堂，这就是位于下胡坑坑口靠西的禄房祠堂。据程振锋老人回忆，禄房祠堂是其祖父程昭纯主持修建的，并作为祠堂管事。祠堂的规模比世远公祠略小，只有前后两进院落。这样算来，祠堂大约建于二十世纪初。

不幸的是在"文革"期间，由于疏于维护，这座祠堂最终毁于白蚁和火灾。目前仅存一些残垣断壁，连基本的平面布局也无法辨认。

· · · · · ·
英文 English

In an agrarian society, whether building houses, repairing roads, digging irrigation channels, tending fields, or resisting various natural and man-made disasters, none of these could be accomplished independently by one person or one family; they required mutual support and joint efforts of many people.

In China's thousands of years of patrilineal society, patrilineal clans built on blood ties and kinship naturally assumed this responsibility.Thus, living together as a clan and forming blood-lineage villages naturally became a widespread phenomenon.

The existence of this objective phenomenon, along with the ethical principle of "filial piety" revered throughout Chinese history, provided the preconditions for the emergence of ancestral halls.

Looking at Chinese history, the north experienced long-term warfare and severe population loss, while the south was relatively stable, more conducive to the formation and development of blood-lineage villages.

Therefore, in the Jiangsu-Zhejiang region, especially in villages like Yanxia Old Street located in hilly areas with underdeveloped transportation, strong clan consciousness and relatively complete lineage systems are generally preserved.

The basic function of an ancestral hall is as a ritual place for enshrining and worshipping ancestors.

The halls here all enshrine the ancestral tablets of successive generations; during festivals and sacrifices, portraits of the branch ancestors are also hung.

Some ancestral halls also have genealogy rooms to manage clan population branches, generational rankings, marriages, and other matters.

The hall is simultaneously a place for discussing major clan affairs, mediating clan disputes, commending academic achievements, running schools, and storing public utensils.

Larger halls also have stages; on special days, the ancestral hall temporarily sets aside its sacred status and becomes the village's cultural and entertainment center, even allowing clan members to gamble for entertainment.

All these reflect the significance and importance of ancestral halls—namely, strengthening the internal cohesion of the Cheng clan through a series of ancestor-worship rituals and activities.

"Strengthening clan cohesion means strengthening the energy of social units for interpersonal cooperation, which is the most important reliance for people's survival and development, thereby促成 ancestor worship, which is also blood-lineage identity.

" Although Chenglu Village and Yanxia Village worship the same founding ancestor Cheng Kai, at least eight ancestral halls of various Cheng branch lineages, large and small, were successively built beside the old street.

They are the most important and usually the most exquisitely built buildings in the village.

In most villages in the Jiangsu-Zhejiang region, ancestral halls are usually located in relatively central areas of the village, and their position and orientation more or less influence the village layout.

However, due to topographical constraints on the old street's development, most halls here are situated along the street and did not play a decisive role in the village's spatial sequence.

Even so, under conditions of tight land use, there is usually a pond or open space in front of the hall, and this space remains a stage for village life.

Cheng Clan Ancestral Hall The Cheng Clan Ancestral Hall was first built in the Jihai year of Ming Zhengde (1515), located at the foot of Xiangbi Hill in Chenglu Village.

It was the first hall built by the Cheng family who moved here, and the only one among the many halls of Yanxia Old Street included in the county gazetteer: "Cheng Ancestral Hall of Thirty-Fifth Capital: Founding ancestors Run-er and Run-san.

" The hall faces east with its back to west; to its east is Luositang Mountain, where the tomb of the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan is located.

During the Jiajing period, the hall was rebuilt into front and rear two-courtyard form; in the Bingwu year of Daoguang it was rebuilt into five bays with front and rear three courtyards, using stone pillars to replace already decayed wooden pillars.

According to local memories: the entire hall was over twenty meters wide and nearly fifty meters deep; with one memorial gate, five bays of offering hall, nine bays of dormitory, and wing rooms on the north and south sides.

During the War of Resistance against Japan, Liu Ying, Secretary of the Zhejiang Provincial Committee of the CPC, was imprisoned in the Cheng Clan Ancestral Hall and killed there on May 13, 1942.

On January 21, 1992, the Cheng Clan Ancestral Hall was demolished with the start of construction of the Liu Ying Martyrs Cemetery.

Through the efforts of local Cheng descendants, some architectural components such as stone pillars, pillar bases, and lintels were sent to Wenlou Village for preservation for future reconstruction.

Elder Cheng Jialin, who lives in Chenglu Village, also spent money to buy some pillar bases, which are still placed outside his courtyard wall.

Some pillar bases have stone slabs placed on top, becoming places for villagers to rest and chat.

By the pond and along paths in Chenglu Village, many large stone blocks that seem to have been used as platform stones can still be seen; on a laundry stone in the pond there are two vigorous regular-script characters "Zhi Mu" (Tomb).

Elders no longer remember the origin of this stone, nor can they find the whereabouts of the other half of the tombstone.These点点滴滴 traces all record the glory of that building that once stood at the village entrance for five hundred years.

Shiyuan Gong Hall and Zuoxun Gong Hall Background Shiyuan Gong Hall, commonly called the Small Hall, was built to commemorate Cheng Tan (1411-1477).

Cheng Tan, courtesy name Shiyuan, was the sixth-generation ancestor of the Yongkang Cheng family.Zuoxun Gong Hall refers to the thirteenth-generation ancestor Cheng Jilie (1646-1725).

Zuoxun Gong Hall is also called Yougongchang Hall; Youchang and Gongchang respectively represent the branch lineages of Zuoxun Gong's two sons, Cheng Yichen (1691-1765) and Cheng Jingchen (1697-1771).

Village elders no longer remember the exact construction dates of these two halls, but most are quite certain of one point: although Shiyuan Gong was born over two hundred years earlier than Zuoxun Gong, Zuoxun Gong Hall was built before Shiyuan Gong Hall, and both are earlier than the nearby Shaochang Hall.

From the relationship between the two halls, Shiyuan Gong Hall is located in front of Zuoxun Gong Hall and blocks its main entrance, which also indirectly supports the claim that Shiyuan Gong Hall was built later.

Elder Cheng Maofa (born 1934, Chong generation, 21st generation of Cheng family), who now lives in Zuoxun Gong Hall, says that Zuoxun Gong Hall was jointly built by Cheng Yichen and Cheng Jingchen for their father, hence also called Yougongchang Hall; while Shiyuan Gong Hall was built by Zuoxun Gong's grandson.

This说法 sounds reasonable and is also chronologically plausible—i.e., Zuoxun Gong Hall was built in the mid-eighteenth century, and Shiyuan Gong Hall possibly one or two decades later.

Cheng Longxing (born 1930, Sheng generation, 22nd generation), who participated in the War to Resist US Aggression and Aid Korea, asserts with conviction that Shiyuan Gong Hall was built during the Ming Jiajing period (1522-1566) by Shiyuan Gong's grandson.

Genealogy records show that Shiyuan Gong's eldest grandson lived from 1494 to 1568, so this is also chronologically possible.

The successive construction of these two halls marks Yanxia Village's transformation from a multi-surname settlement into a Cheng blood-lineage village.

The reason people built a hall for Zuoxun Gong was not only because Cheng Jilie's family was wealthy, but more because he "was prominent in the neighborhood, and whenever there was a famine year he would certainly distribute grain to relieve hunger, and whoever sought help, whether urgent or not, he would never fail to assist.

All people praised his virtue." Moreover, Cheng Jilie's two sons were also widely respected by villagers.

Genealogy records that his eldest son Yichen was "skilled in literature and arts" and alone revised the clan genealogy; his second son Jingchen "ably inherited the family business.

" Building a hall for Shiyuan Gong was even more significant—the Cheng family of Yanxia Village has always revered Shiyuan Gong as their founding ancestor, and the newly revised village genealogy is called the "Shiyuan Gong Genealogy.

" The reason is that although the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan was the migration founder of Yanxia Village, all his descendants except Cheng Tan—i.e., Shiyuan Gong—moved elsewhere.

Only from the sixth-generation ancestor Cheng Tan onward did this Cheng branch remain in Yanxia Village for generations.Environment These two halls are located outside the Shangkeng mouth, west of the old street.

Fangyan Stream is to their west, closely following the rear wall of Shiyuan Gong Hall in a semicircle before continuing north.

The axes of these two buildings are nearly perpendicular to each other: Shiyuan Gong Hall faces southeast, and Zuoxun Gong Hall faces southwest.

Between their exterior walls is only a path over a meter wide, called Shiyuan Lane.Closely following the east wall and part of the south wall of Shiyuan Gong Hall is a rectangular pond called Yuanliu Pond.

The pond itself has no spatial relationship such as axis, symmetry, or echo with either Shiyuan Gong Hall or Zuoxun Gong Hall; rather, it seems as if Shiyuan Gong Hall "ate" a corner of the pond.

Therefore, Shiyuan Gong Hall could not, like other halls, place its entrance grandly in the center, but instead set it at the northeast corner.Even so, part of the entrance still falls within the pond boundary.

Another entrance of Shiyuan Gong Hall is also closely adjacent to the pond, located at the southeast corner of the side facade.

Not far outside this secondary entrance, Fangyan Stream flows slowly, and the hall entrance almost directly faces a small bridge over the stream.

This is a flat stone slab bridge with diagonal supports on both sides, called Wangping Bridge.Not far across the bridge is Shaochang Hall.

This group of hall buildings lacks the majesty and domineering quality typical of ancestral hall architecture, instead being quite vivid and亲切.

Its flexible and asymmetrical spatial composition inevitably reminds one of the famous Piazza San Marco.

There is a courtyard's distance between the hall buildings and the old street, which excellently avoids the noise of pilgrims.

Walking on the old street, through the "Shiyuan Lane" between the two courtyards, one can also faintly glimpse a corner of the halls and pond.

The space enclosed by these two halls and Yuanliu Pond, although not large, is one of the village's important public spaces.

One can often see nearby villagers coming here to wash clothes; at mealtime, people sit on stone slabs by the pond to eat; and some people dry firewood in the small open space between the pond and Zuoxun Gong Hall.

Shiyuan Gong Hall According to village说法, Shiyuan Gong Hall is a hall shared by all villagers.Its planar layout and structure represent a common form in this region.

The hall is about 26 meters long and 17 meters wide, with five bays horizontally.

The middle three bays are fully open longitudinally, directly exposing the post-and-lintel roof frame; from front to back are respectively the front hall, skylight, main hall, stage with two small skylights on left and right, and finally the offering hall.

The sides are wing rooms with column-and-tie structure, with mezzanines.Except for ten wooden pillars inside the front hall, all other pillars are stone pillars.

Different combinations of three pillar sizes and five pillar base specifications indirectly define the spatial hierarchy.

The most important space is of course the main hall, located between three skylights, with eight cylindrical pillars supplemented by giant basin-shaped pillar bases.

The main hall's name still takes the two characters "Degeng" (Obtaining Cultivation) from the third-generation ancestor Cheng Quan's words "content to cultivate fields for life," called "Degeng Hall.

" Pillars in the wing rooms and offering hall are all square, but only the pillars on both sides of the main hall and the four central pillars of the offering hall are paired with a higher-specification square pillar base; the rest use simple four-sided inverted-cone pillar bases of size comparable to the pillar cross-section.

The two eave pillars in the middle of the front hall facing the main hall use stone cylindrical pillars with drum-shaped pillar bases.

The remaining pillars in the front hall are thinner wooden pillars with smaller drum-shaped pillar bases.

Inside the two front gold pillars of the main hall are respectively written "Six generations extend the clan lineage with rules and models; a thousand years grandly honor filial offerings as instruction and practice," and the couplet on the rear gold pillars, facing forward, reads "Descendants inherit ancestral virtue coeval with Fang Mountain; former glory undimmed, grandsons' plans grow with Lishui River.

" The "Degeng Hall" plaque is also hung between the rear gold pillars, slightly tilted downward.Obviously, the place these two couplets and horizontal plaque jointly "point to" is where Cheng descendants kneel in the hall.

The offering hall is five bays wide, with no wing rooms on either side, and its floor is forty centimeters higher than the main hall.

Behind the offering hall was originally the place for placing tablets according to the left-zhi-right-mu system; although these wooden platforms and shrines are now all missing, their positions and heights can be inferred from mortises on the pillar sides.

Formerly, if a Cheng male on the old street passed away, his family would make a tablet and send it to Shiyuan Gong Hall; if he was a descendant of Yougongchang, another would be sent to Zuoxun Gong Hall; if also of Gongxia Yichang, a third would be sent to Shaochang Hall; for married males, husband-and-wife tablets would be made.

If the wife was still alive at the time, a red cloth would temporarily cover her tablet; if the wife passed away first, they would wait until the husband's death to enter the hall together.

The wing rooms on both sides of the main hall are mainly used for storing sacrificial supplies.

The second-floor mezzanine stores large utensils, such as the dragon head and tail used in annual dragon lantern dances.

Outside the south wall of the hall is an irregular small room, normally the office and residence of the schoolteacher, used as a kitchen during New Year and Qingming festivals.

Zuoxun Gong Hall Zuoxun Gong Hall is much smaller in architectural scale, simply a three-sided courtyard.

The main hall is three bays, post-and-lintel structure, with dimensions very close to Shiyuan Gong Hall's main hall.

The east and west wing rooms are each three bays, column-and-tie structure, with the two southern bays having mezzanines.

The first bay of the wing rooms and the main hall enclose an inner courtyard about three meters deep.

Since the "Great Leap Forward" in 1958, Zuoxun Gong's property has been jointly owned by four families.

According to Elder Cheng Maofa (born 1934, Chong generation, 21st generation), who lives in the west wing room, his stepfather Cheng Xuanzhan, being very poor, was allocated part of Zuoxun Gong Hall's property in 1946, and he moved in with his stepfather and biological father's family.

Before that, due to poverty and lack of housing, they would also rent rooms in the hall.Later, three more families bought rooms.Due to residential needs, Zuoxun Gong Hall is now divided into many small compartments.

But according to Elder Cheng Maofa, the main rooms in the hall were formerly all open; the partition boards now used by each family as doors and windows were dismantled from the door panels in front of the tablets in the original main hall; the hall formerly had a plaque inscribed "Gong Yuan" (Tribute First), unfortunately damaged during the Cultural Revolution.

Shaochang Hall It is said that Shaochang Hall was built by Cheng Lizhong (1736-1793) in the fifty-third year of Qianlong (1788) to repay his father-in-law's gift of silver for "building a flower hall at the foot of the rock.

" Cheng Lizhong, courtesy name Fengshi, art name Yi'an, was the grandson of Zuoxun Gong and son of Cheng Jingchen.

Cheng Jingchen's four sons each took "Fa" (Prosperity), "Qiang" (Strength), "Gang" (Firmness), "Yi" (Resolution) as branch names, so Cheng Lizhong's descendants are called "Gongxia Yichang.

" Because Cheng Lizhong was the youngest, "Yichang" was colloquially called "Xiaochang" (Small Chang).In Yongkang dialect, "xiao" (small) and "shao" are homophones, hence the name Shaochang Hall.

Environment Shaochang Hall faces south with its back to north, located west of Fangyan Stream, with its back diagonally facing Fangyan Mountain.

Before Shaochang Hall was built, this may have been just farmland.For a long time after its completion, it stood alone on this side of Fangyan Stream.

In front of the hall is a relatively large open space, which serves both as a threshing ground for villagers after the autumn harvest and as the climax location for welcoming dragon lanterns in the first lunar month.

South of the open space is a cinema built decades ago; now that every household has a television, the cinema has become quiet.

The east edge of the open space is defined by Fangyan Stream; on the east bank of the stream is the large mansion funded by Cheng Lizhong's father-in-law, with an in-house pond, in-house well, garden, and vegetable plots.

At the south and north ends of the open space, a small bridge each spans Fangyan Stream leading to the mansion.

Not far north from Shaochang Hall, one can also reach Shiyuan Gong Hall and Zuoxun Gong Hall via Wangping Bridge.

Architecture Shaochang Hall's planar layout is basically similar to Shiyuan Gong Hall, with comparable main hall and offering hall dimensions, but its total area is twice that of Shiyuan Gong Hall.

Compared with Shiyuan Gong Hall, Shaochang Hall's main planar differences are as follows: the front hall and wing rooms are deeper; there are local corridors between the wing rooms and the main spaces on the axis; the three main skylights are all relatively large; and between the wing rooms on both sides of the main hall there is also a small skylight with a staircase each.

The left and right ends of the hall's front facade are高低错落 horse-head walls, with the main entrance in the center and a secondary entrance on each side connecting to corridors of the wing rooms and front hall.

Additionally, several square and round small windows are高低错落 inserted in this gray brick wall, mainly for ventilating the end wing rooms and front hall side bays.

The front hall central bay is full-height, with the second-floor space of the side bays connected to the wing rooms on both sides.

The central bay uses crescent beams, front and rear single-step beams, with a child pillar erected on the middle double-step beam; the upper floor uses column-and-tie structure.

Passing through the front hall is a central courtyard of the same width, with corridors on all four sides and twelve eave pillars surrounding it.

Above each eave pillar is an ox-leg carved with coiled dragons, birds and beasts, or flowers and trees; above the ox-leg is a set of exquisite stacked dou (bracket sets) supporting the overhanging eave lintel above.

The outer sides of the stacked dou all have openwork carved insert leaves, and the inner sides are coiled-dragon-shaped curved beams.

The three-bay main hall's beam frame is fully exposed, with a nine-purlin depth.

The central bay has four pillars, using front and rear double-step beams plus a middle five-purlin beam; the side bays use five pillars, combining column-and-tie with post-and-lintel, with four sets of double-step beams between pillars.

The entire roof weight of the main hall is transmitted successively through purlins and lintels, stacked dou, large dou, flower blocks under large dou, crescent beams, beam brackets, and finally to the pillars.

Between the stacked dou supporting the purlins and the pillars, curved beams are also used for connection; curved beams not only have a strong decorative effect but also serve to stabilize the structure longitudinally.

Besides curved beams, stacked dou, spandrel brackets, flower blocks, beam heads, and beam brackets are all key points of large wooden structure carving in Shaochang Hall.

Additionally, the undersides of the two upper gold purlins, front lower gold purlin, eave purlin, and overhanging eave lintel have exquisite openwork relief carvings of flowers, birds, fish, and patterns.

Behind the main hall is a square-plan stage, with a nearly square skylight on each side.From here, the wing room depth becomes shallower, allowing the main spaces on the axis to widen to five bays.

The five-bay offering hall is located on a two-step-high platform.Its beam frame structure is generally similar to the main hall.

The difference is that the front and rear double-step beams became single-step beams; outside the rear single-step beam, a corridor with column-and-tie structure was added as space for placing shrines; the front of the central bay underwent some beam frame changes due to the need to connect with the stage.

The middle three bays of the offering hall have doors between the gold pillars but can be fully opened.

According to elders, the doors were once carved with exquisite Twenty-Four Paragons of Filial Piety, to educate Cheng descendants who came to worship during festivals.

The offering hall's large wooden carvings are slightly simpler than the main hall's; no curved beams are used between the stacked dou under the purlins, only scrolling-grass-shaped insert leaves on both sides of the stacked dou.

The east and west wing rooms are divided from south to north into five spaces of different sizes.

The middle three bays each open windows toward different skylights for lighting, while the two end bays use exterior wall windows for ventilation.

There are corridors between the wing rooms and the front hall, first skylight, and main hall; between the wing rooms behind the main hall side walls there is also a small skylight each, with a staircase on one side of each skylight leading to the second floor.

Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall The Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall is located in a small ravine at the western foot of Lion Mountain, facing west with its back to east, closely adjacent to the old street.

The hall's position is the highest part of this section of the old street; there are no buildings between it and Fangyan Mountain, giving a feeling of facing Fangyan and overlooking the valley.

The Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall is the only non-Cheng surname hall on the old street.According to the "Youchuan Zhou Clan Genealogy," this Zhou branch is called "Caoshu Zhou.

" Its founding ancestor Shiwen Gong (1254-1315) served as Liangzhe Tiju (Provincial Commissioner) from the seventeenth to twenty-eighth year of Yuan Zhiyuan (1280-1291), and because "by imperial order he also supervised the salt tax of the Taiwen Branch Division; after completing his term he passed through Yongkang and loved its beautiful customs," he moved from Jinhua Pantang to Caoshu in Shengping Township, Yongkang, hence named "Caoshu Zhou.

" Since then, Shiwen Gong's descendants moved from Caoshu to various places in Yongkang.

The Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall was built here because the tomb of founding ancestor Zhou Shiwen is located not far behind it, in a cave called "Lion Excreting" on Gongdao Mountain.

It is said that the Zhou family only lived on the old street for a very short time and left before the Cheng family moved here; others say the Zhou never lived here, and only in the ten days before Qingming would over a hundred people come to worship the Zhou founding ancestor.

The "Youchuan Zhou Clan Genealogy" records that here originally there were only "three bays of flat hall, with several thatched rooms in front of the hall," used to provide resting places for descendants coming from afar to worship ancestors during Qingming.

Later, the three clans of Youchuan, Xianchuan, and Zhangchuan expanded it to its current scale; the existing building was rebuilt in the thirty-fourth year of Guangxu (1908).

When the Kuomintang Zhejiang Provincial Government was temporarily stationed in Fangyan, this place served as the office of the Zhejiang Provincial Police Brigade, with a small prison inside.

The Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall is a nearly square four-sided courtyard.

The street-facing side has horse-head walls on both sides; the white wall tops were once painted with colored pictures, but now only mottled colors can be faintly seen.

The main door is centered, with a side door on each side leading to inner corridors.

The first entrance is a three-bay, seven-purlin-deep worship hall, the most exquisitely carved part of the entire building; behind the skylight, on a high platform, is a five-bay offering hall of slightly shallower depth; on both sides are five wing rooms each for visiting worshippers to stay temporarily.

Moreover, "small halls on both sides outside the hall, with eight rooms of buildings, serve as the residence of the caretaker.

" These "small halls" refer to the road pavilions on both sides of the Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall, spanning across the old street; these two pavilions are among the few places on the old street that can both shelter from wind and rain and provide rest.

Of the "eight rooms of buildings," only three now belong to the Zhou family; the tomb keeper Zhou Yanhao and his wife, along with his sister-in-law, currently live here.

Genealogy records that the hall and tomb should be guarded by reliable and capable descendants轮流 sent by the three clans of Youchuan, Xianchuan, and Zhangchuan, and "Yanhao Gong is a descendant of the Youchuan fourth small branch; from his great-grandfather onward, he has lived guarding the founding ancestor's hall and tomb for over a hundred years.

" Other Halls Run-er Ancestral Hall is located east of Chenglu Village, first built in the Ming Dynasty.From its architectural layout, it should be similar to Shiyuan Gong Hall—i.e.

, three-courtyard main hall with pin-shaped skylight layout.Based on land reform period policies, Run-er Ancestral Hall, belonging to a hall over five generations old, was nationalized.

It thus first served as a primary school, then in 1988 was converted into the activity venue of the Chenglu Village Senior Citizens Association.

In 2006, the hall interior was completely renovated, with floors and部分 walls redone in concrete, and the central large-span roof also changed to steel truss structure.

Cheng Zhaofu Hall is located at the western foot of Lion Mountain, west of the old street.Built by Cheng Zhaofu in 1924, it is part of Cheng Zhenxing Inn.

The hall's form is basically the same as Shiyuan Gong Hall, with slightly larger planar dimensions.

The hall also served as guest rooms during incense seasons; its central open space not only served as the inn's hall for receiving pilgrims and hosting banquets, but at night artists would also be invited to sing opera here.

During the period when the Kuomintang Zhejiang Provincial Government was temporarily stationed in Fangyan, this place also hosted government meetings, various banquets, and even weddings.

Qiwen Gong Hall is located west of Huilong Bridge, built by Cheng Zhaochun, younger brother of the Cheng Zhenxing Inn owner.

Cheng Zhaochun (born 1864, death year unknown), courtesy name Qiwen, was the fifth son of Cheng Lelai, and in 1900 opened Cheng Chengxing Umbrella Shop at the south end of the old street.

According to Elder Cheng Zhenfeng (born 1939, Chong generation, 21st generation), grandson of Cheng Zhaochun, the hall layout was originally similar to Shiyuan Gong Hall.Unfortunately now only the middle three bays remain.

There is also a hall on the old street almost forgotten by people: the Lufang Hall, located west of the Xiahukeng mouth.

According to Elder Cheng Zhenfeng's recollection, Lufang Hall was built under the supervision of his grandfather Cheng Zhaochun, who also served as hall manager.

The hall's scale is slightly smaller than Shiyuan Gong Hall, with only front and rear two courtyards.By this calculation, the hall was built around the early twentieth century.

Unfortunately during the Cultural Revolution, due to lack of maintenance, this hall was ultimately destroyed by termites and fire.Currently only some ruined walls remain, and even the basic planar layout cannot be identified.

宗祠供奉和祭拜祖先仪式性场所 祠堂里供奉历代先人牌位逢年过节祭祀时挂本房祖宗画像 宗祠设谱房管理宗族人口派系行辈婚姻 祠堂商议族内大事调解族人矛盾旌表功名办学堂存放公共器物 大祠堂有戏台特殊日子宗祠暂时放下神圣地位成为全村文化娱乐中心甚至允许族人赌博娱乐 通过一系列祖先崇拜仪式及活动加强程氏家族内部凝聚力 加强宗族内聚力就是加强人际合作社会单元能量这是人们生存并发展最重要依靠从而促成祖先崇拜也便是血缘认同 橙麓村和岩下村供奉同一始祖程锴老街旁曾先后建造大大小小至少八座程氏各房派祠堂 江浙地区多数村落中祠堂通常位于村落相对中心地带其位置和朝向多少影响村落布局 老街发展受到地形限制所以这里祠堂大多都沿街坐落并没能对村落空间序列起到决定性影响 用地紧张情况下祠堂前通常都有水塘或空地这个空间依旧是村落生活舞台 程氏宗祠始建于明正德己亥年1515年位于橙麓村象鼻岗山脚 程氏宗祠是迁居这里程氏所建第一个祠堂也是岩下老街众多祠堂中唯一被收入县志的三十五都程宗祠始祖闰二闰三 祠堂坐西朝东东侧是螺蛳塘山山上建有三世祖程权之墓 嘉靖年间祠堂曾改建为前后两进道光丙午年重修成五开间前后三进院落用石柱代替糟朽木柱 整个祠堂宽二十多米进深近五十米有牌门一座享堂五间寝室九间还有厢房位于南北两侧 抗日战争时期中共浙江省委书记刘英被关在程氏宗祠内并于1942年5月13日在祠堂内遇害 1992年1月21日程氏宗祠随着刘英烈士陵园开工而被拆毁 当地程氏后人努力下部分石柱柱础和门楣等建筑构件被送到文楼村保存以便日后重建 橙麓村程家麟老人花钱买下一些柱础现在还依然摆放在他家院墙外 一些柱础上面架上石条成了村里人小憩和唠家常地方 橙麓村水塘边和小路旁还能看到许多似乎曾被用作为台明石大块石料水塘里一块洗衣石上还有两个刚劲楷体大字之墓 世远公祠俗称小祠堂是为纪念程坦1411-1477而建造 程坦字世远为永康程氏六世祖 作勳公祠指十三世祖程际烈1646-1725 作勳公祠又叫友恭常祠友常和恭常分别代表作勳公两个儿子程伊臣1691-1765和程经臣1697-1771的房派 虽然世远公比作勳公早出生二百多年但是作勳公祠建在世远公祠之前并且它们都比不远处绍常祠堂要早 世远公祠位于作勳公祠前并挡住了其正门也从侧面支持世远公祠建造较晚说法 程茂发1934年生崇字辈程氏21代老人讲作勳公祠是程伊臣和程经臣为其父亲共同建造的所以又叫友恭常祠而世远公祠是作勳公孙子建造的 作勳公祠建于十八世纪中叶世远公祠可能在随后一二十年后 ...等共159个
四、宗教建筑
中文 Chinese

由于方岩山上的广慈寺和佑顺侯祠历来香火旺盛，村里的人如果遇到了什么大事，就会上山去求菩萨和胡公。对许多人来讲，胡公就是一个能管天管地、神通广大的万能神。即使是对胡公将信将疑的村民，也多会在正月里上山请三炷香、求个签，保佑自己和家人在新的一年能欢欢喜喜、平平安安。

正因为如此，岩下老街上的宗教建筑相对并不发达，只有几个并不重要的小庙。老街上的人对求神拜佛的态度，充分体现了汉人普遍见庙就烧香，见菩萨就拜的泛宗教信仰。

这一特点也会生动地体现在这里的宗教建筑上，例如，方岩山上胡公大帝后面端坐着如来佛，玉皇大帝脚下供着地藏菩萨，广慈寺前厅屋顶彩画《西游记》，山下观音旁边陪坐关公和土地等。

经堂 经堂原位于下胡坑溪以南，即原城头以南，老街以西的位置，由尼姑常年驻守。听老人们讲，经堂原是一幢东西向的合院建筑，大殿内原主供观音，观音两旁供关公和土地。以前每月初一、十五都会有人去烧香、请愿、抽签。不幸的是，经堂在二十多年前被付之一炬。

本保庙 本保庙，俗称土地庙，通常是用来供奉民间神祇的小庙。岩下村的"本保庙"原本位于蜥蜴山，前山头上的一棵大柏树下，面向它所保护的村落。所谓"庙"，其实仅仅是一个用石头围合的，坐南朝北，供奉着朱相公的小神龛。据民间传说，朱相公的原型为朱丹溪。

记载，朱丹溪（1281—1358年），名震亨，字彦修，元代金华人，因世居丹溪，故人称朱丹溪。作为"滋阴派"的创始人，他被称为金元四大名医之一，并于去世后成为当地的主要民间神祇。每年六月初六，地处东阳的岩背村和桂村还有以朱相公为名的庙会。

虽然朱相公和胡则生活的年代不同，但因为他们都曾为当地人做过好事，所以在老街上朱相公又被尊称为胡则十兄弟之一，并曾与其他九兄弟一起被供奉在广慈寺前殿，胡则像的两侧。解放后，新建的本保庙位于原址对面的蜈蚣山上，所以也被称作南山庙。

这座建筑高高地坐落在蜈蚣山的第二个小山包上，与周围环境形成鲜明对比。站在门口，可以对下坑口这片建筑群一览无余。本保庙是一个一进的小院，院内有一座三开间的正殿。正殿中间供朱相公及夫人，左边供土地公和土地婆，右边供财神公和财神婆。每月初一和十五都会有人来祭拜，村里也有专门的人负责添加庙里的灯油。

万成庙 远在五峰鸡鸣峰后的山坳里，还有一个观音庙，又叫万成庙。老街上和邻村的年轻夫妇，不免都会来这里拜拜，得子后还会来还愿，所以这里的香火一直也还不错。万成庙的入口，在高大茂密林木的掩映下，十分隐蔽。进门之后还要走上十多分钟才可以看见正殿。正殿三开间，同样是利用岩体本身的洞穴，依山而建。

在较高的一处洞穴前，还建有三间僧房，曾作为庙里尼姑的住所。殿内正中供送子观音，左供观音菩萨，右供千手千眼观音。三座观音均为立像，其中送子观音双臂前伸，手中抱一男童。当求子的年轻夫妇拜倒在观音像前的时候，他们刚好可以看到观音手中的男童，正在向他们张开双臂、面带甜甜的微笑。在大殿的角落里，还供有地藏菩萨。想来人们在求子的同时，也会希望这个孩子能够降生在一个国泰民安、风调雨顺的年景里。

在浙江省国民政府临时迁驻方岩的那几年，万成庙还曾作为浙江省史料征集委员会的办公场所。民政厅厅长阮毅成的阮公馆也位于万成庙前这块幽静之地。

· · · · · ·
英文 English

Because Guangci Temple and Youshun Marquis Temple on Fangyan Mountain have always had prosperous incense, villagers encountering major events would go up the mountain to seek Bodhisattvas and Hugong.

For many people, Hugong is an omnipotent deity who governs heaven and earth with boundless supernatural powers.

Even villagers skeptical of Hugong would mostly go up the mountain in the first lunar month to light three sticks of incense and draw a divination slip, praying that they and their families would be joyful and safe in the new year.

Precisely because of this, religious architecture on Yanxia Old Street is relatively undeveloped, with only a few unimportant small temples.

The attitude of old street residents toward praying to deities fully reflects the Han people's widespread pan-religious belief of burning incense at every temple and worshipping every Bodhisattva.

This characteristic is also vividly reflected in the religious architecture here—for example, Tathagata Buddha sits upright behind Emperor Hugong on Fangyan Mountain, Ksitigarbha Bodhisattva is enshrined at the feet of the Jade Emperor, the front hall roof of Guangci Temple has colored paintings of "Journey to the West," and at the foot of the mountain, Guan Gong and the Earth God sit beside Guanyin.

Jingtang (Nunnery) Jingtang was originally located south of Xiahukeng Stream, i.e., south of the original Chengtou (city gate), west of the old street, with nuns permanently stationed.

According to elders, Jingtang was originally an east-west courtyard building, with the main hall primarily enshrining Guanyin, flanked by Guan Gong and the Earth God.

Formerly, on the first and fifteenth days of each lunar month, people would go to burn incense, make vows, and draw divination slips.Unfortunately, Jingtang was burned to the ground over twenty years ago.

Benbao Temple (Local Protection Temple) Benbao Temple, commonly called the Earth God Temple, is usually a small temple for enshrining folk deities.

Yanxia Village's "Benbao Temple" was originally located under a large cypress tree on the front hilltop of Lizard Mountain, facing the village it protected.

The so-called "temple" was actually merely a small shrine enclosed by stones, facing north with its back to south, enshrining Lord Zhu.According to folk legend, Lord Zhu was modeled after Zhu Danxi.

Records show Zhu Danxi (1281-1358), named Zhenheng, courtesy name Yanxiu, was a native of Jinhua in the Yuan Dynasty; because his family lived at Danxi for generations, he was called Zhu Danxi.

As the founder of the "Nourishing Yin School," he was known as one of the Four Great Physicians of the Jin and Yuan Dynasties, and after his death became a major local folk deity.

Every year on the sixth day of the sixth lunar month, Yanbei Village and Gui Village in Dongyang still hold temple fairs named after Lord Zhu.

Although Lord Zhu and Hu Ze lived in different eras, because both did good deeds for local people, Lord Zhu was also revered on the old street as one of Hu Ze's ten sworn brothers, and was once enshrined together with the other nine brothers in the front hall of Guangci Temple, on both sides of Hu Ze's statue.

After liberation, the newly built Benbao Temple was located on Centipede Mountain opposite the original site, hence also called Nanshan Temple (South Mountain Temple).

This building sits high on the second small hill of Centipede Mountain, forming a sharp contrast with the surrounding environment.Standing at the entrance, one can have a panoramic view of the building complex at Xiakeng mouth.

Benbao Temple is a single-courtyard small compound, with a three-bay main hall inside.

The center of the main hall enshrines Lord Zhu and his wife, the left side enshrines the Earth God and Earth Goddess, and the right side enshrines the God of Wealth and Goddess of Wealth.

On the first and fifteenth days of each lunar month, people come to worship, and the village also has dedicated persons responsible for adding lamp oil to the temple.

Wancheng Temple Far in the ravine behind Jiming Peak of the Five Peaks, there is another Guanyin temple, also called Wancheng Temple.

Young couples from the old street and neighboring villages would inevitably come here to pray, and after having a child would come to fulfill their vows, so the incense here has always been quite good.

The entrance of Wancheng Temple, hidden by tall dense trees, is very concealed.After entering, one must walk for over ten minutes before seeing the main hall.

The three-bay main hall similarly utilizes caves in the rock itself, built against the mountain.In front of a higher cave, three monk's rooms were also built, once serving as residences for the temple nuns.

The center of the hall enshrines Songzi Guanyin (Child-Giving Guanyin), the left enshrines Guanyin Bodhisattva, and the right enshrines Thousand-Hand Thousand-Eye Guanyin.

All three Guanyin statues are standing figures; among them, Songzi Guanyin extends both arms forward, holding a boy child.

When young couples praying for a child kneel before the Guanyin statue, they can just see the boy in Guanyin's arms opening his arms to them with a sweet smile.

In a corner of the main hall, Ksitigarbha Bodhisattva is also enshrined.

Presumably while praying for a child, people also hope that this child will be born in a year of national peace and prosperity with favorable weather.

During the years when the Kuomintang Zhejiang Provincial Government was temporarily stationed in Fangyan, Wancheng Temple also served as the office of the Zhejiang Provincial Historical Materials Collection Committee.

The Ruan Residence of Ruan Yicheng, Director of the Civil Affairs Department, was also located in this quiet place in front of Wancheng Temple.

五、塘堰与井泉
中文 Chinese

塘堰之设，水利之所在也。塘以储水，堰以节出。遇旱则尤资其利。井与泉并列从其类也。山下出泉，蒙象也，易则之以。果行育德。木上有水，井象也，易则之以。劳民劝相。古之为民者，凿井而饮，耕田而食。古之立邑者，相其阴阳，观其流泉，是经制之大端也。县志里对塘堰和井泉的概述，既明确地说明了它们的作用和重要性，又强调了它们是"为民"和"立邑"者首要考虑的问题。

这里主要的溪流是方岩溪，它自南向北，蜿蜒流过岩下老街所在的山谷。沿途又有几条小溪，从东西两侧的山坑中流出，最终汇入方岩溪。这几条溪水便是岩下老街灌溉的主要水源。但正如县志里所说的，每当干旱的时候，溪水就不够用，就要动用平时储存在水塘里的水。

在岩下老街两侧，有大大小小的水塘二十几个。有名字的就约有十个，例如位于程立重宅院内的澈月塘、世远公祠前的源流塘、城头外的八沢塘（又称八百把塘）等。由于位置和大小的不同，它们不光有灌溉作用，还兼有救火、浣洗等功能。在祠堂、或是大型宅院附近，大都有水塘。这些水塘又被称作"火种塘"，它们的另一个重要功能就是救火。

例如，新隆兴旅店后面的"屋后塘"；还有程正伟火腿店前的"屋前塘"。这里最大的水塘就是位于五峰固厚峰东麓的"深塘"，它也是目前老街上居民饮用水的主要来源。最有讲头的水塘，要算是得耕居前的前塘。这个半月形水塘的池底不但有用卵石镶嵌成的"双龙戏珠"图案，它还给得耕居带来了"玉带环腰"的吉象。

老街上有两个别名为"屋里塘"的水塘。其中一个是位于程阮阮旅店旁边的澈月塘，另一个位于作勳公祠东边的院落内，又被称为"后塘"。澈月塘为长方形，面积只比绍常祠堂小一点儿，它原本属于十五世祖程立重宅院的一部分，与之呼应的还有位于水塘南边的"屋里井"。

屋里塘和屋里井都被当地人视为吉象，不但由于它们的存在能方便日常生活，降低火灾的可能性，更因为能做到有屋里塘或屋里井的，都是极其富有的大户人家。后塘是一个名副其实的"屋里塘"，它被东西两组层高相差一层的"L"形房屋围在当中，西侧房屋的一层和水塘同高，东侧的一层和老街同高，约是西侧房屋二层的高度。两组建筑之间围合了一个小的天井，天井即是水塘，只有东北边的那排房前廊可以到达。

现在水塘里长满了绿绿的苔藓，衬着乱石垒砌的、高高的墙基，别有一番风味。据1989年编写的《永康县志》所述，岩下老街两侧共有水井18眼。这其中大部分水井属于村里共有，也有少数几个位于大型宅院内。最有名的可能就是位于狮子山脚下，周氏宗祠以南，老仁昌旅店前的"饮泉"。

"饮泉"紧邻老街东侧，井前有一小块开敞的空间，后面就是拔地而起的狮子山，在面朝老街的一面，还有用碎瓷器镶嵌成的"饮泉"二字。这口水井为老仁昌旅店的老板程宣洁所修，据说井里的水可以治病，所以水井前经常有络绎不绝的取水人。由于水源紧张，老街上的程氏后人逐渐形成了一些相沿成习的用水准则。

例如井水专门用来饮用；蔬菜、水果要拿到溪水里去洗；米则要在家里洗，因为洗米的水要用来喂猪；水塘除了灌溉、救火之外，在应用化学洗涤剂之前，还可以用来浣洗外衣、被子之类的东西；对于马桶、内衣裤和裤子这类的"脏"东西，只能拿到一些特定的小水塘里去洗。

· · · · · ·
英文 English

The establishment of ponds and weirs is where water conservancy lies.Ponds store water, weirs regulate its release.In times of drought, their benefit is especially relied upon.Wells and springs are listed together by category.

Springs emerging from beneath mountains is the image of the Meng hexagram; the I Ching interprets it as: resolute action nurtures virtue.

Water above wood is the image of the Jing (Well) hexagram; the I Ching interprets it as: toiling the people and exhorting mutual aid.The ancients who ruled the people dug wells for drinking and plowed fields for food.

The ancients who established cities examined their yin and yang, observed their flowing springs—this was the major principle of governance.

The county gazetteer's overview of ponds, weirs, wells, and springs both clearly explains their function and importance, and emphasizes that they were the primary consideration for those "ruling the people" and "establishing cities.

" The main stream here is Fangyan Stream, which flows from south to north, winding through the valley where Yanxia Old Street is located.

Along the way, several small streams flow out from the ravines on the east and west sides, finally merging into Fangyan Stream.These streams are the main source of irrigation for Yanxia Old Street.

But as the county gazetteer states, whenever drought occurs, stream water becomes insufficient, and water stored in ponds must be used.On both sides of Yanxia Old Street, there are over twenty ponds of various sizes.

About ten have names, such as Cheyue Pond within Cheng Lizhong's courtyard, Yuanliu Pond in front of Shiyuan Gong Hall, and Baze Pond (also called Eight-Hundred-Handle Pond) outside Chengtou.

Due to differences in location and size, they not only serve irrigation but also functions such as fire-fighting and laundry.Near ancestral halls or large mansions, there are usually ponds.

These ponds are also called "fire-seed ponds," and another important function is fire-fighting.For example, the "rear-house pond" behind Xinlongxing Inn; and the "front-house pond" in front of Cheng Zhengwei Ham Shop.

The largest pond here is "Shen Pond" (Deep Pond) at the eastern foot of Guhou Peak of the Five Peaks, which is also currently the main source of drinking water for old street residents.

The most storied pond is Qiantang (Front Pond) in front of Degeng Residence.

The bottom of this crescent-shaped pond not only has a "Double Dragons Playing with a Pearl" pattern inlaid with cobblestones, but also brings Degeng Residence the auspicious image of "Jade Belt Encircling the Waist.

" On the old street there are two ponds also called "in-house ponds.

" One is Cheyue Pond next to Cheng Ruanyuan Inn, and the other is in the courtyard east of Zuoxun Gong Hall, also called "Rear Pond.

" Cheyue Pond is rectangular, only slightly smaller than Shaochang Hall; it originally belonged to part of the fifteenth-generation ancestor Cheng Lizhong's courtyard, and corresponding to it is the "in-house well" south of the pond.

Both in-house ponds and in-house wells are regarded as auspicious by locals—not only because their existence facilitates daily life and reduces the possibility of fire, but more because those who could afford in-house ponds or wells were all extremely wealthy large households.

Rear Pond is a veritable "in-house pond," enclosed by two groups of east and west L-shaped buildings with a one-story height difference: the first floor of the west building is level with the pond, while the first floor of the east building is level with the old street, approximately the height of the west building's second floor.

Between the two groups of buildings a small skylight is enclosed, and the skylight is the pond, accessible only via the front corridor of the row of buildings on the northeast side.

Now the pond is covered with green moss, set against high wall bases built of random stones, with a distinctive charm.

According to the "Yongkang County Gazetteer" compiled in 1989, there are 18 wells on both sides of Yanxia Old Street.Most of these wells are communally owned by the village, with a few located within large mansions.

The most famous may be "Yinquan" (Drinking Spring) at the foot of Lion Mountain, south of the Zhou Clan Ancestral Hall, in front of Laorenchang Inn.

"Yinquan" is closely adjacent to the east side of the old street, with a small open space in front of the well and Lion Mountain rising abruptly behind it; on the side facing the old street, the two characters "Yinquan" are inlaid with broken porcelain.

This well was dug by Cheng Xuanjie, owner of Laorenchang Inn; it is said the well water can cure illness, so people come to fetch water in an endless stream.

Due to tight water resources, the Cheng descendants on the old street gradually formed customary water-use rules.

For example, well water is exclusively for drinking; vegetables and fruits are washed in the stream; rice is washed at home because rice-washing water is used to feed pigs; besides irrigation and fire-fighting, ponds could also be used for washing outer clothing and quilts before chemical detergents were applied; for "dirty" things like chamber pots, underwear, and trousers, they could only be washed in certain specific small ponds.

塘堰之设水利之所在也塘以储水堰以节出遇旱则尤资其利 井与泉并列从其类也山下出泉蒙象也易则之以果行育德木上有水井象也易则之以劳民劝相 古之为民者凿井而饮耕田而食古之立邑者相其阴阳观其流泉是经制之大端也 县志里对塘堰和井泉的概述既明确说明了它们的作用和重要性又强调了它们是为民和立邑者首要考虑的问题 这里主要的溪流是方岩溪它自南向北蜿蜒流过岩下老街所在的山谷 沿途又有几条小溪从东西两侧的山坑中流出最终汇入方岩溪 这几条溪水便是岩下老街灌溉的主要水源 每当干旱的时候溪水就不够用就要动用平时储存在水塘里的水 岩下老街两侧有大大小小的水塘二十几个有名字的就约有十个 澈月塘位于程立重宅院内源流塘位于世远公祠前八沢塘又称八百把塘位于城头外 由于位置和大小的不同它们不光有灌溉作用还兼有救火浣洗等功能 在祠堂或是大型宅院附近大都有水塘这些水塘又被称作火种塘它们的另一个重要功能就是救火 新隆兴旅店后面的屋后塘程正伟火腿店前的屋前塘 深塘位于五峰固厚峰东麓是这里最大的水塘也是目前老街上居民饮用水的主要来源 得耕居前的前塘是最有讲头的水塘这个半月形水塘的池底不但有用卵石镶嵌成的双龙戏珠图案它还给得耕居带来了玉带环腰的吉象 老街上有两个别名为屋里塘的水塘 澈月塘位于程阮阮旅店旁边为长方形面积只比绍常祠堂小一点儿原本属于十五世祖程立重宅院的一部分与之呼应的还有位于水塘南边的屋里井 屋里塘和屋里井都被当地人视为吉象不但由于它们的存在能方便日常生活降低火灾的可能性更因为能做到有屋里塘或屋里井的都是极其富有的大户人家 后塘位于作勳公祠东边的院落内是一个名副其实的屋里塘 后塘被东西两组层高相差一层的L形房屋围在当中西侧房屋的一层和水塘同高东侧的一层和老街同高约是西侧房屋二层的高度 两组建筑之间围合了一个小的天井天井即是水塘只有东北边的那排房前廊可以到达 现在水塘里长满了绿绿的苔藓衬着乱石垒砌的高高的墙基别有一番风味 据1989年编写的永康县志所述岩下老街两侧共有水井18眼 大部分水井属于村里共有也有少数几个位于大型宅院内 饮泉位于狮子山脚下周氏宗祠以南老仁昌旅店前是最有名的水井 饮泉紧邻老街东侧井前有一小块开敞的空间后面就是拔地而起的狮子山在面朝老街的一面还有用碎瓷器镶嵌成的饮泉二字 饮泉为老仁昌旅店老板程宣洁所修据说井里的水可以治病所以水井前经常有络绎不绝的取水人 由于水源紧张老街上的程氏后人逐渐形成了一些相沿成习的用水准则 井水专门用来饮用蔬菜水果要拿到溪水里去洗米则要在家里洗因为洗米的水要用来喂猪 水塘除了灌溉救火之外在应用化学洗涤剂之前还可以用来浣洗外衣被子之类的东西 ...等共31个

第五章 方岩山与广慈寺

3
节
一、方岩山
中文 Chinese

方岩山坐落在永康县城东五十里，海拔高度约350米。它位于岩下村的西侧，和东面的狮子山、蜥蜴山和蜈蚣山等一起界定了岩下村的空间范围。

清康熙《永康县志》里有一段对方岩山精辟而生动的描写： 高二百丈，周六里，平地拔起，四面如削，惟南通一道，至山腰而绝，叠石为磴，阶级而升，曰百步峻。磴上沿岩架石为栈道，曰飞桥。将至顶，有两石对竖，屋之，曰透关，俗呼曰峰门。下有飞虹亭，寺僧慧韬新筑也。入关，地更平旷，约数百亩，有井，曰砚井，有池，可亩余。

方岩山 对于上方岩的香客，当老街走到底之后，就会向西拐进方岩山脚下的一个山坳里，再顺着"南通一道"上山。所谓的"南通一道"其实就是夹在方岩和南岩之间的一条登山小路。进山后，沿着这条路走不远就是因下马泉而得名的下马泉长廊。

这里因为以前曾经挤满卖方岩纸花的摊位，所以又被称为"花廊"。下马泉顾名思义标志着这"南通一道"的终点，从这里开始人们就可以体会到北宋永康进士应材所描写的那番景色： 石磴巍峨促膝行，行时不觉看时惊。纵教良匠描难就，自是天工造化成。罗汉洞 过下马泉不远，就是坐落在南岩岩腰的罗汉洞。罗汉洞也是当年大悲寺的肇基祖师，正德禅师的栖息之所。

相传正德禅师借化缘得来的资助，塑了十八尊罗汉置于洞中，并希望借助罗汉的法力在方岩山顶修建大悲寺，罗汉洞便由此得名。在大悲寺建好之后，罗汉洞便不再被寺院使用。不知从什么时候开始，便有尼姑在这里安家，并一直持续到"文革"。最后一位尼姑叫应绍为，她在这里不但给人看病，而且还收留流浪的乞丐，所以广受大家尊敬。她去世之后，就被埋葬在罗汉洞对面方岩山腰的山洞里。

在罗汉洞的西南角，有一口名为"蛟龙泉"的古井。其"水甘美如饴，性沉厚，尝置铜钱数枚于杯水中，而水不外溢"。在《蛟龙泉记》里，还记载着关于这口井的一个美丽传说。据说曾经有一个姓应的尼姑居住在罗汉洞里，夏天，她每天都要翻山越岭到五峰下的龙湫泉取水，供香客饮用。她的举动感动了龙王，于是龙王就变成鲤鱼托梦于她，指出可以打井的地点。经过了数年的努力，她终于打井成功并找到了泉水，因此取名为"蛟龙泉"。百步峻与步云亭 过了罗汉洞就是"叠石为磴"的"百步峻"。

由于这段台阶坡度较大，人们在上行的时候，会不由自主地看到每一块条石的侧面和石栏杆上都刻着捐献者的姓名和籍贯，就好像在读一本用石头写的书。这个过程既让人忘却了攀登的辛劳与枯燥，又不禁油然升起一番敬意。百步峻的尽端就是横跨在路中，飞檐翘角的步云亭。步云亭的位置恰到好处，人们可以在这里稍事休息，凭栏远眺。正如挂在上面的对联所说，"憩步邀群仙，随云来半山"。

依清康熙县志的描述，透关"下有飞虹亭，寺僧慧韬新筑也"，但是这段话在清光绪县志中却被删掉了；再加上朱谨的诗句"飞虹亭畔息，贾勇入峰关"，这里的"峰关"应该就是县志里所提的"峰门"。可以推断飞虹亭可能就是现在的步云亭的前身。这也符合广慈寺前住持允明禅师所说，即步云亭是寺里的和尚建造的。飞桥 步云亭再往上就是长一百多米，"沿岩架石"的栈道，又称"飞桥"。

这段栈道架设在悬崖峭壁之间，曲折有致，在清晨和雨后，就会呈现永康十景之一的"飞桥锁雾"。现在的飞桥是1962年夏天用钢筋混凝土重建的，宽度近2.5米。已经比当年少了些险峻。据允明禅师讲，飞桥始建于开山祖师时期，它以前的状况可以从题刻在旁边的《重建飞桥记略》中略知一二，"原砌以石，宽仅尺许，几经改造，增至五尺"。

当地人对飞桥的改造还可以从其他文献中找到一些印记，例如在《程公孟举墓表》中有记载程氏四世祖程鹏在明永乐乙酉年（1405年）曾"造飞桥，设义浆"。透关 走到飞桥头有两巨石对峙，最窄处仅相隔一米多。从现存最早的明正德《永康县志》，到清光绪《永康县志》里都有同样的记载，即两块巨石之上曾建有题额为透关的亭子。

透关亭如今已不复存在，取而代之的是题额为"天门"，材料采用石材加钢筋混凝土的重檐八角亭。据允明禅师讲，"天门"下面的部分，是开山祖师最先建的，虽屡经重修，但是一直都有。目前上面的亭子是民国37年（1948年）造的。天王殿 正如县志所述，过了天门地势就变得较为平缓空旷，而首先看到的便是天王殿。天王殿的出现预示着佛家圣地近在咫尺，但是它的位置和格局却十分独特。

天王殿通常是中国寺院建筑群中轴线上的第一进殿。殿内中间通常供奉弥勒菩萨，并在其后供奉面向北方的韦驮菩萨，东西两旁则供奉四大天王。在广慈寺，天王殿没有和其他的大殿一起位于中轴线上，而是位于寺院主体建筑之前一里多远的位置。这座天王殿骑跨于路中，中间开敞以便走人，仿佛一座城门。不仅如此，殿内只有四大天王在两侧，并没有依常规供奉弥勒和韦驮菩萨。

有学者认为，在历史上天王殿最初多见于净土宗的寺院，在两宋以后才开始出现在大部分其他宗派的寺院里。广慈寺是天台宗下的寺院并始建于唐代，这可能就是为什么寺院主体建筑群内并没有天王殿的位置。这一点还可以和从广慈寺关系密切的天台山国清寺得到旁证。广慈寺的肇基祖师正德禅师来自于天台山国清寺，国清寺在那个年代的平面布局已无从考究。

目前寺院中轴线上的三幢建筑，即弥勒殿、雨花殿和大雄宝殿都是雍正十一年（1733年）重建的。但是殿内的塑像却是在"文革"末期，为了迎接日本天台宗佛教徒参拜祖庙而从其他地方调来的，或是以后重塑的。即便当时调来的塑像是以原有塑像为依据，国清寺的四大天王并没有与弥勒和韦驮菩萨坐落在同一间殿堂内，而是被单独供奉在雨花殿内。

天门头空基 天王殿后面这块稍为平坦的范围以前称作"天门头空基"，每年香汛期间这里都会散落一些卖小吃的摊位，以方便香客歇歇脚，填饱肚子以继续赶路。目前，路旁这些小吃摊位除了经营诸如馄饨、面条、豆干之类的食品外，最有特色的要算是被外地人称为"永康食果"的肉麦饼了。肉麦饼是用肥多瘦少的猪肉丁拌梅干菜作馅，包法类似于北方的馅饼，只是厚一点儿，最后用又浅又平的大锅烤熟。

制作过程中的诀窍是，在捏摺的时候要稍向上提，使空气进入后再封口，这样烤出来的饼里面充满空气，从外面看起来非常饱满，而且还能保证皮又焦又脆。最重要的是，肉麦饼可以事先做好，叠落在一起放在锅边又不致变凉。若有客人来了，可以随来随买，随走随吃，不妨碍香客们赶路。天街 从小吃街转过一个弯就到了"天街"，天街全长约半里，目前两端各矗立着一个石牌坊作为标志。天街以前称为"前街路"，路两边多是两层高的店面，大一点的还有三层高。

这其中包括近十家小规模的旅店和以经营包括《方岩指南》等各类图书为主的"日新书社"。每年从阴历八月到三月之间还会有十余家临时小店开张，它们不但出售京广杂货、铁器、木器、儿童玩具、木梳等，还经营牛肉、油条、肉麦饼、馄饨、豆腐花、豆腐圆和茶水等当地小吃。天街现在只在南侧有一两层高的店面，这些建筑都是在1988年重建的。

店面多经营祭祀用品，例如蜡烛、香纸和佛像等，并兼营方岩货。经营类似商品的店面以前多位于寺院殿堂前后，1925年版的《方岩指南》中就列有杂货店十一家、玩具店一家，以及经营香纸烛锭摊位十余个。天街的北侧是长生塘，传说是仙女的眼泪变成的，饮用塘中水后可以长生不老，并由此得名。

塘边有井，叫砚井，是寺里和尚赖以生存的饮用水源。在天街中段南侧，有一棵据说是和广慈寺同龄的古樟树，因其旁边生长着许多小樟树，所以叫母子樟。母子樟旁边曾经有明万历年间（1573—1620年）建造的程方峰（1498—1585年）祠堂。

现在取而代之的是"方岩仙人洞"，以借助现代化的手段来制造"会说话活菩萨"、"土地公公破土而出"、"猪八戒大闹盘丝洞"等视听效果来吸引香客。从仙人洞旁散落的巨大的柱基石和洞里依稀可见的方砖铺地，可以推测出程方峰祠堂当年的规模。

据允明禅师讲，祠堂里面原有程方峰的塑像，祠堂大门从来都是关着的，只有在清明期间会开，那时候会有五六百人来这里祭祖。程方峰和其夫人孙氏的墓建在祠堂后面。

现存的墓碑是1990年清明重立的，旁边刻有"千古英灵安在，万代浩气永存"的对联。千人坑和听泉楼 在天街尽端的东北角是千人坑。在那里，方岩山被劈成一南一北两段，中间的山谷深数百米，且宽不到一米。关于千人坑，县志里还记载着这样的传说："相传昔乡人避寇岩上，寇至援藤升，顿见有蛇，刃挥之，藤断寇坠，死者若干人，遂溃去，此坑所由名也。

"这里的"寇"指的是在北宋宣和二年（1120年），永康人陈十四和他率领的农民起义军。陈十四的起义军是永康方岩地区响应方腊起义的一支队伍。当年他们攻破县城，迫使知县赵琪退据方岩山，县志里描述的就是发生在这之后的故事。

陈十四的起义虽然在次年就被镇压了，但是人们却借此来纪念当时牺牲的起义军战士。千人坑西边曾有"听泉楼"，如今楼去泉干，只能借县志中的记载来想象那清脆的泉水声： 坑上西行百步许，下有石谷，泉出谷间，如环佩声。旧有楼，曰听泉，里人胡济源作。大夫士赋诗成集，曰听泉楼集。不传，惟吕双泉潆序，见双泉集中。听泉楼：在方岩千人坑，元邑人胡济源筑，邑大夫士多为之赋诗，集录成编，郎中吕双泉序之，李草阁有诗。

并庙有寺 在方岩山顶上，目前并排坐落着一座不大为人知的千年名刹广慈寺和常年香火旺盛的胡公庙。虽然在历史上的很长一段时间里，广慈寺和胡公庙都同时矗立在这里，但是它们的关系却和现在大不相同。

现在的胡公庙用地原属广慈寺，而胡公庙曾经位于广慈寺左面，临近长生塘的位置。这一点可以从历年的县志里找到证明，例如明嘉靖《永康县志》里讲，"有祠，曰佑顺侯祠。

右有佛庐，曰广慈寺"；而清道光《永康县志》中却写道，"临池有庙，曰赫灵。……并庙有寺，曰广慈"。这一寺一庙之间相互关系的变化，要从了解广慈寺的历史开始。

· · · · · ·
英文 English

Fangyan Mountain is located fifty li east of Yongkang county seat, with an elevation of about 350 meters.

It lies west of Yanxia Village, and together with Lion Mountain, Lizard Mountain, and Centipede Mountain to the east, defines the spatial scope of Yanxia Village.

The Qing Kangxi "Yongkang County Gazetteer" contains a concise and vivid description of Fangyan Mountain: Two hundred zhang high, six li in circumference, rising abruptly from the plain, sheer on all four sides, with only a passage to the south that ends at the mountainside; stones stacked as steps, ascending by degrees, called Hundred-Steep Steps.

Above the steps, a stone plank road built along the cliff, called Flying Bridge.Near the summit, two stones stand opposite each other, with a structure atop them, called Transparent Pass, commonly known as Peak Gate.

Below is Flying Rainbow Pavilion, newly built by monk Huitao.Beyond the pass, the land is more level and open, about several hundred mu, with a well called Inkstone Well and a pond of over one mu.

Fangyan Mountain For pilgrims going up Fangyan, after walking to the end of the old street, they turn west into a ravine at the foot of Fangyan Mountain, then ascend along the "Southern Passage.

" The so-called "Southern Passage" is actually a mountain path between Fangyan and Nanyan.After entering the mountain, walking not far along this path is the Xiamquan (Dismounting Horse Spring) Corridor, named after the spring.

Because it was once crowded with stalls selling Fangyan paper flowers, it is also called "Flower Corridor.

" Xiamquan, as its name implies, marks the end of the "Southern Passage"; from here, people can begin to experience the scenery described by Northern Song Yongkang jinshi Ying Cai: Stone steps towering, walking knee-to-knee; while walking one feels nothing, but looking is startling.

Even a skilled craftsman could hardly depict it; it is naturally formed by heaven's work.Luohan Cave Not far past Xiamquan is Luohan (Arhat) Cave, located on the mountainside of Nanyan.

Luohan Cave was also the dwelling place of Zhengde Chan Master, the founding patriarch of Dabei Temple.

It is said that Zhengde Chan Master, using funds obtained through alms-gathering, molded eighteen Arhat statues and placed them in the cave, hoping to借助 the Arhats' spiritual power to build Dabei Temple on the summit of Fangyan Mountain—hence the name Luohan Cave.

After Dabei Temple was built, Luohan Cave was no longer used by the temple.It is unknown when nuns began settling here, but this continued until the Cultural Revolution.

The last nun was Ying Shaowei, who not only treated people's illnesses but also took in wandering beggars, so she was widely respected.After her death, she was buried in a cave on the mountainside of Fangyan opposite Luohan Cave.

In the southwest corner of Luohan Cave is an ancient well called "Jiaolong Spring" (Flood Dragon Spring).Its water "is as sweet as malt syrup, thick in nature; once several copper coins were placed in a cup of the water, and it did not overflow.

" In the "Record of Jiaolong Spring," a beautiful legend about this well is also recorded.

It is said that once a nun surnamed Ying lived in Luohan Cave; in summer, she had to cross mountains every day to fetch water from Longqiu Spring below the Five Peaks for pilgrims to drink.

Her actions moved the Dragon King, so the Dragon King transformed into a carp and appeared to her in a dream, pointing out a location where a well could be dug.

After several years of effort, she finally succeeded in digging the well and finding spring water, hence named "Jiaolong Spring.

" Hundred-Steep Steps and Buyun Pavilion Past Luohan Cave is the "Hundred-Steep Steps" of "stones stacked as steps.

" Because this section of steps has a steep gradient, while ascending people cannot help but notice that the sides of every stone slab and the stone railings are carved with donors' names and native places, as if reading a book written in stone.

This process both makes people forget the toil and tedium of climbing and spontaneously raises a sense of reverence.At the end of the Hundred-Steep Steps is Buyun (Step into Clouds) Pavilion, spanning the road with upturned eaves.

The pavilion's location is just right, where people can rest briefly and lean on the railing to gaze into the distance.

As the couplet hanging above says, "Resting steps invite all immortals; following clouds comes to the half-mountain.

" According to the Qing Kangxi gazetteer, below Transparent Pass "there is Feihong Pavilion, newly built by monk Huitao," but this passage was deleted in the Qing Guangxu gazetteer; plus Zhu Jin's poem "Resting by Feihong Pavilion, summoning courage to enter Peak Pass," the "Peak Pass" here should be the "Peak Gate" mentioned in the gazetteer.

It can be inferred that Feihong Pavilion may be the predecessor of today's Buyun Pavilion.This also aligns with what Yunming Chan Master, former abbot of Guangci Temple, said—that Buyun Pavilion was built by the temple's monks.

Flying Bridge Above Buyun Pavilion is the over-hundred-meter-long plank road "built along the cliff with stones," also called "Flying Bridge.

" This plank road is set between sheer cliffs, winding gracefully; in early morning and after rain, it presents one of Yongkang's Ten Scenes, "Flying Bridge Locked in Mist.

" Today's Flying Bridge was rebuilt in reinforced concrete in the summer of 1962, nearly 2.5 meters wide—already less precipitous than in former times.

According to Yunming Chan Master, Flying Bridge was first built in the founding patriarch's era; its former condition can be glimpsed from the "Brief Record of Rebuilding Flying Bridge" inscribed beside it: "Originally paved with stone, only about a chi wide; after several renovations, increased to five chi.

" Local renovations of Flying Bridge can also find traces in other documents—for example, the "Epitaph of Cheng Gong Mengju" records that the fourth-generation Cheng ancestor Cheng Peng in the Ming Yongle Yiyou year (1405) once "built Flying Bridge and provided free gruel.

" Transparent Pass At the head of Flying Bridge, two giant stones face each other, with the narrowest point only just over a meter apart.

From the earliest surviving Ming Zhengde "Yongkang County Gazetteer" to the Qing Guangxu "Yongkang County Gazetteer," there is the same record: above the two giant stones once stood a pavilion inscribed "Transparent Pass.

" The Transparent Pass Pavilion no longer exists today, replaced by a double-eave octagonal pavilion inscribed "Heavenly Gate," built of stone and reinforced concrete.

According to Yunming Chan Master, the part below "Heavenly Gate" was first built by the founding patriarch; though repeatedly renovated, it has always existed.The current upper pavilion was built in the 37th year of the Republic (1948).

Heavenly King Hall As the gazetteer states, past Heavenly Gate the terrain becomes relatively level and open, and the first thing seen is the Heavenly King Hall.

The appearance of the Heavenly King Hall预示 that the Buddhist holy land is near at hand, but its location and layout are quite unique.The Heavenly King Hall is usually the first hall on the central axis of Chinese temple complexes.

Inside, the center usually enshrines Maitreya Bodhisattva, with Skanda Bodhisattva facing north behind him, and the Four Heavenly Kings on the east and west sides.

At Guangci Temple, the Heavenly King Hall is not located on the central axis with other main halls, but rather over a li away in front of the temple's main buildings.

This Heavenly King Hall straddles the road, with an open center for passage, like a city gate.Moreover, inside only the Four Heavenly Kings stand on both sides, without the conventional Maitreya and Skanda Bodhisattvas.

Some scholars believe that historically, Heavenly King Halls were initially most common in Pure Land temples, only appearing in most other sect temples after the Northern and Southern Song dynasties.

Guangci Temple is a Tiantai sect temple founded in the Tang Dynasty, which may explain why there is no Heavenly King Hall within the temple's main building complex.

This can also be corroborated by Guoqing Temple on Tiantai Mountain, which has close ties with Guangci Temple.

Guangci Temple's founding patriarch Zhengde Chan Master came from Guoqing Temple on Tiantai Mountain; the planar layout of Guoqing Temple in that era can no longer be researched.

Currently, the three buildings on the temple's central axis—Maitreya Hall, Yuhua Hall, and Mahavira Hall—were all rebuilt in the eleventh year of Yongzheng (1733).

But the statues inside were either transferred from elsewhere or later recast in the late Cultural Revolution period, to welcome Japanese Tiantai sect Buddhists visiting the ancestral temple.

Even if the transferred statues were based on original statues, Guoqing Temple's Four Heavenly Kings were not housed in the same hall as Maitreya and Skanda Bodhisattvas, but were separately enshrined in the Yuhua Hall.

Tianmentou Kongji (Heavenly Gate Head Empty Foundation) The relatively level area behind the Heavenly King Hall was formerly called "Tianmentou Kongji.

" Every year during incense season, snack stalls scatter here,方便 pilgrims to rest, fill their stomachs, and continue on their way.

Currently, besides foods like wontons, noodles, and dried tofu, the most distinctive snack at these roadside stalls is the rou mai bing (meat wheat cake), called "Yongkang fruit" by outsiders.

Rou mai bing uses diced pork with more fat than lean mixed with dried preserved vegetables as filling; the wrapping method is similar to northern pies, only thicker, finally baked in a shallow flat pan.

The trick in making it is to lift slightly while pinching the folds, allowing air to enter before sealing—so the baked cake is full of air inside, looks very plump from outside, and ensures the crust is both焦 and crisp.

Most importantly, rou mai bing can be made in advance, stacked by the pan without getting cold.When customers come, they can buy and eat as they go, without hindering pilgrims' journey.

Tianjie (Heavenly Street) Turning a corner from the snack street brings one to "Tianjie" (Heavenly Street), about half a li long, currently marked by a stone paifang standing at each end.

Tianjie was formerly called "Qianjie Road" (Front Street Road), with mostly two-story shops on both sides, and larger ones three stories high.

These include nearly ten small-scale inns and the "Rixin Book Society," mainly selling various books including "Fangyan Guide.

" Every year from the eighth lunar month to the third month, over a dozen temporary shops open, selling not only Jingguang (Beijing-Guangzhou) general goods, ironware, woodenware, children's toys, and wooden combs, but also local snacks such as beef, fried dough sticks, rou mai bing, wontons, tofu pudding, tofu balls, and tea.

Tianjie now only has one- to two-story shops on the south side, all rebuilt in 1988.

The shops mostly sell sacrificial supplies such as candles, incense paper, and Buddha statues, and also deal in Fangyan goods.

Shops selling similar goods were formerly mostly located in front of and behind temple halls; the 1925 edition of "Fangyan Guide" lists eleven general stores, one toy store, and over a dozen stalls selling incense, paper, candles, and ingots.

North of Tianjie is Changsheng Pond (Long Life Pond), legendarily formed from a fairy's tears; drinking the pond water can grant eternal youth, hence the name.

Beside the pond is a well called Yanjing (Inkstone Well), the drinking water source on which the temple monks relied for survival.

South of the middle section of Tianjie stands an ancient camphor tree said to be the same age as Guangci Temple; because many small camphor trees grow beside it, it is called Mother-and-Child Camphor.

Beside the Mother-and-Child Camphor once stood the Cheng Fangfeng (1498-1585) Ancestral Hall, built during the Ming Wanli period (1573-1620).

Now replaced by "Fangyan Immortal Cave," which uses modern means to create audio-visual effects like "Talking Living Bodhisattva," "Earth God Bursting Forth," and "Zhu Bajie's Great Disturbance in the Spider Silk Cave" to attract pilgrims.

From the giant pillar foundation stones scattered beside the Immortal Cave and the faintly visible square brick paving inside, the scale of the former Cheng Fangfeng Ancestral Hall can be inferred.

According to Yunming Chan Master, the hall originally contained a statue of Cheng Fangfeng; the hall gate was always closed, only opening during Qingming, when five to six hundred people would come to worship ancestors.

The tombs of Cheng Fangfeng and his wife Lady Sun were built behind the hall.

The existing tombstone was re-erected at Qingming in 1990, with the couplet "Eternal heroic spirit abides; myriad generations of noble spirit endure" carved beside it.

Thousand-Man Pit and Tingquan Tower At the northeast corner of Tianjie's end is the Thousand-Man Pit.

There, Fangyan Mountain is split into north and south sections, with the valley between them hundreds of meters deep and less than a meter wide.

About the Thousand-Man Pit, the gazetteer records this legend: "It is said that in ancient times, villagers took refuge from bandits on the mountain; when bandits came and grabbed vines to climb, they suddenly saw snakes; they struck with blades, the vines broke, the bandits fell, and many died, then they retreated in disarray—hence the name of this pit.

" The "bandits" here refer to Chen Shisi of Yongkang and his peasant rebel army in the second year of Northern Song Xuanhe (1120).

Chen Shisi's rebel army was a unit in the Yongkang-Fangyan area responding to Fang La's rebellion.

That year they captured the county seat, forcing County Magistrate Zhao Qi to retreat to Fangyan Mountain; what the gazetteer describes is the story that followed.

Although Chen Shisi's rebellion was suppressed the following year, people used it to commemorate the rebel soldiers who died then.

West of the Thousand-Man Pit once stood "Tingquan Tower" (Listening to Spring Tower); now the tower is gone and the spring dry, and one can only imagine the clear spring sound through gazetteer records: Westward from the pit, about a hundred paces, below is a stone valley; spring emerges from the valley, sounding like jade pendants.

Formerly there was a tower called Tingquan, built by local Hu Jiyuan.Scholars and officials composed poems into a collection called Tingquan Tower Collection.Not transmitted, only preface by Lü Shuangquan (Ying), seen in Shuangquan Collection.

Tingquan Tower: at Fangyan Thousand-Man Pit, built by Yuan dynasty local Hu Jiyuan; county officials and scholars mostly composed poems for it, collected into a volume, preface by Director Lü Shuangquan, with poems by Li Caoge.

Temple Beside Temple On the summit of Fangyan Mountain, currently stand side by side the little-known thousand-year-old famous temple Guangci Temple and the perpetually incense-prosperous Hugong Temple.

Although for a long period in history both Guangci Temple and Hugong Temple stood here simultaneously, their relationship was very different from today.

The current Huguang Temple site originally belonged to Guangci Temple, while Hugong Temple was once located to the left of Guangci Temple, near Changsheng Pond.

This can be proven by gazetteers over the years—for example, the Ming Jiajing "Yongkang County Gazetteer" states, "There is a shrine called Youshun Marquis Shrine.

To the right is a Buddhist dwelling called Guangci Temple"; while the Qing Daoguang "Yongkang County Gazetteer" writes, "By the pond there is a temple called Heling....

Beside the temple there is a temple called Guangci." The change in the relationship between this one temple and one shrine begins with understanding the history of Guangci Temple.

关键术语 Key Terms (91)
方岩山坐落在永康县城东五十里海拔高度约350米 方岩山位于岩下村西侧和东面狮子山蜥蜴山蜈蚣山等一起界定岩下村空间范围 清康熙永康县志对方岩山描写高二百丈周六里平地拔起四面如削惟南通一道至山腰而绝叠石为磴阶级而升曰百步峻 磴上沿岩架石为栈道曰飞桥将至顶有两石对竖屋之曰透关俗呼曰峰门下有飞虹亭寺僧慧韬新筑也入关地更平旷约数百亩有井曰砚井有池可亩余 对于上方岩的香客当老街走到底之后就会向西拐进方岩山脚下的一个山坳里再顺着南通一道上山 南通一道其实就是夹在方岩和南岩之间的一条登山小路 进山后沿着这条路走不远就是因下马泉而得名的下马泉长廊 下马泉长廊因为以前曾经挤满卖方岩纸花的摊位所以又被称为花廊 下马泉顾名思义标志着南通一道的终点从这里开始人们就可以体会到北宋永康进士应材所描写的那番景色石磴巍峨促膝行行时不觉看时惊纵教良匠描难就自是天工造化成 罗汉洞坐落在南岩岩腰过下马泉不远 罗汉洞也是当年大悲寺的肇基祖师正德禅师的栖息之所 相传正德禅师借化缘得来的资助塑了十八尊罗汉置于洞中并希望借助罗汉的法力在方岩山顶修建大悲寺罗汉洞便由此得名 大悲寺建好之后罗汉洞便不再被寺院使用 不知从什么时候开始便有尼姑在这里安家并一直持续到文革 最后一位尼姑叫应绍为她在这里不但给人看病而且还收留流浪的乞丐所以广受大家尊敬 应绍为去世之后就被埋葬在罗汉洞对面方岩山腰的山洞里 罗汉洞西南角有一口名为蛟龙泉的古井其水甘美如饴性沉厚尝置铜钱数枚于杯水中而水不外溢 蛟龙泉记里记载着关于这口井的一个美丽传说 据说曾经有一个姓应的尼姑居住在罗汉洞里夏天她每天都要翻山越岭到五峰下的龙湫泉取水供香客饮用 她的举动感动了龙王于是龙王就变成鲤鱼托梦于她指出可以打井的地点 经过了数年的努力她终于打井成功并找到了泉水因此取名为蛟龙泉 过了罗汉洞就是叠石为磴的百步峻 百步峻这段台阶坡度较大人们在上行的时候会不由自主地看到每一块条石的侧面和石栏杆上都刻着捐献者的姓名和籍贯就好像在读一本用石头写的书 这个过程既让人忘却了攀登的辛劳与枯燥又不禁油然升起一番敬意 百步峻的尽端就是横跨在路中飞檐翘角的步云亭 步云亭的位置恰到好处人们可以在这里稍事休息凭栏远眺 步云亭对联憩步邀群仙随云来半山 依清康熙县志描述透关下有飞虹亭寺僧慧韬新筑也但是这段话在清光绪县志中却被删掉了 朱谨诗句飞虹亭畔息贾勇入峰关这里的峰关应该就是县志里所提的峰门 可以推断飞虹亭可能就是现在的步云亭的前身 ...等共91个
二、广慈寺历史
中文 Chinese

创建初期 广慈寺原名大悲寺，是一座有上千年历史的天台宗名刹。它始建于唐宣宗大中四年（850年），也就是"会昌法难"结束后，恢复佛教的第四年。佛教自西汉传入中国以来，到隋唐时期已发展到鼎盛。在唐朝建立之初的二百年里，历任皇帝都大力提倡佛教并给予极高的礼遇。

到唐朝后期，随着佛教影响力的不断扩大和经济势力的日益膨胀，作为一种宗教信仰，它便逐渐威胁到了唐朝的中央统治。不仅如此，唐武宗（840—846年）本人信仰道教，因此在他即位后，便推行了一系列的"灭佛"政策，包括下令拆毁寺宇，勒令僧尼还俗等。广慈寺的肇基祖师正德禅师在那个时候也被迫离开了天台山国清寺。正德禅师，原为寺里的一个火头和尚，即负责烧饭的和尚。

离开国清寺之前，他曾在国清寺第十二世祖师物外禅师门下修习止观（即是禅定、智慧二学，是修学一切佛法的根本）。公元847年，崇信佛教的唐宣宗即位，改国号为大中元年，并立即下令恢复所有在会昌年间被废的佛寺。

由于天台山国清寺没有能被马上恢复，正德禅师可能因此便来到不远的方岩山来修头陀行，即佛教苦行之一，并居住在现方岩山腰罗汉洞内。他见方岩山"仍属混沌绝境，遂访山农指点，缘藤而上，匍匐跻攀，透洞始见山巅古树参天，槁木横卧，荆棘遍地，万般寂寥。探至凹处见上中下三潭水清澈见底，涧水淙淙不绝"。

于是，正德禅师在上、中潭畔先建茅棚以弘扬佛法，并于大中四年（850年）在原地创建了大悲寺。在这之后的一百多年间，方岩山清清静静，只听得到寺院里回荡的晨钟暮鼓和木鱼罄声。

胡则借读 在北宋端拱元年（988年）的春天，永康胡库人胡则来到寺院里借住，专心读书以准备赴考。作为回报，他在读书堂里教授寺院里的小和尚们读书写字，正因为如此，他广受寺院里和尚们的尊敬。

据广慈寺前任住持允明禅师讲，胡则走的时候，当时的住持资助了胡则上京赶考的路费，并叮嘱他，如果考不上，就一定要回来；如果考上了，也一定要回来报喜。胡则就这样带着老和尚的嘱托和小和尚们的希望走了，他在这里读书的故事也从此被老和尚传小和尚这样一代一代地流传了下来。

胡则在离别之前的阴历八月初一，"率为五言十二韵，书于屋壁下"，题名为《别方岩》，以抒发他临行前的依依不舍之情： 寓居峰顶寺，不觉度炎天。山叟频为约，林僧每出禅。虚怀思往事，宴坐息诸缘。照像龛镫暗，通宵磬韵传。宴心资寂寞，琢句极幽玄。拾菌寒云外，烹茶翠竹前。远阴临岳树，清响落岩泉。僻道无来客，深秋足乱蝉。松风生井浪，溪雨长苔钱。自省浮尘世，终难驻永年。遍游曾宛转，欲别重留连。

明日东西路，依依独黯然。胡则于北宋端拱二年（989年）进士及第，并随即开始了他的为官生涯。他辗转睦州、温州、信州、福州、杭州、池州等地，但是却再也没有回到方岩山大悲寺来看看。胡则于北宋宝元二年（1039年）病死他乡，葬于杭州钱塘县南山履泰乡龙井源。

当消息传到大悲寺，当年受胡则教诲的小和尚都已经成了老和尚，出于对老师的怀念，他们便给胡则立了一个牌位，供奉在读书堂里。关于胡则读书堂的位置，在明嘉靖《永康县志》里，还有这样一段描写，在广慈寺东边的千人坑"之侧有小径，缘崖而登，行二百余步，有石穴，曰读书堂。

俗传胡侯弦诵之地"。在清光绪《永康县志》里则把读书堂列入了地理志，古迹篇，"读书堂：去听泉楼百许步，岩腰有小石洞即胡侍郎读书处。好事者架飞甍为游人临眺之所"。

但是广慈寺前住持允明禅师却说，读书堂位于长生塘的西侧，又由于"宋高宗敕赐赫灵庙额诰"中提到"遂就旧读书处永康方岩寺池边立祠"，可以认为在山腰上的小石穴可能只是胡则的"弦诵之地"，而在长生塘西侧的读书堂则是他读书及教书的地方，也是后来被改称为佑顺侯祠和赫灵庙的建筑。

胡则从"人"到"神"的转变 在胡则去世后的八十多年里，他可能只有一个排位被寺里的和尚供奉在当年的读书堂，并享用一些来自里人的香火；读书堂也可能依然被用作小和尚的读书处。在这期间，大悲寺于北宋治平二年（1065年）失火，同年重建后易名为广慈寺。

据广慈寺前住持允明禅师讲，从那时起胡则的牌位和一尊小的塑像就被放在了如来佛的前面。

在近六十年后，方岩山广慈寺在北宋宣和二年（1120年）爆发的方腊起义中，曾作为永康知县的赵琪退守并最终击败起义军的地方。这件貌似偶然且毫无关系的事件却成为胡则从"人"变成"神"的关键第一步，其实这两件事之间暗含着深刻的历史原因。

自汉朝以来，中国历代的皇帝都独尊儒术，不但封其为官教，而且把它作为维护统治阶级的一种精神工具。但是儒家学说中教化的理念占到主要成分，它对生命的体会却十分肤浅，甚至不能回答一些有关生、死、祸、福等从皇帝到老百姓都关心的人生根本问题。所以历代皇帝在独尊儒术的同时，又利用佛教或道教来满足这些世俗的需求，同时进一步巩固其统治地位。换句话说，"原始宗教的天命观、鬼神观、巫术等，在皇权的暗中保护下，大量地保存下来，并在人民中找到适宜的生存空间。

封建皇帝对民间信仰中的神灵也从不一概排斥，甚至对许多地方涌现出的神予以册封。"与胡则生活在同一时代，家喻户晓的"妈祖"（960—987年）就是一个有力的证明。"妈祖"真名为林默，由于她生前和死后的众多传说，宋、元、明、清的历代帝王都对她有多次的册封，封号也从"夫人"、"天妃"、"天后"到"天上圣母"，并列入国家祀典。历代帝王们不但通过对神祇的册封来确立神的地位，从而巩固自己的统治，更重要的是借此来证明一种远远比"君权神授"更高的权力地位，即"神权君授"。

宋代政权建立之后的前几代皇帝都推崇佛教，但是宋徽宗（在位1100—1125年），却尊信道教，他曾一度命令佛教和道教合流，改寺院为道观，并使佛号、僧尼的名称都道教化。在历史上，每当新任皇帝弃佛从道的时期，起源于异国的佛教为了生存，都不得不对土生土长在中国大地上的道教进行妥协，把一些属于道教的，或者是民间的神祇请入佛堂共享香火。在这一点上，广慈寺也不例外，这也是它能历经千年依然香火旺盛的原因之一。

在宋徽宗当政时期发生的一件事，恰好让广慈寺顺理成章地把一位民间神祇纳入了自己的领地。宣和二年（1120年）十月初九，歙州农民方腊由于不满北宋腐朽统治，借摩尼教的旗号在睦州青溪爆发起义。摩尼教又称明教，在唐会昌年间被禁，随后转入民间秘密流行，历经五代至宋朝仍在闽浙一带有广泛的信徒，因此这次起义迅速得到了广大农民的热烈拥护和响应。

北宋王朝的统治者在镇压这次带有宗教色彩的农民起义的时候，也充分利用了地方神祇的力量，宣传神人相助的神话。由于这次起义在短短一年半的时间就被全部镇压，所以在这其中的所有战斗都能最终以正派神祇的胜利作为结束，从而一箭双雕地打击"邪教"和"寇匪"。在此期间，北宋王朝大力褒奖地方神祇，因此"在方腊起义中显灵庇护官军而报至朝廷请求嘉奖的祠庙不在少数"，如"上虞遗德庙、嵊县显应庙等"。

有些即使是未受"寇匪"侵扰的地方，地方统治者也会宣称是因为有神人暗中相助的结果。在方腊起义中，永康方岩陈十四也揭竿而起。他们攻破永康县城，迫使知县赵琪退据方岩山。

虽然赵琪最终是仓皇逃离了方岩山，但是因为陈十四的起义军不久后就被镇压了，可以推测知县赵琪也顺势迎合皇帝的意愿，杜撰了在方岩山有神人助王师灭寇的神话。而曾经"逮事三朝，十握州符，六持使节"，并且已经被当地人所熟悉和爱戴的永康人胡则，也顺理成章地成了神人助王师神话的主角。

况且当时胡则在广慈寺读书堂里已立有牌位，把他神化成方岩山神便再合适不过了。这些神话故事有许多版本，它们的真实性和逻辑性并不是本文所讨论的重点，所以不妨就引用"宋高宗敕赐赫灵庙额诰"中所记述的官方版本，"民以重罹兵革为患，神独感梦于主将，显灵而阴助之。

又贼魁梦一巨人，衣白衣跨白马饮池，黎明视水涸，其徒惊骇，遂至大败"。在宋徽宗宣和四年（1122年），也就是方腊起义被全面镇压后的这年，胡则被封为"佑顺侯"，这也是他去世后得到的第一次封赏。或许因为在同时有众多的地方神祇都因为类似的原因而得到了封赏，以至于"王廉访当时奏请失于勘斟，遂致所封敕中不言姓名"，而"止用方岩神奏"。

或许是这次册封的确不重要，以至于没能引起胡氏后人的重视，因为这次册封的诰书并没有收录进《库川胡氏宗谱》。广慈寺在当时并没有推崇道教的大背景下，倒是可能利用了这次册封，把胡则称为"方岩神佑顺侯"而纳入佛家圣地，并"香火供养甚谨"。而同时，方岩附近的一些地区也开始供奉胡则。

作为一方神祇，胡则是"若水若旱若疫若疠，有求无不应，有祷无不答"。随着他在当地的名气越来越大，胡氏后人可能不满足于这次未言姓名的封赏，于是"阖邑之士状于有司"。在绍兴二十一年（1151年），作为建安县丞，也是胡则重孙的胡廷直向当时的宋高宗"详述始末，力求正名"。

在核实了衢州委司户参军赵善和绍兴府诸暨县主簿赵彦伦之后，礼部依建炎三年（1129年）颁布的"挥神祠遇有灵应即先赐额"的圣旨，向皇帝建议赐"赫灵庙"为胡则祠的匾额。绍兴二十二年（1152年）二月，宋高宗降旨"敕赐赫灵庙"。胡则的这次被册封，只是他日后一系列册封的开始。

在宋代，册封制度已经逐步完善，规定对于有灵应的神祠要"无爵号者赐以额，已赐额者加封爵。初封侯，再封公，次封王"。而对于同一级别内封号的字数也有限制，开始为两个字，可逐步增加最多到八个字。关于册封制度的这些具体要求，从胡则的历次册封就可以看出来。

在宋绍熙三年（1192年），胡则被宋光宗赐为"佑顺嘉应侯"；随后在嘉泰三年（1203年）和嘉定三年（1210年），被宋宁宗分两次册封至"佑顺嘉应福泽显灵侯"。从这些封号用词的选择中，可以看出在表彰胡则时间中态度的变化。

"佑顺"是从皇帝角度上一种客观的认可；"嘉应"代表了一种赞许和肯定；"福泽"则是站在人民的立场上一种不遗余力的褒扬；最终到"显灵"，即一种超客观的神化。与此同时，胡则被"庙祀于衢、婺之间，无虑数十"。到淳祐六年（1246年），宋理宗封胡则"显应公"，随后"加正惠，宝祐初再加忠祐"。

在胡则被一次一次加封的同时，方岩山上供奉胡则的读书堂却"岁久事殊，址存堂废"。宋景定三年（1262年），里人王氏"不忍其坏，举而新之夫"。在这篇吴邃写的《方岩读书堂记》中还提到了作者本人在读书堂重修之后，"谨即斯堂拜公遗像"。

由此可见，位于读书堂内的胡则塑像，最迟在重修后就已经存在了。据广慈寺前住持允明禅师讲，胡公庙以前就位于读书堂的位置。

如此看来，有了胡则塑像的读书堂，日后就逐渐演化成了明嘉靖《永康县志》中提到的"佑顺侯祠"和清道光《永康县志》里所指的"赫灵祠"。

近一百年后，写于元至正九年（1349年）的《胡侍郎庙碑阴记》中再次提到地方的胡则庙"布满于郡境，不啻数十百区"，胡则更是被演化成了一位"能出云为风雨"的万能神。明清两代的继续发展 在经历了崇奉喇嘛教的元代，明代是佛教各个宗派逐步恢复和发展的时期。在这个时期里，广慈寺的香火也逐步趋于鼎盛。

值得一提的是在明万历年间（1589—1619年）任住持的聿孤禅师，以及在他任职期间发生的两件事。据说聿孤禅师买了八个小孩当徒弟，改革创寺之始就实行的"子孙派"，将其分为乾、天、承、地、人、才、宝、智八房，轮流管理寺院香火和常务工作。"子孙派"规定只接收不超过五岁的幼儿皈依，小沙弥自幼与师父同起居、同饮食，形同父子，并死后合葬。

聿孤禅师的改革既保持了"子孙派"极具亲和力的特点，又使寺院管理可以互相监督，并在此基础上发展了众多"下属寺院"。这也是广慈寺能历经多种磨难而成为千年古刹矗立在此的主要原因。另一件事对广慈寺则是一个"重创"。据允明禅师讲，在明隆庆五年（1571年），附近的文楼村出了一个榜眼叫程正谊，后任四川左布政。

程正谊的父亲叫程方峰（1498—1585年），他看上了方岩山这块宝地，便让儿子向当时的住持聿孤禅师借地做坟。聿孤禅师开始并不愿意，但念在自己是程方峰的孙子辈，便写了"卖地十丈"的地契。

若干年后，聿孤禅师圆寂了，程方峰也过世了，程氏子孙把"卖地十丈"改成了"卖地千丈"，并在前街路边上的母子樟旁开始建造程方峰祠堂，一场官司便由此而生。寺里的和尚以整个方岩山也没有千丈为据来证明"卖地千丈"不实。

审判结果是"卖地是真，千丈是假"，并判以千人坑和长生塘为界，南边归程氏，北侧归广慈寺。而这个审判结果在清光绪《永康县志》中也有明确记载，"程方峰先生梓墓：方岩上自千人坑流水为界，至峰门悬崖峭壁俱属墓地。

万历（1573—1620年）年间奉勘合给。"这件事不知过了多久以后，文楼村的程氏看出了方岩山上的商机，开始在天街上做起了香烛、供品等生意。

一半的方岩山归了别人，广慈寺的香火和收入多少都会受到影响。清顺治十一年（1654年），赫灵庙就在胡氏后人的帮助下历经了一次修葺。

可是几十年后，清康熙《永康县志》中就已经记载赫灵祠因年久失修，几近坍塌，寺里的僧人便把胡则的塑像搬到了广慈寺内。

同时搬过去的应该还有胡则的牌位和那块敕赐的"赫灵庙"匾，它们一起被供奉在当时广慈寺的前殿内，从而真正意义上做到了寺庙合一。

在这以后，从雍正七年（1729年）到道光二十七年（1847年）的一百多年里，奉胡则为伯祖的库川胡氏后人，先后多次出资帮助广慈寺修理或重建前厅和大殿，仅有记载的就有六次。

其中嘉庆二十四年（1819年）对前厅的重建和扩建，基本上奠定了广慈寺中轴线最终的平面格局。据允明禅师回忆，广慈寺中轴线上有四进殿堂，各殿堂之间有廊相连。

第一进为广慈寺前厅，挂"广慈寺"匾；第二进为前殿，挂"赫灵庙"匾，中间供吃荤的"行公"胡则，背面为韦驮，两旁供胡则十弟兄；第三进为大雄宝殿，中间供如来佛，前面是乾隆三年（1738年）重塑的胡则像，旁边是文殊和普贤菩萨，殿堂两侧为十八罗汉；第四进是屏风阁，主供玉皇大帝；在大雄宝殿的东侧还建有观音阁。

八房僧人的僧房则位于这些殿堂的外围，在平面上形成一个"八卦"图案。在宣统二年（1910年），出于对"人神共庙"批判的回应，寺里的僧人把玉皇大帝请到了位于西侧的地藏殿，把如来佛前的胡则像单独供奉于屏风阁，为吃素的"坐公"胡则。

胡公虽然从大雄宝殿里被请了出来，但是据允明禅师说，即使现在胡公像后面依旧有如来、文殊和普贤菩萨的小塑像。走向衰落 不幸的是，道光二十九年（1849年）九月初四的一场大火烧毁了整个祠庙及僧房。虽然在一年多以后，广慈寺得以重建，但是这已经成为它走向衰落的第一步。

据允明禅师讲，清朝末年，当地老百姓的生活十分穷苦。再加上鸦片的泛滥，所以时常有人上山拿走庙里的东西，甚至是拆掉殿宇靠卖木头的钱为生。僧人也因此越来越少，八房僧人也只剩下了四房。《方岩指南》中也提到"清光绪年间（1875—1908年），寺僧相率而去"。

在民国20和21两年，广慈寺还被后钱村来的一个道士霸占，寺内的僧人还被迫流落到附近的下属寺院。在接下来几十年里一系列的人为灾害中，文化大革命无疑带给了广慈寺前所未有的重创。广慈寺几乎所有的建筑均在1966年8月下旬被拆除，仅有屏风阁和一幢僧房幸免于难。

屏风阁原归方岩公社拆除，但是由于近千年对胡则的尊重，老街上的人拒绝这样做。于是，屏风阁被留了下来，并先后被用作大队开会的地方，林场仓库、邮电学校和602工厂。不仅如此，广慈寺内的字画和书籍从山上往下挑了十担，整整烧了一天。

虽然《广慈寺寺志》在允明禅师的保护下逃过了这场浩劫，但却不明不白地遗失在永康市宗教科的各种卷宗之间。"文革"后的复苏 文化大革命结束两年后，在1978年八月初一的凌晨，由岩下村村民黄埔林的母亲发起，她和另外三个妇女把一尊新塑的小胡公像抬上了山。

"文革"后的第一任住持允明禅师于1979年十月初三上山，并凭记忆重塑了胡则像和屏风阁里其他的塑像，包括文曲星、财神、龙王和关公等。由于屏风阁还在，所以就有香客慕名来拜胡则。

允明禅师用募捐来的香火钱，在1980年到1984年的五年间，买了四座祠堂，用以恢复广慈寺的建筑。第一个是在1980年用3000多元买的古竹畈村的一个厅，用来重建屏风阁前的大雄宝殿。

紧接着用比较便宜的价格买下了因建杨汘水库而被迫拆除的汘下周村的周氏祠堂。随后用4000多元买了龙油县山買村的牛王殿，用来恢复天王殿、步云亭、后山的亭子和做一些修修补补的工作。最后买了永康市里的徐氏祠堂，在观音阁原址上重建了现在的广慈寺观音殿。

· · · · · ·
英文 English
